#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain how a Jenkins CI/CD pipeline works?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A Jenkins CI/CD pipeline automates the software delivery lifecycle from code commit to deployment using a declarative `Jenkinsfile`.
1. **Trigger:** A developer pushes code or creates a PR in Git, triggering the pipeline via webhook.
2. **Checkout:** Jenkins pulls the latest code from the branch.
3. **Build:** Compiles the source code and packages artifacts (e.g., `mvn clean package`).
4. **Test & Code Quality:** Runs unit tests and executes SonarQube code quality scans.
5. **Docker Build & Push:** Packages the application into a Docker container, runs vulnerability scans (Trivy), and pushes the image to AWS ECR or Docker Hub.
6. **Deploy:** Deploys the application to Dev, QA, or Production environments using Helm or `kubectl`.
</details>

#### 【 DOCKER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between the ADD and COPY instructions in a Dockerfile?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Both instructions copy files from the host build context into the Docker image, but they have distinct differences:
- **`COPY`:** Simple and straightforward. It copies local files or folders from the build context into the image destination. It is the best practice for almost all file-copying needs.
- **`ADD`:** Has two additional features: it can fetch files from remote URLs, and it automatically unpacks recognized local tar archives (`.tar.gz`, `.tar.bz2`) into the target directory.
Best practice is to use `COPY` unless you specifically need tar extraction.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ What is the Docker command used to create an image?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The command is `docker build`.
```bash
docker build -t myapp:1.0 .
```
`-t` tags the image with a name and version, and `.` specifies the current directory as the build context containing the `Dockerfile`.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are Ansible playbooks, and why are they used? Can you explain with an example?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Ansible playbooks are human-readable YAML files that define automation tasks in a declarative format. They are used for configuration management, package installation, and software deployments across remote servers over SSH without requiring client agents.

Example playbook to install and start Apache HTTP server:
```yaml
---
- name: Setup Web Server
  hosts: webservers
  become: yes
  tasks:
    - name: Install Apache
      yum:
        name: httpd
        state: present

    - name: Start and enable Apache service
      service:
        name: httpd
        state: started
        enabled: yes
```
Playbooks are idempotent, ensuring identical, predictable state regardless of how many times they are executed.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ What is the command used to run an Ansible playbook?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The command is `ansible-playbook`.
```bash
ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini playbook.yml
```
Common flags include `-C` (dry run / check mode), `--syntax-check` (validates YAML syntax), and `-e` (passes extra variables).
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is AWS CloudWatch?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AWS CloudWatch is a fully managed monitoring and observability service for AWS resources and cloud applications. It provides:
- **Metrics:** Automatically collects performance metrics (CPU utilization, Disk I/O, Network in/out) for services like EC2, RDS, and ALB.
- **Logs:** Centralizes and searches application and system logs using CloudWatch Logs and CloudWatch Log Insights.
- **Alarms:** Monitors metrics against thresholds and triggers notifications via Amazon SNS or actions like EC2 Auto Scaling and instance reboot.
</details>

#### 【 MONITORING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you configure Prometheus and Grafana for monitoring?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Expose Metrics:** Install `node-exporter` on target Linux servers or enable `/metrics` endpoints on applications.
2. **Configure Prometheus:** Add scrape targets in `prometheus.yml` under `scrape_configs` with target IPs, ports, and scrape intervals. (In Kubernetes, we deploy the Prometheus Operator and use `ServiceMonitor` resources).
3. **Connect Grafana:** In the Grafana web console, navigate to **Configuration > Data Sources**, select **Prometheus**, and enter the Prometheus server URL (e.g., `http://prometheus:9090`).
4. **Visualize:** Import standard community dashboard templates or create custom panels querying Prometheus metrics using PromQL.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ How do you create a dashboard in Grafana?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. Log into Grafana and go to **Dashboards > New Dashboard > Add Visualization**.
2. Select your connected data source (e.g., Prometheus).
3. Write a PromQL metric query, for example:
   ```promql
   100 - (avg by (instance) (rate(node_cpu_seconds_total{mode="idle"}[5m])) * 100)
   ```
4. Choose a visualization type (Time Series, Gauge, Stat panel, or Bar chart).
5. Configure panel properties (Title, Legends, Threshold colors, and Units like `%` or `bytes`).
6. Click **Apply**, arrange the panel layout, and click **Save Dashboard**.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ How do you set up alerts in Grafana or Prometheus?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **In Prometheus:** Define alert rules in a YAML file using PromQL expressions, evaluation duration (`for: 5m`), and annotations. When triggered, Prometheus sends alerts to **Alertmanager**, which deduplicates and routes notifications to Slack, PagerDuty, or Email.
- **In Grafana:** Open any dashboard panel, navigate to the **Alert** tab, define the threshold rule (e.g., `CPU > 85% for 5m`), select a **Contact Point** (Slack webhook or Email), and save the alert rule.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you filter logs in Kibana?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Kibana's **Discover** tab:
1. Select the appropriate **Data View / Index Pattern** (e.g., `app-logs-*`).
2. Set the desired time range in the top-right corner.
3. Use the **KQL (Kibana Query Language)** search bar to filter logs, for example:
   ```
   level: "ERROR" and service.name: "order-service" and http.response.status_code >= 500
   ```
4. Click **+ Add filter** from the left-hand field panel to filter by specific fields like `host.name` or `environment`.
</details>

</details>
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Did you write Jenkins pipeline manifest files as part of your role?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, writing and maintaining Declarative `Jenkinsfile` scripts has been a regular part of my role. I write pipelines that automate code checkout, unit testing, SonarQube quality analysis, Docker image building and vulnerability scanning, publishing images to AWS ECR, and automated deployments to Kubernetes environments using Helm or `kubectl`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the Jenkins home directory, and what does it contain?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The Jenkins home directory (typically `/var/lib/jenkins`) is the central storage location where Jenkins stores all its configuration, build history, and plugin data. It contains:
- `config.xml`: Main Jenkins system configuration.
- `jobs/`: Directories for each job containing build records, logs, and job configs.
- `plugins/`: Installed plugin binaries (`.jpi`/`.hpi` files).
- `workspace/`: Working directories where source code is checked out and built.
- `secrets/`: Master encryption keys and stored credentials.
- `nodes/`: Configurations for connected build agents.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a Jenkins plugin fails to load after a restart, how would you troubleshoot this issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Check System Logs:** Review `/var/log/jenkins/jenkins.log` or navigate to `Manage Jenkins > System Log` to inspect the stack trace and reason for failure.
2. **Check Plugin Dependencies:** Most failures stem from missing dependency plugins or version incompatibility with the current Jenkins core version.
3. **Manual CLI Removal/Rollback:** If Jenkins fails to boot, navigate to `/var/lib/jenkins/plugins/`, remove or rename the offending plugin file (`.jpi`), and restart Jenkins.
4. **Upgrade Jenkins Core or Plugin:** Upgrade Jenkins to the minimum required version or download a compatible earlier version of the plugin from the Jenkins plugin archive.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Have you ever encountered a 'RejectedExecutionException' error in Jenkins?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, `RejectedExecutionException` occurs when Jenkins' internal thread pool executor is saturated or when Jenkins is shutting down / restarting while new builds or tasks are being queued. It can also happen when the master server runs out of heap memory. To resolve it, I verify JVM memory utilization, restart Jenkins cleanly, increase JVM heap options (`-Xmx`), and offload heavy build workloads to distributed dynamic agent nodes so the master controller isn't overloaded.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you install plugins in Jenkins?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
There are three standard methods:
1. **Via Jenkins UI (Standard):** Navigate to `Manage Jenkins > Plugins > Available plugins`, search for the plugin (e.g., Docker, Git, SonarQube), check the box, and click **Install without restart**.
2. **Manual Upload:** Download the `.hpi`/`.jpi` file from the Jenkins plugin index and upload it via `Manage Jenkins > Plugins > Advanced settings > Deploy Plugin`.
3. **Automated / Dockerized:** In containerized Jenkins, define plugins in a `plugins.txt` file and run `jenkins-plugin-cli --plugin-file plugins.txt` during the Docker image build.
</details>

#### 【 DOCKER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Why are containers considered better than virtual machines?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Containers offer significant architectural and operational advantages over VMs:
- **Lightweight Architecture:** VMs run a full guest OS on top of a hypervisor, consuming gigabytes of disk and RAM. Containers share the host OS kernel and isolate at the process level.
- **Fast Startup:** Containers start in seconds or milliseconds, whereas VMs take several minutes to boot an entire OS.
- **Resource Efficiency:** Multiple containers run on the same host with minimal CPU and memory overhead, yielding much higher server density.
- **Portability:** Containers package application code with all runtime dependencies, guaranteeing identical execution across development, testing, and production.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Beyond the theoretical explanation, can you give a practical, real-world reason why containers are better than virtual machines?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our day-to-day work, containerization completely eliminated the classic 'it works on my machine' issue. Previously, developers would build code on macOS or Windows with one Java or library patch version, and it would fail when deployed to QA VMs due to slight environmental discrepancies. With Docker, the exact same container image tested locally runs identically in QA and Production. Furthermore, in our CI/CD pipelines, spinning up ephemeral build agents in containers takes 5 seconds compared to 3-5 minutes for an EC2 VM, cutting pipeline execution time dramatically.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Do you have experience working with Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have over 3 years of hands-on experience with Kubernetes, managing production microservices on AWS EKS. My experience includes writing and maintaining manifests for Deployments, Services, Ingress, ConfigMaps, Secrets, and HPAs; packaging applications with Helm; and handling day-to-day cluster operations, troubleshooting, and upgrades.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ In Amazon EKS, does AWS manage both the master (control plane) node and the worker nodes, or only one of them?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AWS manages only the **master (control plane)** nodes. AWS runs the control plane components (API server, etcd, scheduler, controller-manager) across multiple Availability Zones in an AWS-owned VPC, handling high availability, patching, and backups. The **worker nodes** (where user application pods run) are provisioned inside the customer's VPC, managed either via EKS Managed Node Groups, self-managed EC2 instances, or AWS Fargate.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ How are worker nodes managed and used in your EKS setup?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our environment, we use **EKS Managed Node Groups** spread across multiple private subnets in our VPC. We manage them using Terraform, specifying EC2 instance types (such as `t3.medium` or `m5.large`), scaling limits (min, max, desired capacity), and Amazon Linux 2 / Bottlerocket AMIs. We run the Kubernetes Cluster Autoscaler to automatically adjust the node count based on pending pod demands, while AWS handles automated node provisioning and graceful rolling upgrades.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● In an EKS/Kubernetes cluster, what happens to pod scheduling if the Kubernetes scheduler component goes down?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
If `kube-scheduler` goes down:
- **Existing running pods** continue running without interruption because node `kubelets` continue managing running containers.
- **New pods** created by deployments or manual applies will remain stuck in the **Pending** state because no scheduler is available to assign a node to `pod.spec.nodeName`.
- In AWS EKS, because the control plane is managed and monitored by AWS across multiple AZs, AWS automatically detects the failure and restarts or replaces the scheduler instance within seconds.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you troubleshoot and resolve an OOMKilled (Out of Memory) issue in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Identify the Issue:** Run `kubectl get pods` (shows `OOMKilled` or frequent crash restarts) and inspect `kubectl describe pod <pod-name>` (shows `Last State: Terminated, Reason: OOMKilled, Exit Code: 137`).
2. **Analyze Metrics:** Use Grafana or `kubectl top pod` to examine memory consumption curves before the crash.
3. **Determine Cause:** Check whether the issue is a sudden legitimate spike in application traffic or an application memory leak.
4. **Resolution:** If it's legitimate traffic, increase `resources.limits.memory` in the deployment manifest (and adjust JVM heap flags `-Xmx` accordingly for Java apps). If it's a memory leak, collaborate with developers to capture and analyze heap dumps.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is a PriorityClass in Kubernetes, and where is it applied?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A `PriorityClass` is a cluster-scoped object that maps a priority name to an integer value (higher values mean higher priority).
- **Where applied:** It is specified inside the pod manifest under `pod.spec.priorityClassName: <priority-class-name>`.
- **Purpose:** When cluster resources are constrained, the scheduler schedules high-priority pods first. If no node has enough capacity, the scheduler can preempt (evict) lower-priority pods to make room for critical workloads.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is a resource quota in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A `ResourceQuota` is a Kubernetes policy applied at the namespace level to limit the total amount of compute resources (CPU, memory) and object counts (number of Pods, Services, PVCs, ConfigMaps) that can be consumed by all workloads in that namespace. It prevents any single team or environment (e.g., dev or test) from exhausting shared cluster resources.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between Horizontal Pod Autoscaling (HPA) and Vertical Pod Autoscaling (VPA) in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Horizontal Pod Autoscaling (HPA):** Scales **out** or **in** by adding or removing pod replicas based on CPU, memory, or custom metrics (e.g., scaling from 2 to 10 pods when CPU exceeds 75%).
- **Vertical Pod Autoscaling (VPA):** Scales **up** or **down** by adjusting the CPU and memory resource requests and limits of existing pods. Because changing resource limits usually requires restarting the pod, HPA is preferred and widely used for production stateless microservices.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are the different types/modules of Terraform (e.g., root module vs child module)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Root Module:** The working directory containing the main `.tf` files where you run `terraform init`, `plan`, and `apply`. It serves as the primary entry point for Terraform execution.
- **Child Module:** A reusable module called from another module (such as the root module) using a `module` block:
  ```hcl
  module "vpc" {
    source   = "./modules/vpc"
    vpc_cidr = "10.0.0.0/16"
  }
  ```
- **External / Published Modules:** Child modules sourced from external locations like the Terraform Registry or remote Git repositories.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you handle resource drift in Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Resource drift occurs when cloud resources are modified manually outside of Terraform.
1. Run `terraform plan`: Terraform refreshes state against the actual cloud environment and highlights differences between code and live resources.
2. **If change was intended:** Update your Terraform code to reflect the manual change so the plan shows zero pending changes.
3. **If change was unintended/unauthorized:** Run `terraform apply` to overwrite the drift and restore the infrastructure to the desired state defined in your code.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between variables and outputs in Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Variables (`variable`):** Input parameters that pass values into Terraform configurations and modules, making code reusable across multiple environments (e.g., `environment = "prod"`, `instance_type = "t3.medium"`).
- **Outputs (`output`):** Return values exposed after Terraform finishes provisioning. They display important information on the CLI (e.g., VPC ID, RDS endpoint) and pass values from child modules back to the root module.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ What do you typically define/mention inside the output.tf file in Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Inside `outputs.tf`, we define resource attributes that engineers or external automation need after deployment. For example:
```hcl
output "vpc_id" {
  description = "VPC ID of the network"
  value       = aws_vpc.main.id
}

output "rds_endpoint" {
  description = "Database connection endpoint"
  value       = aws_db_instance.db.endpoint
}
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ What is the purpose and advantage of using a variables.tf file in Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The purpose of `variables.tf` is to declare all configurable input parameters, data types, descriptions, and optional default values in one central place.
**Advantages:**
- **Reusability:** The same infrastructure code can deploy Dev, QA, and Prod environments simply by supplying different variable values.
- **Clean Code:** Prevents hardcoding values (such as AMI IDs or CIDR blocks) inside `main.tf`.
- **Validation:** Enables type enforcement (string, number, list, map) and custom validation rules to catch configuration mistakes early.
</details>

#### 【 NETWORKING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are the different OSI model layers?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The OSI model has 7 layers (from top to bottom):
7. **Application Layer:** Network processes and user applications (HTTP, HTTPS, SSH, DNS).
6. **Presentation Layer:** Data formatting, encryption, and compression (SSL/TLS, JSON).
5. **Session Layer:** Establishes and manages connections between applications.
4. **Transport Layer:** End-to-end data delivery and port addressing (TCP, UDP).
3. **Network Layer:** Logical IP addressing and packet routing across networks (IPv4, IPv6, routers).
2. **Data Link Layer:** Physical MAC addressing and frame switching (Ethernet, switches).
1. **Physical Layer:** Physical transmission of raw bits over hardware (cables, fiber, radio).
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is an AWS Fargate profile, and what is its use case in the context of EKS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
An AWS Fargate profile is an EKS configuration that defines which Kubernetes pods should run on AWS Fargate serverless compute instead of traditional EC2 worker nodes. In the profile, you specify Kubernetes namespaces and optional label selectors.
**Use Case:** Ideal for running isolated, batch, or variable workloads (such as scheduled cron jobs or CI/CD build agents) where you do not want to manage, patch, or pay for idle EC2 worker node capacity.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is AWS WAF (Web Application Firewall)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AWS WAF is a web application firewall that protects web applications and APIs from common web exploits and malicious bots. It attaches to Application Load Balancers (ALB), Amazon CloudFront distributions, or API Gateways. Using managed or custom Web ACL rules, WAF inspects incoming HTTP/HTTPS traffic to block SQL injection (SQLi), Cross-Site Scripting (XSS), rate-limit DDoS requests, and filter traffic by IP or geographic region.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are AWS Step Functions?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AWS Step Functions is a serverless visual orchestration service used to coordinate multiple AWS services (such as AWS Lambda functions, ECS tasks, and SNS/SQS) into automated, multi-step workflows called state machines. It handles execution state, branching logic (if/then/else), automated retries, and error handling without requiring complex custom code.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the AWS CLI command used to create an S3 bucket?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Using the high-level `aws s3` command:
```bash
aws s3 mb s3://my-unique-bucket-name --region us-east-1
```
Or using the low-level `aws s3api` command:
```bash
aws s3api create-bucket --bucket my-unique-bucket-name --region us-east-1
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● For a gaming application, which type of AWS load balancer would be more suitable?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A **Network Load Balancer (NLB)** is the most suitable load balancer for gaming applications.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Why is a Network Load Balancer (NLB) more suitable for a gaming application compared to other load balancer types?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Layer 4 Operation:** NLB operates at the transport layer, handling both TCP and UDP protocols (multiplayer real-time games rely heavily on UDP for speed).
2. **Ultra-Low Latency:** Delivers sub-millisecond latency compared to ALB (Layer 7), which terminates and parses HTTP headers.
3. **Extreme Throughput:** Capable of handling millions of requests per second and sudden traffic spikes without requiring pre-warming.
4. **Static IP & Client IP Preservation:** Provides static IP addresses per AZ and preserves client source IPs directly to backend game servers.
</details>

#### 【 SECURITY 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What tool is typically used for handling/managing secrets in Jenkins (similar to how HashiCorp Vault is used with Terraform)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Jenkins, secrets are natively managed using the built-in **Jenkins Credentials Provider** (Credentials Plugin). Credentials (API tokens, passwords, SSH keys) are stored encrypted on disk and injected into pipelines securely using the `credentials('credential-id')` wrapper. For enterprise centralized secrets management, Jenkins integrates directly with **HashiCorp Vault** (via the Jenkins HashiCorp Vault Plugin) or **AWS Secrets Manager** to fetch short-lived dynamic credentials at runtime.
</details>

</details>
</details>

#### 【 LINUX 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● In Linux, how do you search for a specific word inside a file?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Using the `grep` command:
```bash
grep "error" app.log
```
Common useful options:
- `-i`: Case-insensitive search (`grep -i "error" app.log`).
- `-n`: Shows line numbers (`grep -n "error" app.log`).
- `-r` / `-rn`: Searches recursively across all files in a directory (`grep -rn "error" /var/log/`).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a Linux file system becomes full, what steps would you follow to identify the root cause?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Identify Full Partition:** Run `df -h` to see which mount point is at 100%.
2. **Check Inodes:** Run `df -i` to verify the partition isn't out of inodes due to millions of small files.
3. **Locate Large Files:** Change directory to the affected mount point and identify largest folders and files:
   ```bash
   du -sh /* 2>/dev/null | sort -rh | head -10
   ```
   Or search for files over 500MB: `find /var/log -type f -size +500M -exec ls -lh {} \;`.
4. **Check Deleted Files Open by Processes:** If `df` shows 100% but `du` doesn't find large files, run `lsof | grep deleted` and restart the process holding the unlinked file.
5. **Mitigate:** Truncate large logs (`> app.log`) and set up logrotate.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you view all running processes on a Linux system?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We use `ps` or `top`:
- `ps aux` or `ps -ef`: Lists all currently running processes with PID, user, CPU%, memory%, and start command.
- `top` or `htop`: Provides an interactive, real-time dynamic monitor of running processes.
- To search for a specific process:
  ```bash
  ps aux | grep nginx
  # or
  pgrep -l nginx
  ```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a user encounters a 'permission denied' error on Linux, what would you check to troubleshoot it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Check Ownership and Permissions:** Run `ls -ld <file_or_directory>` to verify read, write, and execute permissions (`rwx`) and owner/group.
2. **Check User Identity:** Run `whoami` and `id` to see the user's UID and group memberships.
3. **Check Parent Directory Permissions:** Verify the user has execute (`x`) permission on all parent directories required to traverse to the file.
4. **Check SELinux / AppArmor:** Run `getenforce` to verify whether SELinux in `Enforcing` mode is blocking the operation.
5. **Check Sudo Privileges:** Verify if elevated permissions (`sudo`) are needed.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you schedule a script to run every day at 1 AM on Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Using the `cron` scheduler:
1. Open crontab with `crontab -e`.
2. Add the following entry:
```bash
0 1 * * * /bin/bash /opt/scripts/daily_backup.sh >> /var/log/daily_backup.log 2>&1
```
- `0`: Minute 0
- `1`: Hour 1 (1:00 AM)
- `* * *`: Every day of the month, every month, every day of the week
- Output and errors are redirected to a log file for auditing.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the purpose and use of the Linux command 'ss \-tulmp'?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The `ss` (socket statistics) command is used to inspect network socket connections.
The flags `-tulmp` mean:
- `-t`: Display TCP sockets.
- `-u`: Display UDP sockets.
- `-l`: Show only listening sockets.
- `-m`: Show socket memory usage.
- `-p`: Show the process name and PID using the socket.
It is primarily used to check which services are listening on specific ports and identify their process IDs.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Which scripting languages have you worked with?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I work primarily with **Bash / Shell scripting** for operating system automation, backup routines, system maintenance, and CI/CD pipelines. I also write **Python** scripts using the `boto3` library for AWS cloud automation, interacting with REST APIs, and processing JSON/YAML configuration data.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you write a simple shell script that adds the numbers from 1 to 100?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
```bash
#!/bin/bash
sum=0
for i in {1..100}; do
  sum=$((sum + i))
done
echo "The sum of numbers from 1 to 100 is: $sum"
```
This loop runs from 1 to 100, adds each number to `sum`, and prints the final total (`5050`).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you explain the logic behind the shell script you just wrote to sum numbers from 1 to 100?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. We initialize an accumulator variable `sum=0`.
2. The `for i in {1..100}` construct generates a sequence of numbers from 1 through 100.
3. In each iteration of the loop, `$((sum + i))` performs arithmetic addition, adding the current value of `i` to `sum`.
4. After completing 100 iterations, the script prints the accumulated total, which equals 5050.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If an application is not responding, how would you troubleshoot and handle this situation, particularly in a Kubernetes environment?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Check Pod Status:** Run `kubectl get pods -n <namespace>` to check if pods are Running, Pending, or restarting (`CrashLoopBackOff`, `OOMKilled`).
2. **Inspect Pod Details & Events:** Run `kubectl describe pod <pod-name>` to check recent events and health probe failures.
3. **Inspect Application Logs:** Run `kubectl logs <pod-name> --tail=100 -f` to check for exceptions or deadlock traces.
4. **Verify Service Endpoints:** Run `kubectl get endpoints <service-name>` to verify that healthy pods are actively registered behind the Service.
5. **Check Resource Usage:** Run `kubectl top pod` to check for CPU throttling or memory saturation.
</details>

#### 【 MONITORING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Which monitoring tools have you worked with?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I have worked with:
- **Prometheus & Grafana:** For collecting metrics, monitoring Kubernetes clusters and Linux hosts, and visualizing dashboards.
- **AWS CloudWatch:** For EC2, RDS, ALB metrics, CloudWatch alarms, and log groups.

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would you set up a Grafana dashboard?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. Ensure the data source (e.g., Prometheus) is connected in Grafana.
2. Click **Dashboards > New Dashboard > Add Visualization**.
3. Select the data source and enter a PromQL query (e.g., `rate(http_requests_total[5m])`).
4. Select the chart visualization type (Time series, Stat panel, Gauge).
5. Customize panel title, legends, thresholds, and units.
6. Repeat for key metrics (CPU, memory, request rate, error rate), organize panels into rows, and click **Save Dashboard**.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would you set up alerting in Grafana?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. In Grafana, navigate to **Alerting > Alert rules > New alert rule**.
2. Select your data source, define the metric query, and set the condition threshold (e.g., `CPU utilization > 80%`).
3. Set the evaluation frequency and pending duration (e.g., evaluate every 1m `for: 5m`).
4. Select a **Contact Point** (Slack webhook, PagerDuty, or Email).
5. Add alert summary labels and details, and save the rule.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Do you have any knowledge about annotations in the context of Prometheus/Grafana monitoring?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes:
- **In Prometheus Alert Rules:** Annotations are key-value pairs used to add human-readable descriptions to alerts (e.g., `summary`, `description`, `runbook_url`). They support templating like `{{ $labels.instance }}` to provide immediate context to on-call engineers.
- **In Grafana Dashboards:** Annotations are visual markers (vertical lines or flags) overlaid on time-series graphs to indicate events like application deployments or server reboots, helping correlate performance changes with operational events.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you explain what templates are used for in Grafana's Alert Manager?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Grafana Alerting / Alertmanager, templates are reusable Go-template text blocks used to format outgoing alert notifications. Instead of sending raw, cluttered alert data, templates dynamically extract labels and annotations (e.g., `{{ .Labels.alertname }}`, `{{ .Annotations.description }}`) to produce clean, well-formatted messages for Slack, Microsoft Teams, or Email, including links to dashboards and runbooks.
</details>

#### 【 SYSTEM DESIGN 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you manage a situation where two P1 (highest priority) production issues occur simultaneously?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Triage & Assess Business Impact:** Quickly evaluate both incidents to determine which has greater customer or financial impact (e.g., total payment failure vs internal reporting outage).
2. **Divide and Delegate:** Split the team immediately so neither issue is ignored. Assign one senior engineer/lead to Incident 1 and another to Incident 2.
3. **Open Separate War Rooms:** Create separate incident bridges/Slack channels to keep communication focused and avoid confusion.
4. **Stakeholder Communication:** Appoint an incident commander or manager to send regular 15-minute status updates to leadership.
5. **Mitigate First:** Focus on quick restoration of service (rollback recent release, failover to standby, scale replicas) before deep root cause analysis.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is the difference between a primary key and a unique key in a database?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Primary Key:** Uniquely identifies each record in a table. A table can have only **one** primary key, and it **cannot contain NULL** values.
- **Unique Key:** Ensures all values in a column or set of columns are distinct. A table can have **multiple** unique keys, and it **can accept NULL** values (typically one NULL per column in most SQL databases).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is indexing in the context of databases?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
An index is a database data structure (typically a B-Tree) created on table columns to significantly speed up search and data retrieval queries (`SELECT` with `WHERE` or `JOIN`). Instead of scanning the entire table from top to bottom, the database engine uses the index to jump directly to matching rows. The tradeoff is that write operations (`INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE`) become slightly slower because indexes must also be updated.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is the difference between the DELETE, DROP, and TRUNCATE commands in SQL?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **`DELETE`:** DML command that removes specific rows matching a `WHERE` clause. It logs each deleted row, can be rolled back in a transaction, and triggers fire.
- **`TRUNCATE`:** DDL command that quickly removes all rows from a table by deallocating data pages. It cannot use a `WHERE` clause, is much faster than DELETE, uses minimal transaction log space, and resets auto-increment IDs.
- **`DROP`:** DDL command that completely deletes the table structure, data, schema, and indexes from the database.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Do you know what ITIL (IT Infrastructure Library / IT service management framework) is?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, ITIL is a globally recognized framework of best practices for delivering and managing IT services (ITSM). It provides standardized processes to align IT operations with business needs, covering core areas like Incident Management, Problem Management, Change Management, and Service Request management (typically managed using tools like ServiceNow or Jira Service Desk).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Do you have any knowledge of ITIL processes such as change management and problem management?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes:
- **Change Management:** A structured process to review, approve, and schedule changes to production environments (via Change Requests and Change Advisory Boards / CAB) to minimize risk and unexpected downtime.
- **Problem Management:** Focuses on identifying and resolving the root cause of recurring incidents. While incident management restores service as quickly as possible, problem management conducts Root Cause Analysis (RCA) to prevent the issue from happening again.
</details>

</details>
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Do you have experience working with Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have over 3 years of hands-on production experience with Kubernetes on AWS EKS. I manage Deployments, StatefulSets, Services, Ingress controllers, ConfigMaps, Secrets, and HPAs; handle Helm chart deployments; and troubleshoot cluster and application issues.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What kinds of issues have you troubleshooted in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I have troubleshooted:
- **CrashLoopBackOff:** Due to application runtime exceptions, incorrect config maps, or missing database credentials.
- **OOMKilled (Exit Code 137):** Containers exceeding memory limits during traffic surges.
- **ImagePullBackOff / ErrImagePull:** Typos in image tags, missing ECR IAM permissions, or network timeouts.
- **Pending Pods:** Node resource exhaustion, CPU/memory constraints, or unschedulable node taints.
- **Service/Ingress Connectivity:** Mismatched target ports, missing endpoints, or incorrect ALB ingress controller annotations.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you have two Kubernetes nodes and one node needs to be taken down for maintenance. How would you migrate all the pods running on that node to the other working node?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Cordon the node:** Mark the node as unschedulable so no new pods get scheduled on it:
   ```bash
   kubectl cordon <node-to-maintain>
   ```
2. **Drain the node:** Gracefully evict running pods to the other working node:
   ```bash
   kubectl drain <node-to-maintain> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
   ```
The deployment replica controller detects the evicted pods and automatically schedules replacement pods onto the remaining healthy worker node.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Before or after cordoning a node, how do you drain the target node to gracefully evict the existing pods running on it, and what command would you use?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
`kubectl drain` automatically cordons the node as its first action before evicting pods.
The command used is:
```bash
kubectl drain <node-name> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data --force
```
- `--ignore-daemonsets`: Leaves DaemonSet pods (like kube-proxy or monitoring agents) on the node.
- `--delete-emptydir-data`: Allows draining pods that use local `emptyDir` volumes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you describe the Kubernetes architecture, specifically what services/components run on the master (control plane) node versus the worker node?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Master (Control Plane) Components:**
  - `kube-apiserver`: The central management hub and entry point for all REST API commands.
  - `etcd`: Highly available distributed key-value store for all cluster data and state.
  - `kube-scheduler`: Assigns newly created pods to available worker nodes based on resource capacity.
  - `kube-controller-manager`: Runs core control loops (Deployment, Node, ReplicaSet controllers).
- **Worker Node Components:**
  - `kubelet`: Primary agent on each node that communicates with the API server and ensures assigned containers are running healthy.
  - `kube-proxy`: Manages network rules and traffic routing for Kubernetes Services.
  - `Container Runtime`: Software running the actual containers (e.g., `containerd`).
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose a customer is on AWS with a midsize environment of around 400-500 VMs, along with RDS, S3, and other AWS services, all of which were deployed manually. They now want to automate everything using Terraform and a CI/CD tool like Jenkins or GitLab, converting the manually deployed infrastructure into automated infrastructure-as-code. How would you approach automating this entire environment?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I would follow a structured phased approach:
1. **Discovery & Inventory:** Use AWS Resource Groups, Tagging, and CLI to catalog all 400-500 EC2 instances, VPCs, subnets, RDS databases, S3 buckets, and Security Groups.
2. **Modular Architecture:** Design clean, reusable Terraform modules (VPC module, EC2/Compute module, RDS module, S3 module).
3. **Phased Reverse Engineering:** Start from foundational networking (VPCs, subnets, route tables), then shared services (S3, RDS), and lastly compute (EC2).
4. **Automated Import:** Use tools like `terraformer` or modern Terraform `import` blocks to reverse-engineer and generate HCL code and state in bulk.
5. **State & Locking:** Configure S3 remote backend with DynamoDB state locking.
6. **CI/CD Integration:** Set up GitLab/Jenkins pipelines with automated `terraform plan` on merge requests and manual approval gates for `terraform apply`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Given that you would need to write Terraform code and import every existing resource individually (VPCs, subnets, IPs, disks, S3 buckets, etc.), wouldn't this be a very time-consuming and hectic task? How would you address that?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Doing it manually resource-by-resource for 500 VMs would indeed be slow and prone to errors. To address this, I would:
1. **Use Terraformer:** Leverage the open-source CLI tool `terraformer` to automatically scan existing AWS infrastructure and generate both the Terraform HCL files and the `terraform.tfstate` in bulk.
2. **Use Terraform 1.5+ Declarative Import:** Write automated Python/Boto3 scripts to inspect AWS resources and output bulk `import {}` blocks.
3. **Batch by Environment/App:** Divide the infrastructure into manageable waves (Dev first, then QA, then Prod) rather than doing everything at once.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Since the VPCs, subnets, and other resources already exist because they were deployed manually, is there a better way to import this existing manually-deployed environment into Terraform state?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, using **Terraform 1.5+ declarative `import` blocks** combined with automatic code generation:
```hcl
import {
  to = aws_vpc.existing_vpc
  id = "vpc-0123456789abcdef0"
}
```
When you run `terraform plan -generate-config-out=generated_vpc.tf`, Terraform inspects the live AWS resource and automatically generates the corresponding HCL code. This is significantly faster and less error-prone than legacy manual CLI imports.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: When using Terraform import to bring existing manually-created resources under Terraform management, do you have to import each resource one by one?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
With the older CLI command `terraform import <resource> <id>`, you had to execute it one by one (or write custom shell loops). However, with modern Terraform (v1.5+), you can define hundreds of declarative `import {}` blocks in your code and import them all in a single `terraform apply` run. Alternatively, tools like `terraformer` import entire resource categories (all subnets, all instances) in a single command.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Where do you store the Terraform state files?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We store Terraform state files remotely in an **Amazon S3 bucket**.
Best practices applied:
- **Bucket Versioning:** Enabled to preserve historical state versions and allow recovery from corruption.
- **Server-Side Encryption:** Configured with AWS KMS to protect sensitive data stored in state.
- **Block Public Access:** Strictly enforced to prevent unauthorized access.
- **State Locking:** Backed by an Amazon DynamoDB table (or native S3 locking in newer versions) to prevent concurrent executions.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How is the Terraform state file locked to prevent concurrent modifications?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Terraform uses an **Amazon DynamoDB table** configured with a partition key named `LockID` (string). When an engineer or CI/CD pipeline runs `terraform plan` or `terraform apply`, Terraform writes a lock record containing the execution details to DynamoDB. Any other concurrent execution detects the lock and halts with an error, preventing conflicting writes until the running operation releases the lock.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Isn't DynamoDB for Terraform state locking deprecated now, with Terraform using native S3 state locking instead?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
It is not deprecated, but Terraform v1.10 introduced native S3 state locking using S3 conditional writes (`PutObject` with conditional checks), which allows locking directly inside S3 without requiring DynamoDB. While native S3 locking is the simpler modern option for new projects, DynamoDB-based locking remains fully supported and is the battle-tested standard across existing enterprise production environments.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the purpose of the tfvars file in Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A `terraform.tfvars` (or `<env>.tfvars`) file is used to assign concrete values to input variables defined in `variables.tf`. It separates variable definitions (schema and data types) from specific environment configurations (e.g., `instance_type = "t3.micro"` for dev vs `"m5.large"` for prod), allowing the same Terraform codebase to manage multiple environments cleanly.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If you have three environments (prod, dev, QA) each with its own tfvars file (prod.tfvars, dev.tfvars, qa.tfvars), how would you configure Terraform so that running 'terraform apply' automatically picks up the correct tfvars file for the target environment (e.g., prod.tfvars) without explicitly specifying it on the command line?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Terraform automatically loads variable files named `terraform.tfvars` or `*.auto.tfvars`.
To make this automatic:
1. **Directory-Based Environment Structure (Industry Best Practice):** Create folders `environments/dev`, `environments/qa`, and `environments/prod`. Each directory has its own `terraform.tfvars` and calls the root module. Running `terraform apply` inside `environments/prod` automatically picks up its local `terraform.tfvars`.
2. **Environment Variable:** Set the environment variable `TF_VAR_FILE=prod.tfvars` or define individual variables via `TF_VAR_<var_name>`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If a customer asked you to perform a cost assessment to review whether their EC2 instances are right-sized and to check if they are overspending on cloud, what would be your approach to performing this assessment?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Data Collection:** Review AWS Compute Optimizer and CloudWatch metrics over the past 14-30 days for CPU utilization, memory usage, network, and disk I/O.
2. **Identify Over-Provisioned Instances:** Flag instances consistently running below 15-20% CPU and low memory.
3. **Audit Ancillary Waste:** Identify unattached EBS volumes, old EBS snapshots, and idle Elastic IPs.
4. **Review Purchasing Models:** Identify steady-state 24/7 instances running on On-Demand pricing that can transition to Compute Savings Plans for 30-60% discounts.
5. **Present Findings:** Deliver a structured report categorizing quick wins (cleanup), medium-term adjustments (downsizing), and long-term commitments (Savings Plans).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In the context of cost optimization for S3 storage, how would you identify which data needs to be moved to a different storage tier?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Amazon S3 Storage Lens:** Provides organization-wide analytics on storage activity, showing which buckets have large amounts of inactive or non-current version data.
2. **S3 Storage Class Analysis:** Monitors object access patterns over 30 to 60 days for specific bucket prefixes and recommends when objects can be safely transitioned from S3 Standard to S3 Standard-IA or Glacier.
3. **S3 Inventory Reports:** Generates periodic CSV reports of all object metadata, age, and storage classes to analyze data age distribution.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What S3 lifecycle policy would you apply to move infrequently used data to lower-cost storage tiers?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A standard S3 Lifecycle policy would include:
1. **Transition to Standard-IA:** Move objects to `STANDARD_IA` after **30 days** of creation (for infrequently accessed data).
2. **Transition to Glacier Flexible Retrieval:** Move objects to `GLACIER` after **90 days** (for archival and compliance).
3. **Expiration:** Permanently delete non-current object versions after **180 days** or 1 year.
Alternatively, for data with unpredictable access patterns, we enable **S3 Intelligent-Tiering** to automatically move objects between frequent and infrequent tiers with zero operational overhead.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In terms of cost optimization, how would you optimize the VMs (EC2 instances) in this environment?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Right-Sizing:** Downsize over-provisioned instances based on Compute Optimizer recommendations (e.g., moving from `m5.xlarge` to `m5.large`).
2. **Modernize Generations:** Upgrade older instance families to newer generations (e.g., `t2` to `t3`, `m5` to `m6i` or Graviton `m6g`), which provide 15-20% better price-to-performance.
3. **Instance Scheduling:** Implement AWS Instance Scheduler to automatically stop Dev and QA instances during nights and weekends, saving up to 60% on non-production compute.
4. **Savings Plans:** Purchase 1-year or 3-year Compute Savings Plans for predictable baseline production workloads.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If AWS's recommendation tool identified around 50 EC2 instances that could be right-sized due to less than 20% CPU and memory utilization, what would be your next step? Would you right-size them immediately?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
No, I would never right-size production instances immediately.
My next steps:
1. **Verify Metric Window:** Check if the low utilization period excluded month-end peaks, seasonal spikes, or batch processing jobs.
2. **Verify Memory Metrics:** Confirm that the CloudWatch agent was actively streaming memory metrics, as AWS default metrics only track CPU.
3. **Consult Application Teams:** Confirm workload characteristics and SLAs with the application owners.
4. **Test in Non-Prod:** Downsize instances in Dev/QA first to ensure no performance degradation.
5. **Scheduled Change:** Raise a formal Change Request (CR) and downsize production instances during scheduled maintenance windows with rollback plans ready.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Given the AWS right-sizing recommendations for those 50 underutilized EC2 instances, what would you recommend to the customer?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I would present a clear, prioritized recommendation:
1. **Categorize by Risk:** Split the 50 instances into Non-Production (Dev/QA) and Production.
2. **Quick Wins (Non-Prod):** Recommend immediately downsizing the Dev/QA instances and applying automated start/stop schedules.
3. **Phased Production Downsizing:** Recommend downsizing production instances step-by-step (e.g., from `2xlarge` to `xlarge`) during maintenance windows after testing.
4. **Cost-Benefit Projections:** Provide estimated monthly and annual dollar savings (e.g., 'Downsizing these instances will save approximately $3,000 per month').
</details>

</details>
</details>

#### 【 MONITORING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you set up and configure monitoring for a hybrid environment that connects on-premises infrastructure to an Azure data center?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Network Connectivity:** Ensure reliable connectivity via Azure ExpressRoute or Site-to-Site VPN.
2. **Deploy Azure Arc:** Install the Azure Arc agent on on-premises virtual and physical servers, registering them as managed Azure resources.
3. **Deploy Azure Monitor Agent (AMA):** Install the AMA agent on both Azure VMs and on-premises Arc-enabled servers.
4. **Centralized Log Analytics Workspace:** Use Azure Data Collection Rules (DCRs) to route performance metrics, syslog, and event logs from both environments into a single Log Analytics Workspace.
5. **Dashboards & Alerts:** Build unified Grafana or Azure Monitor dashboards and configure alert rules to monitor host health, network tunnel status, and application metrics across both sites.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose your application shows a latency of 5 (seconds), but CPU and memory metrics look normal. How would you perform root cause analysis (RCA) to identify the source of this latency issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Since CPU and memory are normal, the delay is caused by downstream dependencies, I/O waits, or application threading:
1. **Trace via APM / Distributed Tracing:** Check tools like Azure Application Insights, Datadog, or Jaeger to inspect end-to-end request spans and identify exactly which component is taking 5 seconds.
2. **Check Database Queries:** Inspect slow query logs, table lock waits, and database connection pool exhaustion.
3. **Check External API Calls:** Verify if the application is waiting synchronously on slow third-party services or microservices.
4. **Check Network & DNS:** Test network latency, packet loss, and DNS resolution response times between tiers using `curl -w "@curl-format.txt"` and `traceroute`.
5. **Check Thread Dumps:** Inspect application thread dumps for thread contention or locks on shared resources.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you scale Azure Monitor to handle large environments?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Centralize Log Analytics Workspaces:** Use a well-planned workspace topology (e.g., one per region or compliance boundary) rather than fragmented workspaces.
2. **Data Collection Rules (DCRs):** Filter out noisy, unwanted log categories at source using DCR transformation queries before ingestion.
3. **Data Tiering:** Use Basic Logs or Auxiliary Logs (lower ingestion cost) for high-volume debugging logs, reserving Analytics Logs for alerting and analytics.
4. **Manage Data Retention:** Set active retention to 30 days and export long-term logs to low-cost Azure Storage Accounts.
5. **Commitment Tiers:** Move from pay-as-you-go to Capacity Reservation pricing tiers (100 GB/day+) for predictable high-volume workloads.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In the context of Azure Monitor, what is an action rule?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Azure Monitor, an **Action Rule** (now called an **Alert Processing Rule**) allows you to apply actions or modifications to fired alerts at scale across subscriptions or resource groups.
Common uses:
- **Suppress Notifications:** Mute alert notifications during planned maintenance windows without disabling the underlying alert rules.
- **Attach Action Groups:** Automatically attach specific notification channels (e.g., SMS, email, or webhook to ServiceNow) to all alerts generated within a resource group.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● In the case of a VM CPU spike, how would you monitor and configure alerts for high CPU usage?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Configure Alert Rule:** In Azure Monitor or AWS CloudWatch, create a metric alert rule for the VM's CPU metric (`Percentage CPU` or `CPUUtilization`).
2. **Set Threshold & Duration:** Set condition `CPU > 80%` evaluated over a `5-minute` rolling window (preventing false alarms on brief spikes).
3. **Configure Action Group / SNS:** Link the alert to an Action Group or SNS Topic that notifies the team via Slack, PagerDuty, or Email.
4. **Troubleshoot on VM:** When alerted, log into the VM and run `top` / `htop` and `ps aux --sort=-%cpu | head -10` to identify the culprit process.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you are facing a storage cost issue where log ingestion and metric retention costs have skyrocketed. What steps would you take to address this?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Identify Top Ingestion Sources:** Query Log Analytics or CloudWatch to identify the top 5 log groups generating the highest ingestion volume.
2. **Reduce Application Log Verbosity:** Switch verbose `DEBUG` and `TRACE` log levels in production to `INFO` or `WARN`.
3. **Filter at Ingestion Source:** Use Data Collection Rules (DCRs) or Fluentd/Logstash filters to drop health checks, HTTP 200 access logs, and noisy debug events before ingestion.
4. **Lower Retention Periods:** Shorten retention from unlimited or 90 days to 14 or 30 days.
5. **Archive to Cold Storage:** Export historical logs to Amazon S3 Glacier or Azure Blob Cool/Archive storage for long-term compliance at a fraction of the cost.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Do you have any knowledge of the OpenTelemetry (OTel) framework?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, OpenTelemetry (OTel) is an open-source, vendor-neutral observability framework under the CNCF. It provides standardized APIs, SDKs, and an **OTel Collector** to generate, collect, and export telemetry data—specifically traces, metrics, and logs. Its major advantage is preventing vendor lock-in: applications instrumented once with OTel can send telemetry to any backend (Prometheus, Jaeger, Datadog, AWS X-Ray, or Azure Monitor) simply by updating collector export configurations.
</details>

#### 【 SECURITY 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you have configured monitoring tools like Kibana or Grafana such that different teams can view the data, but only the SRE team can edit it. How would you configure this access control setup?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Using Role-Based Access Control (RBAC):
- **In Grafana:**
  1. Integrate Grafana with central SSO (Azure AD, Okta, or LDAP).
  2. Assign the **Viewer** role to developer and business teams (enabling them to view dashboards and execute queries without editing rights).
  3. Assign the **Editor** or **Admin** role exclusively to the SRE team.
  4. Use Folder Permissions to restrict editing of critical production dashboards.
- **In Kibana:**
  1. Go to **Stack Management > Roles and Spaces**.
  2. Assign the built-in `kibana_user` (Read-only access to spaces) to development teams.
  3. Grant `kibana_admin` or full customize write privileges on spaces only to the SRE group.
</details>

</details>
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What CI/CD platforms have you used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In my 5 years of DevOps experience, I have primarily used **Jenkins** as our main CI/CD tool to automate builds, tests, and deployments. I write Declarative Jenkinsfiles to maintain pipelines as code. In addition to Jenkins, I have hands-on experience with **GitLab CI** and **GitHub Actions** for containerized CI workflows, and I use **Argo CD** for GitOps continuous delivery into Kubernetes clusters.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you set up a pipeline in Jenkins? Can you walk through the stages you typically include?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I set up pipelines using a Declarative `Jenkinsfile` stored directly in the Git repository. The standard stages in our pipeline are:
1. **Checkout:** Pulls the latest code from GitHub or GitLab.
2. **Build:** Compiles the application code (e.g., `mvn clean package` or `npm run build`).
3. **Test & SonarQube:** Runs unit tests and scans code quality and security gates via SonarQube.
4. **Docker Build & Push:** Builds the Docker image tagged with the Git commit SHA and pushes it to AWS ECR.
5. **Deploy:** Updates Kubernetes manifests using Helm or `kubectl apply` to roll out the new image.
6. **Post Actions:** Sends Slack notifications on build success or failure.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Suppose you want to display certain messages on the console after deployment, such as showing a URL or a command the user needs to run — how would you achieve that?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In a Declarative Jenkins pipeline, I achieve this using the `echo` step inside the `post { success { ... } }` block or at the end of the deployment stage:

```groovy
post {
    success {
        echo "=========================================="
        echo "Deployment Successful!"
        echo "Application URL: https://app.example.com"
        echo "Check status command: kubectl get pods -n prod"
        echo "=========================================="
    }
}
```
This cleanly prints the application endpoint and verification commands directly in the Jenkins console log for the team to see.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Where exactly have you set up the deployment stage for the Kubernetes environment — what platform did you use?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our setup, the deployment stage runs inside a Jenkins build agent configured with AWS CLI, `kubectl`, and Helm. The target platform is **AWS EKS (Elastic Kubernetes Service)**. The Jenkins agent assumes an AWS IAM role with RBAC permissions on the EKS cluster, runs `aws eks update-kubeconfig`, and applies the manifests directly to our EKS namespaces.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Are you using native Kubernetes manifest files, Helm charts, or something else for deploying into the Kubernetes environment?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We primarily use **Helm charts** for deploying applications to Kubernetes. While native Kubernetes manifests (`deployment.yaml`, `service.yaml`, `ingress.yaml`) work well for static setups, Helm allows us to templatize our manifests. We manage different environments (dev, QA, prod) cleanly by maintaining a single base chart and simply overriding values using separate `values-dev.yaml` and `values-prod.yaml` files.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Is it possible to display such post-deployment messages using a Helm chart?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, Helm provides this feature using the `NOTES.txt` file located inside the chart's `templates/` directory. When you run `helm install` or `helm upgrade`, Helm automatically evaluates and prints the content of `NOTES.txt` to the terminal. We use it to show dynamic application URLs, ingress hostnames, and useful `kubectl` commands to verify pod status.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What kind of AWS resources have you set up using Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Using Terraform, I have provisioned end-to-end AWS infrastructure, including:
- **Networking:** VPC, public and private subnets across multiple AZs, Internet Gateway, NAT Gateways, and Route Tables.
- **Compute:** EC2 instances, Launch Templates, and Auto Scaling Groups (ASG).
- **Containers:** AWS EKS cluster, managed node groups, and ECR repositories.
- **Load Balancing:** Application Load Balancers (ALB), Target Groups, and SSL listeners.
- **Storage & Database:** S3 buckets, EBS volumes, and Amazon RDS PostgreSQL/MySQL databases.
- **Security:** IAM roles, instance profiles, and Security Groups.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you are given a task to set up Terraform and provision resources into AWS completely from scratch, with nothing existing in AWS currently. What checklist or set of steps would you follow while setting up the Terraform pipeline?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Here is the checklist I follow:
1. **Remote State Backend:** Create an S3 bucket with versioning enabled for storing state, and a DynamoDB table with `LockID` for state locking.
2. **Provider & Backend Config:** Configure `provider.tf` for AWS and `backend.tf` pointing to the remote S3/DynamoDB.
3. **Modular Code Structure:** Organize code into reusable modules (VPC, Security Groups, EC2/EKS) with separate `variables.tf` and `outputs.tf`.
4. **Environment Separation:** Use separate folders or `*.tfvars` files for dev, staging, and prod.
5. **CI/CD Pipeline:** Set up a pipeline in Jenkins or GitHub Actions with stages: `terraform fmt -check`, `terraform init`, `terraform validate`, `terraform plan`, and manual approval for `terraform apply`.
6. **Least Privilege:** Configure IAM roles with least-privilege permissions for the CI/CD runner.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is the purpose of storing the Terraform state file in an S3 bucket?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Storing the Terraform state file in an S3 bucket provides:
1. **Centralized Team Collaboration:** All team members and CI/CD pipelines access the single source of truth rather than keeping local state files.
2. **State Locking (via DynamoDB):** Prevents concurrent executions where two people or pipelines apply changes at the same time and corrupt the state.
3. **Safety & History:** S3 bucket versioning keeps a history of all state changes, allowing recovery if the state is accidentally damaged.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you have a Terraform project managing infrastructure that you've been maintaining for about 3 months, and recently created new EC2 instances/web apps using it. If you then revert the Terraform state file to a version from one month ago and run 'terraform apply', what will happen?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Because the 1-month-old state file does not know about the new EC2 instances and resources created recently:
- Terraform compares the current `.tf` code with the old state and sees these resources exist in code but are missing from state.
- It will attempt to **create them again**.
- For resources requiring globally unique names (like S3 buckets or IAM roles), Terraform will fail with an "already exists" error.
- For resources like EC2 instances that don't enforce unique names, Terraform will provision **duplicate instances**, causing unwanted infrastructure and extra costs.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If a 'prevent\_destroy' lifecycle block has been applied to those resources, what will happen when you run 'terraform apply' after reverting the state file?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In this scenario, because the reverted state file does not contain those resources, Terraform's plan is to *create* them, not destroy them. Therefore, the `prevent_destroy` block will not trigger during creation. 
However, if you had also reverted the `.tf` code to one month ago while the state file still had them, Terraform would plan to destroy them—and `prevent_destroy` would immediately throw an error and block the apply, preventing accidental deletion.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If you actually want those resources to be destroyed despite the 'prevent\_destroy' lifecycle block, how would you go about destroying them?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To destroy a resource protected by `prevent_destroy`:
1. Edit the Terraform code for that resource and remove or comment out `lifecycle { prevent_destroy = true }`.
2. Run `terraform apply` or push the change through Git so Terraform updates the resource definition.
3. Then remove the resource block from the code and run `terraform apply` (or run `terraform destroy -target=<resource>`).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Suppose the same Terraform pipeline is working fine after destroying resources, but 2-3 days later you encounter an error while running the pipeline. What troubleshooting approach would you follow?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
My troubleshooting approach is:
1. **Check Pipeline Logs:** Inspect the exact error message in the CI/CD console (e.g., state lock timeout, authentication failure, or resource limit).
2. **Check State Lock:** If locked, verify if a previous run crashed and release the lock using `terraform force-unlock <LOCK-ID>`.
3. **Run `terraform plan` locally:** Run a read-only plan against non-prod to detect any configuration drift.
4. **Review Git Commits:** Check if any recent commits modified provider versions, modules, or variable files.
5. **Inspect AWS CloudTrail:** Check if someone manually deleted or changed dependent resources in the AWS console.
</details>

</details>

2. **Standard CI/CD Templates:** Use shared pipeline libraries (Jenkins Shared Libraries or reusable GitHub Actions) enforcing standard linting, testing, and security gates.
3. **Golden AMIs and Containers:** Use Packer to build standardized, pre-hardened base AMIs and Docker images.

<summary><h3>Level 1</h3></summary>

*Date: 05-08-2026 08:58 PM*

#### 【 LINUX 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain how the Linux boot process works and describe its different stages?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The Linux boot process follows 6 core stages:
1. **BIOS / UEFI:** Performs hardware Power-On Self-Test (POST) and locates the bootable drive.
2. **MBR / GPT:** Reads the master boot record (512 bytes) or EFI partition containing the bootloader.
3. **GRUB (Grand Unified Bootloader):** Displays the boot menu, selects kernel version, and loads the kernel image and `initramfs` into RAM.
4. **Kernel:** Mounts root filesystem in read-only mode, initializes hardware drivers, and starts the initial process (`PID 1`).
5. **Systemd (Init):** Reads the default target (e.g., `multi-user.target`), starts essential system services, mounts filesystems, and brings up networking.
6. **Runlevel / Target:** Presents the login prompt (CLI or GUI).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: When a Linux system is normally booted, what runlevel is it typically at?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In modern Linux systems using `systemd`, a server typically boots into **Runlevel 3**, which corresponds to `multi-user.target` (multi-user CLI mode with networking and no GUI). If it is a desktop or workstation system with a graphical interface, it boots into **Runlevel 5**, which corresponds to `graphical.target`. You can check the current target using `systemctl get-default` or `runlevel`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you troubleshoot slowness on a system or server?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
When troubleshooting a slow Linux server, I check 4 core resources:
1. **CPU:** Run `uptime` to check 1, 5, 15-minute load averages against core count, and use `top` or `htop` to identify CPU-intensive processes.
2. **Memory:** Run `free -m` to check used, free, and swap usage. High swap usage indicates memory exhaustion.
3. **Disk I/O:** Run `df -h` to verify disk space is not at 100%, and `iostat -xz 1` or `iotop` to spot processes saturating disk read/write bandwidth.
4. **Network:** Run `ss -tunap` or `netstat -plnt` to inspect open ports, connection spikes, and dropped packets.
5. **Logs:** Check `journalctl -xe` or `/var/log/messages` to spot errors or hung processes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you ever encountered a situation where inodes on a system were full, and if so, what did you do to resolve it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have encountered an "out of disk space" error even when `df -h` showed plenty of free gigabytes. 
1. **Diagnosis:** I ran `df -i` which revealed that the root or `/var` filesystem was at 100% inode utilization.
2. **Root Cause:** A crashed service was generating millions of tiny log or session files in `/var/log` or `/var/spool/clientmqueue`.
3. **Resolution:** I located the culprit directory using:
   ```bash
   for d in /var/*; do echo -n "$d: "; find "$d" -type f | wc -l; done
   ```
   Once identified, I deleted the backlog using `find /path/to/dir -type f -delete` (since standard `rm *` fails with argument list too long). Finally, I fixed log rotation in `/etc/logrotate.conf` to prevent recurrence.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain the difference between a process and a thread?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Process:** An independent program in execution with its own dedicated memory space, file descriptors, and system resources. Processes are fully isolated; one process crashing does not directly crash another. Creating a process requires significant system overhead.
- **Thread:** A lightweight execution unit created *inside* a process. Multiple threads share the parent process's memory space, code, and global variables, but have their own individual stack and program counter. Threads are much faster to create and communicate, but an unhandled exception in one thread can crash the entire process.
</details>
