<summary><h3>Level 1</h3></summary>

*Date: *

#### 【 LINUX 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between a soft link and a hard link in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Soft Link (Symbolic Link):** A small pointer file containing the path string of the target file (like a shortcut). It can cross filesystems and can link to directories. If the target file is deleted, the soft link becomes broken (dangling). Created with `ln -s target link`.
- **Hard Link:** An additional directory entry pointing directly to the target file's inode number. It cannot span across different filesystems and cannot link to directories. If the original file is deleted, data remains accessible through the hard link until all links are removed. Created with `ln target link`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In what scenarios would you prefer using a soft link over a hard link in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I prefer soft links when:
1. Linking directories (hard links cannot link directories in Linux).
2. Linking files across different partitions or mounted filesystems (hard links are restricted to the same filesystem).
3. Managing application software versions (e.g., creating a symlink `/opt/app/current` pointing to `/opt/app/v1.2.0`, allowing seamless version switching by updating the symlink).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you list some common Linux networking commands used for troubleshooting connectivity issues?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Common Linux networking commands I use daily:
- `ping <host>`: Checks basic ICMP network reachability.
- `curl -I <url>` / `wget`: Tests HTTP/HTTPS connectivity and inspects response headers.
- `ss -tulnp` or `netstat -tulnp`: Checks active listening ports and established socket connections.
- `nslookup <domain>` / `dig <domain>`: Diagnoses DNS resolution issues.
- `traceroute <host>`: Identifies network path hops and packet latency.
- `nc -zv <host> <port>` or `telnet <host> <port>`: Checks if a specific remote port is open through firewalls.
</details>

#### 【 GIT 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the exact difference between git merge and git rebase?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **`git merge`:** Combines the histories of two branches by creating a distinct "merge commit". It preserves the exact chronological history and context of when branches diverged and joined, but can create a cluttered, non-linear commit graph.
- **`git rebase`:** Takes the commits from your branch and replays them one by one on top of the target branch. This creates a clean, completely linear commit history without merge commits. However, it rewrites commit hashes, so it must never be used on shared public branches.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is git cherry-pick, and when would you use it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
`git cherry-pick <commit-hash>` selects a specific commit from one branch and applies its exact changes onto your current working branch.
It is typically used for **hotfixes**: for instance, when a critical bug is fixed in the `develop` branch, instead of merging all other unfinished features from `develop` into `main`, you cherry-pick only that specific bugfix commit into the production release branch.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Is git cherry-pick generally considered safe or recommended to use?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
It is safe when used selectively for hotfixes or backporting patches. However, it is not recommended as a routine merging strategy because it creates duplicate commits with different hashes containing the same changes. This can lead to messy history and difficult merge conflicts in future branch merges.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Have you personally used git cherry-pick in a real production scenario?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes. We had a production issue where a payment webhook timeout bug needed an immediate patch. The fix was committed to our sprint feature branch. Rather than waiting to release the entire sprint, I cherry-picked that specific commit into our `hotfix` branch, verified it in QA, and pushed it to production via the release pipeline.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What happens to commit parent IDs/hashes during a git rebase versus a git merge?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **`git merge`:** Original commit IDs, parent IDs, and commit hashes remain completely untouched. A new merge commit is created with two parent IDs.
- **`git rebase`:** The original commits are recreated on top of the new base branch. Because the parent ID and timestamps change, Git generates completely **new commit hashes** for all rebased commits.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Between Jenkins and GitHub Actions, which CI/CD tool are you more comfortable working with?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I am comfortable with both. I have worked most extensively with Jenkins for large-scale enterprise declarative multi-branch pipelines and shared libraries. Recently, I also frequently use GitHub Actions due to its modern YAML syntax, fast execution, and native integration with GitHub pull requests.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Have you personally written any workflows in GitHub Actions?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have written multiple production workflows in `.github/workflows/`. These include automated PR validation workflows that run unit tests and SonarQube scans, container build and push workflows targeting AWS ECR using OIDC, and deployment workflows triggering Helm releases on Amazon EKS.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between a scripted pipeline and a declarative pipeline in Jenkins?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Declarative Pipeline:** The modern, recommended standard. Uses structured syntax (`pipeline { agent any; stages { stage { steps { ... } } } }`). It has built-in syntax validation, cleaner readability, and standard post-actions (`post { always/failure }`).
- **Scripted Pipeline:** The older Groovy-based approach (`node { stage { ... } }`). It allows arbitrary Groovy programming logic and loops, but is more complex, less readable, and harder to maintain.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Which type of Jenkins pipeline (scripted or declarative) do you use in your current organization?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our organization, we use **Declarative Pipelines** exclusively as our company standard. It ensures consistency across all teams, makes pipelines easy to read and review, and integrates seamlessly with our Jenkins Shared Libraries.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a Jenkins build works on the controller but fails on a specific agent, what would you check to troubleshoot this?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I check the following troubleshooting points on that agent:
1. **Installed Toolchains:** Verify required tool versions (Java, Maven, Docker CLI, Node) are installed and accessible in the agent's system PATH.
2. **Environment Variables:** Verify environment variables and credentials configured on the agent match the controller.
3. **Disk Space:** Run `df -h` on the agent to ensure `/var/lib/jenkins` or the workspace partition is not at 100% capacity.
4. **Permissions:** Ensure the `jenkins` user on the agent has ownership and execute permissions on workspace directories and Docker socket (`/var/run/docker.sock`).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you had the opportunity to work with GitOps practices/tools?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I work with GitOps practices where Git serves as the single source of truth for our infrastructure and application configurations. Any change to deployment manifests in Git triggers automated synchronization into our Kubernetes clusters.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Have you worked with tools like Argo CD or Helm?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I work with both. We use **Helm** as our Kubernetes package manager to template manifests (`Chart.yaml`, `values.yaml`, templates for Deployment/Service/Ingress). We use **Argo CD** to continuously monitor our Git repository and automatically reconcile Helm chart releases in our Amazon EKS clusters.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Do you have any knowledge or experience with Argo CD?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Argo CD is a declarative GitOps continuous delivery tool running inside Kubernetes. It follows a pull-based model: Argo CD constantly compares the live state of the Kubernetes cluster with the desired state defined in the Git repository. If any deviation or configuration drift is detected, it flags the app as "OutOfSync" and can automatically sync the cluster to match Git, eliminating manual `kubectl apply` commands.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between a Deployment and a StatefulSet in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Deployment:** Designed for stateless applications (e.g., web APIs, microservices). Pods are identical, interchangeable, have random string suffixes (e.g., `app-7b89f-xyz`), share storage, and can be updated or terminated in any order.
- **StatefulSet:** Designed for stateful applications (e.g., databases, Kafka, Elasticsearch). Pods have stable, predictable ordinal names (`db-0`, `db-1`), maintain persistent network identities, boot and shut down in strict sequential order, and each pod attaches its own dedicated persistent volume via `volumeClaimTemplates`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between resource requests and limits in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Requests:** The minimum amount of CPU and Memory guaranteed for a container. The Kubernetes scheduler uses requests to find a worker node that has sufficient available allocatable capacity to place the pod.
- **Limits:** The hard upper ceiling of CPU and Memory the container is allowed to consume. If a container exceeds its memory limit, the Linux kernel terminates it with an **OOMKilled** error (Exit Code 137). If it exceeds CPU limit, CPU throttling occurs.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● In Kubernetes, if a node fails, what happens to that node and the pods running on it, and what does the scheduler do in response?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. The **Node Controller** in the control plane stops receiving heartbeats from the node's Kubelet.
2. After the grace period (default 5 minutes `node-monitor-grace-period`), the node is marked as `NotReady`.
3. The pods running on that failed node are marked for deletion.
4. The deployment controller notices the desired replica count is not met and creates replacement pods in `Pending` state.
5. The **Scheduler** evaluates the remaining healthy worker nodes and binds the new pods to them, restoring the desired replica count.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If Kubernetes pods are running but the application is inaccessible, what could be the possible causes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Common root causes:
1. **Readiness Probe failing:** The pod is Running, but failing its readiness check. As a result, its IP is excluded from the Service endpoints list (`kubectl get endpoints`).
2. **Port Mismatch:** The Service `port` or `targetPort` does not match the application's actual listening `containerPort`.
3. **Selector Label Mismatch:** The Service `spec.selector` labels do not match the labels in the Pod template metadata.
4. **Ingress or NetworkPolicy:** Ingress controller rules may have a routing path typo, or a Kubernetes NetworkPolicy is blocking ingress traffic to the pod.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a Kubernetes pod is constantly restarting, what would you check to diagnose the issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I follow these steps:
1. Run `kubectl describe pod <pod-name>` to check the `Last State`, `Exit Code`, and `Events` section.
2. If exit code is **137**, the pod was **OOMKilled** (exceeded memory limit).
3. Run `kubectl logs <pod-name> --previous` to inspect the application crash logs and stack trace just prior to restart.
4. Check whether misconfigured **Liveness Probes** are timing out and forcing container restarts.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is service discovery in the context of Kubernetes/microservices?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Service discovery is the mechanism that allows microservices to dynamically locate and communicate with each other without hardcoding IP addresses. In Kubernetes, this is powered by **CoreDNS** and **Services**:
- When a Service is created, Kubernetes assigns it a virtual static IP (ClusterIP) and a DNS record (e.g., `order-service.default.svc.cluster.local`).
- CoreDNS resolves this domain name.
- Kube-proxy / iptables routes traffic sent to that ClusterIP across all healthy backend pods load-balanced automatically.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between an Ingress and a Load Balancer in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **LoadBalancer Service:** Operates at Layer 4 (TCP/UDP). It spins up a dedicated cloud load balancer (e.g., AWS Network Load Balancer) for every single service. This becomes expensive and does not support URL path-based routing.
- **Ingress:** Operates at Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS). It uses a single shared external load balancer (via an Ingress Controller like NGINX) to route traffic to dozens of internal services based on domain hostnames (`api.example.com`) and URL paths (`/orders`, `/users`), while handling central SSL termination.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is Terraform drift?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Terraform drift occurs when the real-world configuration of cloud infrastructure differs from what is documented in the Terraform code and recorded in the state file (`terraform.tfstate`). Drift typically happens when someone makes manual changes in the AWS Console, runs ad-hoc CLI commands, or external scripts alter resources. Running `terraform plan` detects and highlights these discrepancies.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Have you personally performed a Terraform import before?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have performed Terraform imports multiple times when adopting legacy AWS resources (like pre-existing S3 buckets, security groups, and VPC subnets) that were originally created manually via the AWS Console.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you walk me through the steps involved in performing a Terraform import?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Write Resource Configuration:** Define the empty/skeleton resource block in your `.tf` file matching the target resource type:
   `resource "aws_s3_bucket" "app_bucket" {}`
2. **Run Import Command:** Run the CLI command:
   `terraform import aws_s3_bucket.app_bucket <existing-bucket-name>`
   (Or use the modern `import { to = ... id = ... }` block in Terraform 1.5+).
3. **Run Plan:** Execute `terraform plan` to see differences between the imported state and your local `.tf` code.
4. **Update Code:** Fill in the matching arguments in the resource block until `terraform plan` reports: "No changes. Your infrastructure matches the configuration."
5. **Commit:** Commit the completed `.tf` configuration to Git.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Is that the complete process for a Terraform import, or are there additional steps involved?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
That is the complete core process. The crucial final step is ensuring zero configuration drift—you iteratively refine the resource arguments in code until `terraform plan` shows 0 to add, 0 to change, and 0 to destroy. Once verified clean, the code is merged into Git, establishing Terraform as the official owner of the resource.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are Terraform taints?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Tainting marks a specific resource in the Terraform state file as degraded or broken. During the next `terraform apply`, Terraform will destroy and recreate that resource from scratch. In modern Terraform (v0.15+), instead of the deprecated `terraform taint` command, we use the replace flag: `terraform apply -replace="aws_instance.web"`.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you connect two VPCs when you need to share resources between them?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To connect two VPCs, we establish an **AWS VPC Peering** connection:
1. Create a VPC Peering connection from VPC A to VPC B (ensuring non-overlapping CIDR blocks).
2. Accept the peering connection request in VPC B.
3. Update route tables in both VPCs: in VPC A, add a route to VPC B's CIDR pointing to the peering ID (`pcx-xxxx`), and vice versa in VPC B.
4. Update Security Groups in both VPCs to allow traffic on required ports.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would you connect multiple VPCs together, rather than just two?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To connect multiple VPCs (e.g., 3 or more), VPC Peering becomes unmanageable due to full-mesh complexity and lack of transitive routing. Instead, we use an **AWS Transit Gateway (TGW)**. Transit Gateway acts as a central cloud router (hub-and-spoke model). Each VPC attaches to the Transit Gateway, and route tables inside the Transit Gateway manage routing between all VPCs and on-premises networks cleanly.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Who typically manages the Transit Gateway in your organization, and have you personally created one?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In enterprise organizations, Transit Gateway is typically centrally owned and managed by the Cloud Network / Infrastructure team in a dedicated Network/Core Services AWS account. In our team, we create the VPC attachments and configure our application VPC route tables via Terraform. In lab and project environments, I have personally provisioned and configured complete Transit Gateways from scratch.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you confirm and elaborate on your hands-on experience creating a Transit Gateway?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes. In Terraform, I configured the `aws_ec2_transit_gateway` resource with Amazon Side ASN, attached multiple application VPCs using `aws_ec2_transit_gateway_vpc_attachment`, and configured the Transit Gateway Route Tables with route propagations. Finally, in each VPC's private route tables, I pointed inter-VPC traffic to the transit gateway ID.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Which database service does your project use?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Our project uses **Amazon RDS** (PostgreSQL / MySQL) for relational transactional data and **Amazon DynamoDB** for high-throughput, low-latency NoSQL data storage.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Is RDS used specifically for the analytics reporting part of the project?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
No, RDS is primarily used as the primary OLTP database for live transactional data. For analytics and heavy reporting, we use **RDS Read Replicas** or export data pipelines to **Amazon S3 / Athena** to prevent reporting queries from consuming CPU and I/O resources needed by production transactions.
</details>

For centralized log searching, indexing, and visualization, we stream logs from CloudWatch into an **OpenSearch / Elasticsearch** cluster visualized through **Grafana** and **OpenSearch Dashboards / Kibana**.

- **Logstash / OpenSearch Ingestion:** Parses, filters, and formats log timestamps and JSON fields.
- **Elasticsearch / OpenSearch:** Indexes logs for fast full-text searching.

#### 【 SYSTEM DESIGN 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you've hosted a static website and made a code change, pushed it, and the CI/CD pipeline ran successfully and deployment completed without errors. However, when you browse the website, you still see the old content. What could be causing this issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The most common causes are:
1. **Amazon CloudFront CDN Caching:** CloudFront edge locations have cached the older static HTML/JS files based on TTL. Solution: Create a CloudFront Cache Invalidation (`aws cloudfront create-invalidation --distribution-id <id> --paths "/*"`).
2. **Browser Cache:** The user's web browser cached the files locally. Solution: Perform a hard refresh (`Ctrl + Shift + R`) or check in Incognito mode.
3. **Pipeline Upload Target:** The pipeline may have uploaded assets to a subfolder or failed to overwrite `index.html` on S3.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Besides the causes you mentioned, are there any other possible reasons the website might still show old content even though the pipeline and deployment succeeded?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Additional possible reasons:
1. **S3 Cache-Control Headers:** Files uploaded to S3 were configured with long `Cache-Control: max-age` metadata, telling browsers and proxies to cache for days.
2. **DNS TTL:** If a domain or load balancer endpoint was changed, DNS resolvers may still serve the old IP until the DNS record TTL expires.
3. **Service Worker Caching:** If the website is a Progressive Web App (PWA), a registered client-side Service Worker might be serving older cached assets offline.
</details>

For major application versions or high-risk releases, we use **Blue-Green Deployment** to allow thorough staging testing before switching live traffic.

In our regular production workflows, we rely primarily on Rolling Updates for standard microservices and Blue-Green for major database-backed releases. While we have tested and understand Canary deployments (gradually splitting traffic via Ingress/Istio), we do not use canary for every deployment to avoid the extra operational overhead and metric tracking complexity.

#### 【 OTHER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is shift-left testing?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Shift-left testing is the DevOps practice of moving quality, security, and compliance testing earlier in the software development lifecycle—literally "shifting them to the left" on the project timeline.
Instead of discovering bugs late in QA or production, unit tests, linting, static code analysis (SonarQube), and dependency vulnerability scans (SAST/Trivy) run automatically on developer commits and PR creation. This catches defects when they are easiest, fastest, and cheapest to fix.
</details>

</details>

</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain the stages of a Jenkins CI/CD pipeline that you have implemented for your applications?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In my current project, I have implemented a declarative Jenkins pipeline consisting of the following key stages:
- **Checkout**: Automatically triggered by a GitHub webhook to pull the latest source code from the repository.
- **Build & Unit Test**: Compiles the application code (e.g., `mvn clean package`) and executes unit tests.
- **Code Quality (SonarQube)**: Runs static code analysis and quality gate checks for code smells, bugs, and test coverage.
- **Docker Build & Push**: Builds a Docker image tagged with the build number/commit hash and pushes it to AWS ECR.
- **Deploy**: Updates the Kubernetes deployment manifest in our EKS cluster using `kubectl` or Helm.
- **Notifications**: Sends a Slack or email alert with the build and deployment status.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you set up a master-slave (controller-agent) architecture in Jenkins?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To set up a master-agent architecture:
1. **Prepare Agent**: On the agent machine, install Java (matching the Jenkins controller version), create a `jenkins` user, and configure the working directory (e.g., `/home/jenkins`).
2. **SSH Authentication**: Generate an SSH key pair. Add the public key to `~/.ssh/authorized_keys` on the agent, and store the private key in Jenkins Credentials.
3. **Configure Node in Jenkins**: Go to **Manage Jenkins** -> **Nodes** -> **New Node**. Provide a node name, set the remote root directory, specify the label (e.g., `linux-agent`), and select "Launch agents via SSH".
4. **Connect**: Enter the agent's IP and credentials, then click Save and Launch Node. Jenkins establishes the SSH connection and runs build tasks on that agent.
</details>

#### 【 DOCKER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you worked with Docker Compose?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I actively work with Docker Compose, primarily for local development, integration testing, and running multi-container stacks. It allows defining multi-container applications in a single YAML file (`docker-compose.yml`)—including application services, databases (e.g., PostgreSQL), caching layers (e.g., Redis), networks, and persistent volumes. With simple commands like `docker compose up -d` and `docker compose down`, we can spin up or tear down the entire application stack consistently across different developer machines.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Have you also worked with Docker networking?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I work with Docker networking regularly. Docker provides several built-in network drivers:
- **Bridge**: The default driver for standalone containers on a single host. Containers on a custom bridge network can communicate using container names via automatic DNS resolution.
- **Host**: Disables network isolation, allowing the container to share the host machine's network stack directly for maximum performance.
- **Overlay**: Enables communication across multiple Docker daemon hosts in Docker Swarm.
- **None**: Completely disables networking for isolated workloads.
In day-to-day work, I use custom user-defined bridge networks so services in Docker Compose can communicate securely using service names.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you optimize Dockerfiles using multi-stage builds?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Multi-stage builds allow using multiple `FROM` statements in a single Dockerfile to separate the build environment from the final runtime image:

```dockerfile
# Stage 1: Build environment
FROM maven:3.8-openjdk-11 AS builder
WORKDIR /app
COPY pom.xml .
RUN mvn dependency:go-offline
COPY src ./src
RUN mvn clean package -DskipTests

# Stage 2: Runtime environment
FROM openjdk:11-jre-slim
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=builder /app/target/*.jar app.jar
EXPOSE 8080
CMD ["java", "-jar", "app.jar"]
```

This leaves behind all build tools, source code, and intermediate Maven artifacts, keeping only the JRE and compiled JAR. It dramatically reduces the final image size from ~800MB to under 150MB, speeding up deployments and shrinking the security attack surface.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between Docker Swarm and Kubernetes as container orchestration platforms?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Docker Swarm**: It is native to Docker, very simple to configure, lightweight, and has a minimal learning curve. However, it lacks advanced features like auto-scaling, complex deployment strategies, and broad third-party tool integrations. It is suitable for small projects and simple architectures.
- **Kubernetes**: An enterprise-grade, highly scalable orchestration platform with rich features—such as Horizontal Pod Autoscaling (HPA), automated rollouts/rollbacks, fine-grained RBAC, self-healing, Ingress controllers, and a massive community ecosystem (Helm, Istio, Prometheus). While it requires more operational setup, it is the industry standard for production microservices.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a downtime occurs in an application running on Kubernetes (e.g., EKS), how would you troubleshoot and distinguish between application-level and system/network-level issues to find the root cause?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I follow a systematic troubleshooting approach:
1. **Check Pod Status**: Run `kubectl get pods -n <namespace>`. If pods are in `CrashLoopBackOff` or `Error`, it is typically an application-level issue. I run `kubectl logs <pod-name> --previous` to check application stack traces, unhandled exceptions, or database connection failures.
2. **Check Events**: Run `kubectl describe pod <pod-name>` to look at liveness/readiness probe failures or `OOMKilled` (Exit Code 137).
3. **System/Node Level**: If pods are in `Pending` state, inspect nodes with `kubectl describe nodes` and `kubectl top nodes` to check for resource exhaustion (CPU/Memory), disk pressure, or taints.
4. **Network Level**: Run `kubectl get svc,ep` to verify endpoints have active pod IPs, test DNS resolution via `nslookup` inside a debug container, and verify Ingress/ALB target group health checks.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What deployment update strategy do you typically follow in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In production, we typically use the **RollingUpdate** strategy, which is the default in Kubernetes. It replaces old pods with new ones gradually without causing application downtime. We fine-tune the rollout behavior using two parameters:
- `maxUnavailable`: Specifies the maximum number of pods that can be unavailable during the update process (e.g., `0` or `25%`), ensuring we never drop below capacity.
- `maxSurge`: Specifies the maximum number of extra pods that can be created above the desired replica count (e.g., `1` or `25%`).
Combined with proper readiness probes, this guarantees zero-downtime deployments.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Are you familiar with other Kubernetes deployment update strategies besides rolling updates?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes:
- **Recreate**: Kills all existing pods simultaneously before spinning up new ones. This causes temporary downtime, but is useful when the backend database schema changes and two versions of the app cannot run concurrently.
- **Blue/Green Deployment**: Runs two identical environments (Blue for live, Green for new). Once the Green deployment is fully tested and verified, we switch the Service selector to point to Green instantly.
- **Canary Deployment**: Routes a small percentage (e.g., 5-10%) of user traffic to the new version using an Ingress controller or service mesh (like Istio), monitoring error rates and latency before rolling it out to 100%.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose an EC2 instance owner has lost their PEM key file, and you need to give a developer access to log into that machine. How would you achieve this?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The cleanest and recommended way is using **AWS Systems Manager (SSM) Session Manager**:
1. Ensure the EC2 instance has the `AmazonSSMManagedInstanceCore` IAM policy attached to its IAM role and the SSM Agent is running.
2. Grant the developer IAM permissions to start an SSM session (`ssm:StartSession`).
3. The developer can immediately connect via the AWS Management Console or AWS CLI (`aws ssm start-session --target <instance-id>`) without needing any SSH keys or open inbound port 22.
Alternatively, if direct SSH is mandatory, you can stop the instance, detach its root EBS volume, attach it as a secondary disk to a temporary EC2 instance, inject the developer's public SSH key into `/home/ec2-user/.ssh/authorized_keys`, and reattach the volume back to the original instance.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Given your proposed solution, are you concluding that the developer would be able to SSH into the EC2 machine directly from their own machine?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, absolutely.
- If using **AWS SSM Session Manager**: The developer can configure SSH over SSM proxy in their local `~/.ssh/config` file (`ProxyCommand sh -c "aws ssm start-session --target %h..."`). This allows standard `ssh ec2-user@<instance-id>` directly from their local terminal without exposing port 22.
- If using the **EBS volume injection** approach: Once the developer's personal public key (`id_rsa.pub`) is placed into `/home/ec2-user/.ssh/authorized_keys`, the developer can SSH directly from their terminal using their own private key: `ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_rsa ec2-user@<instance-public-ip>`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If a developer needs to SSH directly from their terminal into an EC2 instance's IP address without possessing the PEM key file, what steps would allow them to log in?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Here are the exact steps:
1. The developer generates their own SSH key pair locally using `ssh-keygen -t rsa -b 4096` and shares only their public key (`id_rsa.pub`).
2. An administrator logs into the EC2 instance (via SSM Session Manager or EC2 Instance Connect) and appends the developer's public key to `/home/ec2-user/.ssh/authorized_keys`.
3. Set the correct Linux permissions: `chmod 700 ~/.ssh` and `chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys`.
4. Ensure the EC2 Security Group permits inbound traffic on port 22 from the developer's IP.
5. The developer can now SSH directly from their terminal: `ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_rsa ec2-user@<EC2-IP>`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Without ever sharing the actual PEM key with the developer, how can you enable them to SSH into the EC2 instance?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We add the developer's personal public SSH key directly to the EC2 instance's authorized keys list. The administrator connects to the instance (using AWS Systems Manager Session Manager or temporary EC2 Instance Connect) and appends the developer's public key (`id_rsa.pub`) to `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`. This allows the developer to authenticate using their own private key (`id_rsa`), completely eliminating any need to share the original PEM key.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If you need to write a security policy for a particular EC2 instance to allow only specific ports and control public IP exposure, how would you configure this using security groups?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Security Group Configuration**: Create a dedicated Security Group for the instance.
  - Inbound Rules: Explicitly allow only required ports—for example, HTTP (port 80) and HTTPS (port 443) from the Application Load Balancer security group, and SSH (port 22) restricted strictly to our corporate VPN or office CIDR.
  - Outbound Rules: Allow required outbound traffic (e.g., HTTPS on 443 to download packages or all outbound traffic).
- **Control Public Exposure**: Best practice is to place the EC2 instance in a **private subnet** with no public IP assigned, putting an Application Load Balancer in the public subnet to handle external traffic.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● In an EC2 security group, if inbound rule 1 allows traffic on port 8080 and a second inbound rule (rule 2) on the same port 8080 denies traffic, which rule takes effect?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In AWS, Security Groups **do not have deny rules**. They are stateful and operate strictly on an **allow-only** basis. All incoming traffic is blocked by default, and you can only add allow rules. Therefore, rule 1 allowing port 8080 takes effect, and traffic is permitted.
If you need explicit deny rules or rule ordering, you must configure **Network Access Control Lists (NACLs)** at the subnet level, which evaluate numbered allow and deny rules in sequential order.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If you have created an EBS volume attached to an EC2 instance, is it possible to mount that same EBS volume to another EC2 instance, and how would you do it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Standard EBS Volumes (gp2/gp3)**: They can only be attached to one EC2 instance at a time. To attach it to another instance, you must unmount it inside the OS (`umount /mnt/data`), detach it via the AWS Console or CLI (`aws ec2 detach-volume`), and then attach it to the second instance (`aws ec2 attach-volume`) within the same Availability Zone.
- **EBS Multi-Attach (io1/io2 volumes)**: AWS allows attaching the same Provisioned IOPS volume to multiple Nitro-based EC2 instances concurrently in the same AZ, but requires a cluster-aware file system (like GFS2) to prevent data corruption.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Is it possible to transfer an EBS volume to a different AWS account?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, using EBS Snapshots:
1. Create a snapshot of the EBS volume in the source AWS account.
2. In the snapshot permissions, share the snapshot with the target AWS account ID (if encrypted with a custom KMS key, share the KMS key as well).
3. In the target AWS account, locate the shared snapshot and create a copy of it in that account.
4. Create a new EBS volume from that copied snapshot and attach it to the target EC2 instance.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you heard about the latest updates/upgrades to AWS S3?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, recent key upgrades to AWS S3 include:
- **S3 Express One Zone**: A high-performance, single-AZ storage class designed to deliver single-digit millisecond latency and 10x faster data access speeds for latency-sensitive workloads like ML training and analytics.
- **Default Security Enhancements**: S3 now enables "Block Public Access" and default server-side encryption (SSE-S3) automatically for all newly created S3 buckets.
- **Amazon S3 Tables**: Fully managed Apache Iceberg tables built natively into S3 for high-performance tabular data queries.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● In an AWS security group or route table, what does specifying 0.0.0.0/0 for a subnet mean?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
`0.0.0.0/0` represents the default route covering all IPv4 addresses (the entire internet).
- **In a Route Table**: Adding a route with destination `0.0.0.0/0` pointing to an Internet Gateway (`igw-xxxx`) makes the subnet a public subnet, directing all outbound traffic destined outside the VPC to the internet.
- **In a Security Group**: Specifying `0.0.0.0/0` in an inbound rule allows traffic on that port from any IP address worldwide.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you write a Terraform script to provision an AWS VPC with 2 public subnets and 2 private subnets across 2 availability zones, including an internet gateway for the public subnets and a NAT gateway for the private subnets? Also, output the VPC ID, public subnet ID, and private subnet ID.</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
```hcl
provider "aws" {
  region = "us-east-1"
}

resource "aws_vpc" "main" {
  cidr_block           = "10.0.0.0/16"
  enable_dns_hostnames = true
  tags = { Name = "main-vpc" }
}

resource "aws_subnet" "public" {
  count                   = 2
  vpc_id                  = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block              = "10.0.${count.index + 1}.0/24"
  availability_zone       = count.index == 0 ? "us-east-1a" : "us-east-1b"
  map_public_ip_on_launch = true
  tags = { Name = "public-subnet-${count.index + 1}" }
}

resource "aws_subnet" "private" {
  count             = 2
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block        = "10.0.${count.index + 10}.0/24"
  availability_zone = count.index == 0 ? "us-east-1a" : "us-east-1b"
  tags = { Name = "private-subnet-${count.index + 1}" }
}

resource "aws_internet_gateway" "igw" {
  vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id
}

resource "aws_eip" "nat" {
  domain = "vpc"
}

resource "aws_nat_gateway" "nat" {
  allocation_id = aws_eip.nat.id
  subnet_id     = aws_subnet.public[0].id
}

resource "aws_route_table" "public" {
  vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id
  route {
    cidr_block = "0.0.0.0/0"
    gateway_id = aws_internet_gateway.igw.id
  }
}

resource "aws_route_table_association" "public" {
  count          = 2
  subnet_id      = aws_subnet.public[count.index].id
  route_table_id = aws_route_table.public.id
}

resource "aws_route_table" "private" {
  vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id
  route {
    cidr_block     = "0.0.0.0/0"
    nat_gateway_id = aws_nat_gateway.nat.id
  }
}

resource "aws_route_table_association" "private" {
  count          = 2
  subnet_id      = aws_subnet.private[count.index].id
  route_table_id = aws_route_table.private.id
}

output "vpc_id" { value = aws_vpc.main.id }
output "public_subnet_ids" { value = aws_subnet.public[*].id }
output "private_subnet_ids" { value = aws_subnet.private[*].id }
```
This script provisions the VPC across 2 AZs with IGW for public subnets, a NAT Gateway for private subnets, and exports the requested IDs.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you inherit legacy infrastructure managed by inconsistent Ansible playbooks and roles. What steps would you take to standardize, refactor, and document the automation while ensuring minimal disruption to active deployments?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Audit & Inventory**: Inventory all existing playbooks, hosts, and variables. Identify duplicate tasks and hardcoded configuration values.
2. **Adopt Standard Role Structure**: Reorganize tasks into standard Ansible roles (`tasks/`, `handlers/`, `vars/`, `defaults/`, `templates/`) using `ansible-galaxy init`.
3. **Decouple Configurations & Secrets**: Move hardcoded values into `group_vars/` and `host_vars/`, and encrypt sensitive credentials using **Ansible Vault**.
4. **Dry-Run Validation**: Test refactored playbooks against staging hosts using `ansible-playbook --check --diff` to verify changes without applying destructive updates.
5. **Documentation & Version Control**: Document variables in role `README.md` files and enforce linting (`ansible-lint`) in CI.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you need to update an application across hundreds of servers with zero downtime using Ansible. Can you discuss your approach, including the playbook structure, handling of rolling updates, and failure recovery, to minimize service impact?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Rolling Execution**: Use the `serial` keyword (e.g., `serial: "10%"` or `serial: 5`) to deploy changes in small batches rather than targeting all servers at once.
- **Playbook Structure**:
  1. **Pre-tasks**: Drain/deregister the server from the AWS ALB Target Group.
  2. **Deployment Tasks**: Update application files, run configuration changes, and restart the systemd service.
  3. **Health Check**: Use the `uri` module or `wait_for` module to verify the app returns HTTP 200 on `/health`.
  4. **Post-tasks**: Re-register the server back to the ALB target group.
- **Failure Recovery**: Set `max_fail_percentage: 0` so the entire playbook aborts immediately if a single server in a batch fails, preventing broken code from affecting the rest of the fleet.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose a critical production environment requires strict compliance and auditability. How would you use Terraform to implement automated policy enforcement and an audit trail for all infrastructure changes? Can you describe your strategy and the key tools/services involved?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Policy Enforcement**: Integrate static analysis and Policy-as-Code tools like **tfsec**, **Checkov**, or **OPA / Conftest** into the CI/CD pipeline. Any pull request that attempts to create unencrypted S3 buckets or open security groups to `0.0.0.0/0` fails automatically at the build stage.
- **Pull Request Governance**: Enforce Git-based workflows where infrastructure changes require peer reviews and approvals before running `terraform plan` and `terraform apply`.
- **Audit Trail**: Enable **AWS CloudTrail** to log all AWS API calls made by Terraform's execution role. Store the Terraform state file in a version-controlled, encrypted S3 bucket with access logging and MFA delete enabled.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you are tasked with integrating Terraform into a CI/CD pipeline that must deploy infrastructure across multiple cloud providers. What challenges might you face, and how would you design the workflow to ensure reliability, modularity, and secure handling of secrets?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Challenges**: Disparate cloud provider APIs, differing authentication mechanisms, resource schema differences, and risk of widespread blast radius.
- **Workflow Design**:
  - **Modular Decoupling**: Separate configurations and state files by cloud provider (e.g., `environments/aws` and `environments/azure`), preventing cross-cloud dependencies.
  - **Secret Management**: Authenticate using cloud-native OIDC federation (OpenID Connect) between the CI runner and cloud providers (e.g., GitHub Actions / Jenkins to AWS/Azure IAM) to avoid long-lived static credentials.
  - **Pipeline Controls**: Execute `terraform fmt -check`, `tflint`, and `terraform plan` on pull requests, requiring senior approval before running `terraform apply`.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose your organization needs to migrate an application to the cloud, but some third-party integrations rely on static IPs and legacy VPNs. How would you plan the migration to ensure connectivity and minimize disruption for those dependencies?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Static IPs**: Allocate AWS **Elastic IPs (EIPs)** and associate them with our NAT Gateways (for outbound traffic) or Network Load Balancers (for inbound traffic). Provide these dedicated EIPs to the third parties well in advance to whitelist on their firewalls.
- **Legacy VPN Connectivity**: Set up an **AWS Site-to-Site VPN** or AWS Direct Connect between our AWS VPC (via a Virtual Private Gateway or Transit Gateway) and the legacy datacenter / partner network.
- **Staging Validation**: Perform connectivity and handshake testing over the VPN tunnel in our staging environment before production cutover to ensure zero downtime.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If you are migrating the data using a tool like AWS Snowball, how would you handle integrating the static IPs and legacy VPNs used by third-party dependencies during that migration?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- AWS Snowball is an offline, physical appliance used for bulk data transfer (terabytes to petabytes) from on-premises storage to AWS S3. It does not carry live network traffic.
- During the physical shipping and data transfer phase, live application traffic continues running on-premises over the existing legacy VPN.
- Once Snowball data is imported into S3 and restored to the cloud database, we establish the AWS Site-to-Site VPN and run an incremental delta sync (using AWS DMS or rsync).
- During the cutover window, we switch the application endpoints to AWS, routing third-party traffic through the pre-whitelisted Elastic IPs attached to our NAT Gateways.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If there is a failure in one region of your deployment, how would you recover from it, and what strategy would you use?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Multi-Region Disaster Recovery Strategy**: Implement a **Warm Standby** or **Pilot Light** architecture in a secondary AWS region.
- **DNS Failover**: Use **Route 53 Health Checks** with Failover Routing to automatically redirect user traffic to the secondary region if the primary region health checks fail.
- **Data Replication**: Enable cross-region replication for S3 buckets and cross-region read replicas for Amazon RDS / Aurora Global Database to maintain near-zero Recovery Point Objective (RPO).
- **IaC Automation**: Use Terraform to quickly spin up or scale out compute resources in the secondary region.
</details>

#### 【 SYSTEM DESIGN 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you are tasked with migrating a legacy database that has a high transaction volume to a cloud-based solution. What strategy would you use to ensure data integrity and minimize business disruption during the migration?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I would use **AWS Database Migration Service (DMS)** with **Change Data Capture (CDC)**:
1. **Schema & Assessment**: Use the AWS Schema Conversion Tool (SCT) to migrate schema to Amazon RDS/Aurora.
2. **Full Baseline Load**: Perform an initial full load migration of existing database tables while the source database continues serving live traffic.
3. **Continuous Replication (CDC)**: AWS DMS CDC captures and replicates live ongoing transactions from the database transaction logs to the cloud database in real time.
4. **Validation & Cutover**: Run data validation to confirm zero data discrepancies. During a minimal cutover window, stop writes to the source, let replication lag hit zero, and redirect application connection strings to RDS.
</details>

</details>
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you write a simple shell command/script to find the top running services (processes consuming the most memory or CPU) on a Linux machine?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Top 5 processes by Memory:**
```bash
ps aux --sort=-%mem | head -n 6
```
- **Top 5 processes by CPU:**
```bash
ps aux --sort=-%cpu | head -n 6
```
Alternatively, in real time, you can run `top` and press `Shift + M` to sort by memory or `Shift + P` to sort by CPU.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you explain what the 'ps -ef' command does in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The `ps -ef` command displays a full snapshot of all currently running processes across the entire Linux operating system. It provides a standard full-format listing including:
- **UID**: User who owns the process.
- **PID**: Process ID.
- **PPID**: Parent Process ID.
- **C**: CPU utilization.
- **STIME**: Start time of the process.
- **TTY**: Controlling terminal.
- **TIME**: Cumulative CPU execution time.
- **CMD**: The full command and arguments that launched the process.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In the 'ps -ef' command, what do the '-e' and '-f' flags specifically mean?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- `-e`: Selects **every process** running on the system (identical to `-A`). It ensures you see processes from all users and system daemons, not just those belonging to the current terminal session.
- `-f`: Formats the output in **full-format listing**, displaying additional columns such as UID, PPID, start time (STIME), and the full command path and arguments (CMD).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What Linux command would you use to check whether a specific file already exists in a given folder?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
You can check using a simple test expression:
```bash
[ -f /path/to/folder/app.log ] && echo "File exists" || echo "File does not exist"
```
In a shell script:
```bash
if [ -f "/path/to/folder/app.log" ]; then
    echo "File exists"
fi
```
Alternatively, for a quick interactive check, you can run `ls /path/to/folder/app.log` or `test -e /path/to/folder/app.log`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would you search for a file within subfolders of a directory (for example, searching recursively inside /tmp)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
You use the `find` command:
```bash
find /tmp -type f -name "app.log"
```
To search case-insensitively or match wildcard patterns:
```bash
find /tmp -type f -iname "*.log"
```
This searches recursively through `/tmp` and all its subdirectories for files matching the name.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you briefly explain the different stages of a typical DevOps/CI-CD pipeline, the tools used at each stage, and your hands-on experience with those individual steps?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In my current projects, our standard CI/CD pipeline includes:
1. **Source Code Management**: Developers push code to GitHub.
2. **Build**: Jenkins pulls code and builds packages using Maven (`mvn clean package`).
3. **Code Quality**: SonarQube analyzes code quality, security bugs, and test coverage.
4. **Vulnerability Scanning**: Trivy scans dependencies and container images for CVEs.
5. **Containerization**: Docker builds the container image and pushes it to AWS ECR.
6. **Deployment**: Helm deploys the new image tag to AWS EKS.
7. **Monitoring**: Prometheus and Grafana track application health post-deployment.
I have hands-on experience building and maintaining these stages in declarative Jenkinsfiles.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What does 'Jenkins standardization' mean, and what practices/considerations do you follow when setting up a standardized Jenkins pipeline?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Jenkins standardization means creating uniform, reusable, and secure pipeline templates across the organization rather than building ad-hoc custom pipelines:
- **Jenkins Shared Libraries**: Encapsulate reusable Groovy steps (build, test, docker push, deploy, slack notifications) in a centralized Git repo.
- **Pipeline-as-Code**: Mandate version-controlled `Jenkinsfile` in every project repository.
- **Ephemeral Kubernetes Agents**: Run build jobs in dynamic container pods rather than static VMs.
- **Centralized Secrets**: Manage credentials using Jenkins Credential Store or AWS Secrets Manager.
- **Uniform Quality Gates**: Enforce mandatory SonarQube and Trivy scan pass requirements across all pipelines.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is a Kubernetes Service, and can you explain its types and use cases?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A Kubernetes Service is an abstraction that provides a single, stable IP address and DNS name to route traffic to a set of ephemeral Pods identified by label selectors.
The four main types are:
1. **ClusterIP** (Default): Exposes the service on an internal cluster IP, used for communication between internal microservices.
2. **NodePort**: Exposes the service on each node's IP at a static port (30000-32767), used for direct access during testing.
3. **LoadBalancer**: Provisions an external cloud load balancer (e.g., AWS NLB/ALB), used for public-facing production services.
4. **ExternalName**: Maps the service to an external DNS name (e.g., `db.external.com`) using CNAME records.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you describe your experience with Infrastructure as Code (IaC), such as the tools you've used and how you've implemented them?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I have 5 years of practical experience with Infrastructure as Code, using **Terraform** for infrastructure provisioning and **Ansible** for configuration management:
- **Terraform**: I write modular code to provision AWS resources (VPC, Subnets, EKS, ALB, S3, RDS). I manage state securely using remote S3 backends with DynamoDB locking, and separate environment variables per environment.
- **Ansible**: I write playbooks to automate OS configuration, patch packages, manage users, and deploy applications across EC2 fleets.
- **CI/CD Integration**: All IaC code is maintained in Git, reviewed via pull requests, and applied automatically through Jenkins pipelines.
</details>

</details>
</details>

#### 【 DOCKER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain how you understand Docker images and containers, and the difference between them?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Docker Image**: A read-only, immutable template built from a Dockerfile. It bundles the application code, runtime libraries, dependencies, and environment configurations across multiple read-only layers.
- **Docker Container**: A live, runnable instance of a Docker image. When you start an image (`docker run`), Docker adds a thin read-write layer on top of the image layers, running the process in an isolated environment with its own filesystem, network, and process space.
Analogy: A Docker image is like a class blueprint, and a Docker container is an active object instance running in memory.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How can you reduce the size of a Docker image?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Multi-stage Builds**: Use a build stage with SDKs to compile code, and copy only the compiled binary into a lightweight runtime stage.
2. **Minimal Base Images**: Use slim or alpine variants (e.g., `python:3.9-alpine` or `openjdk:11-jre-slim`) instead of full Ubuntu/Debian OS images.
3. **Combine RUN Instructions**: Chain commands with `&& \` and clean package caches in the same layer (`apt-get clean && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*`).
4. **Use `.dockerignore`**: Exclude `.git`, local logs, test artifacts, and `node_modules` from entering the build context.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you explain what Pods and ReplicaSets are in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Pod**: The smallest deployable computing unit in Kubernetes. It wraps one or more tightly coupled containers (sharing the same network IP, port space, and storage volumes) running on a node.
- **ReplicaSet**: A Kubernetes controller whose job is to maintain a stable set of identical Pod replicas running at all times. If a pod crashes or is deleted, the ReplicaSet automatically provisions a replacement to match the desired count. In practice, we rarely create ReplicaSets directly; we manage them via Deployments.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a node in a Kubernetes cluster goes down, what happens to the ReplicaSets and Pods that were running on it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. The node stops reporting heartbeats to the Kubernetes API server.
2. After the `node-monitor-grace-period` (default 40s), the control plane marks the node as `NotReady`.
3. If the node stays down past the pod eviction timeout (default 5 minutes), the control plane evicts the pods from that node.
4. The ReplicaSet controller notices that the actual number of healthy running pods is below the desired replica count.
5. The Kube-Scheduler automatically assigns replacement pods to other healthy worker nodes in the cluster.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If you have one application that needs to run across a few different environments, how would you prefer to organize this in Kubernetes using namespaces, multiple clusters, or other approaches?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The industry best practice I follow:
- **Separate Clusters for Production vs Non-Production**: Run Production in a dedicated EKS cluster to provide hard security isolation, independent upgrades, and prevent blast-radius risks.
- **Namespaces for Non-Production**: Within the non-prod cluster, use **Namespaces** (`dev`, `qa`, `staging`) combined with ResourceQuotas, LimitRanges, and NetworkPolicies to share infrastructure costs.
- **Helm Configuration**: Maintain a single Helm chart with environment-specific values files (`values-dev.yaml`, `values-prod.yaml`) to deploy consistently across all environments.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you describe the typical Terraform workflow and the commands you use at each stage?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The standard Terraform workflow consists of 4 main phases:
1. `terraform init`: Initializes the directory, downloads required provider plugins (e.g., AWS), and configures the backend state.
2. `terraform validate` & `terraform fmt`: Checks configuration syntax and formats code to standard conventions.
3. `terraform plan`: Refreshes live state, compares it with your code, and displays an execution plan showing resources to be created, modified, or deleted.
4. `terraform apply`: Applies the changes to provision or update real infrastructure against the cloud provider.
When tearing down infrastructure, `terraform destroy` is used.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How does Terraform determine what changes (additions or removals) need to be made to your infrastructure when you run a plan?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Terraform performs a 3-way reconciliation:
1. **Desired State**: The configuration files written in your `.tf` files.
2. **Current State**: The recorded state saved in the `terraform.tfstate` file.
3. **Live Infrastructure**: During the refresh phase of `plan`, Terraform queries the cloud provider's APIs to inspect live resources.
Terraform compares the live state against the desired code, calculates the differences, and generates the execution plan showing what must be added, updated in-place, or destroyed.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Where do you prefer to store the Terraform state file, and why?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In AWS environments, I always store the state file in a **remote backend using Amazon S3 with DynamoDB state locking**:
- **Amazon S3**: Centralizes state for team collaboration and CI/CD pipelines. We enable S3 Bucket Versioning for recovery and history, and server-side encryption (KMS) for security.
- **DynamoDB**: Implements distributed state locking to prevent concurrent runs from corrupting the state file if two engineers or CI pipelines run `terraform apply` at the same time.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If you needed to organize a Terraform repository to support three different environments (dev, staging, and production), how would you structure it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I structure the repository using reusable modules and separate environment folders:

```
terraform-repo/
├── modules/
│   ├── vpc/
│   ├── eks/
│   └── rds/
└── environments/
    ├── dev/
    │   ├── backend.tf
    │   ├── main.tf (calls modules)
    │   └── terraform.tfvars
    ├── staging/
    │   ├── backend.tf
    │   ├── main.tf
    │   └── terraform.tfvars
    └── prod/
        ├── backend.tf
        ├── main.tf
        └── terraform.tfvars
```

This isolates the remote state file per environment, eliminating the risk of modifying production when applying dev changes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Would you personally prefer using a single Terraform repository or multiple repositories for managing different environments, and why?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I prefer a **single repository (Monorepo)** structured with dedicated environment directories.
- **Why**: It allows us to maintain shared, reusable modules and environment definitions in one place. It makes testing and promoting module improvements from dev to staging to prod easy via Git pull requests.
- Because each environment directory maintains its own isolated `backend.tf` and state file, we retain full blast-radius isolation while keeping code DRY.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you handle and store secrets when working with Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Never Hardcode Secrets**: Never commit passwords, keys, or `.tfvars` files with sensitive data to Git.
- **AWS Secrets Manager / SSM**: Store secrets in AWS Secrets Manager or Parameter Store, and fetch them dynamically in Terraform using data sources.
- **CI/CD Environment Variables**: In automated pipelines, pass secrets as environment variables prefixed with `TF_VAR_` (e.g., `TF_VAR_db_password`).
- **State File Security**: Encrypt the S3 backend with AWS KMS and restrict bucket access using strict IAM policies.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Do you have experience working with Ansible or Puppet?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have hands-on experience working with **Ansible**. I have used Ansible to automate server configuration management across EC2 instances, write playbooks to install software (e.g., Nginx, Java, Docker), manage Linux users and SSH keys, push application configurations, and run rolling service updates.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Do you know the difference between Ansible and Puppet as configuration management tools?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Architecture**: Ansible is **agentless**; it connects over standard SSH (Linux) or WinRM (Windows) using Python. Puppet is **agent-based**, requiring a Puppet agent daemon running on every node communicating with a central Puppet Master.
- **Language**: Ansible uses simple, human-readable **YAML** for playbooks. Puppet uses its own declarative Ruby-like Domain Specific Language (DSL).
- **Simplicity**: Ansible has a much lower learning curve and requires no master server maintenance.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If a project uses both Terraform and Ansible, what responsibilities would each tool handle?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Terraform handles Infrastructure Provisioning**: It builds the underlying cloud infrastructure components—VPC, subnets, Internet Gateways, EC2 instances, S3 buckets, RDS databases, and Security Groups.
- **Ansible handles Configuration Management**: Once instances are up, Ansible connects to configure the OS, install required packages (e.g., Java, Nginx), deploy application artifacts, manage configuration files, and ensure services are running.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain what AWS IAM (Identity and Access Management) is and describe its main components?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AWS IAM is the identity service used to securely control authentication and authorization to AWS resources. Its main components are:
1. **Users**: Identities representing people or applications requiring access to AWS.
2. **Groups**: Collections of users to easily apply shared permissions.
3. **Roles**: Identities with assigned permission policies that can be temporarily assumed by users, EC2 instances, or AWS services without using static access keys.
4. **Policies**: JSON documents defining explicit `Allow` or `Deny` permissions specifying Actions, Resources, and Conditions.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If you have an EC2 instance that needs access to an S3 bucket, which IAM component would you use to grant that access, and what permissions would you configure?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I would use an **IAM Role** attached to the EC2 instance via an **Instance Profile**:
1. Create an IAM Policy granting the necessary permissions (e.g., `s3:GetObject`, `s3:PutObject`, `s3:ListBucket`) scoped strictly to the target bucket ARN:

```json
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject", "s3:ListBucket"],
    "Resource": ["arn:aws:s3:::my-app-bucket", "arn:aws:s3:::my-app-bucket/*"]
  }]
}
```

2. Attach this policy to the IAM Role and attach the role to the EC2 instance. The application retrieves temporary credentials automatically via the instance metadata service.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you decide which EC2 instance type to use for a given workload?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I evaluate the resource requirements of the application workload:
- **General Purpose (T / M family)**: Balanced compute, memory, and networking. Ideal for web servers, microservices, and development environments.
- **Compute Optimized (C family)**: High ratio of vCPUs to memory. Best for compute-heavy workloads, batch processing, and media transcoding.
- **Memory Optimized (R family)**: High memory-to-CPU ratio. Ideal for in-memory caches (Redis, Memcached), relational databases, and data analytics.
- **Storage Optimized (I family)**: High sequential read/write for NoSQL databases and data warehousing.
I also monitor baseline metrics in CloudWatch to right-size instances over time.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you have an EC2 instance running a web server application, and it's receiving traffic from many clients. During load spikes, the instance becomes unable to handle requests and the service returns errors. What would you do to troubleshoot and improve this situation?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Troubleshooting**: Check CloudWatch metrics for CPU, memory, and network saturation. Log into the instance and run `top` / `htop`, `df -h`, and inspect web server error logs (`/var/log/nginx/error.log`) to check for thread starvation or connection drops.
- **Improvement**:
  1. Place an **Application Load Balancer (ALB)** in front to distribute incoming client traffic across multiple instances.
  2. Put the EC2 instances in an **Auto Scaling Group (ASG)** spanning multiple Availability Zones.
  3. Configure scaling policies to automatically add instances when CPU utilization or request count exceeds target thresholds.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If checking system metrics with the 'top' command shows high CPU utilization, what would you do to improve the situation?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Immediate Action**: If running a standalone instance, temporarily scale up vertically by stopping the instance and changing its type to a larger instance with more vCPUs (e.g., from `t3.medium` to `c5.xlarge`).
- **Architectural Solution**: Place the application behind an Application Load Balancer inside an Auto Scaling Group with a Target Tracking policy set to maintain average CPU utilization at 60-70%, automatically scaling out new instances horizontally during traffic spikes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If you can no longer scale up the instance's resources because it's already at maximum limits, what would you do next \- for example, would you consider scaling?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I would transition from **vertical scaling (scaling up)** to **horizontal scaling (scaling out)**. Instead of relying on a single large machine, deploy multiple instances running identical application code behind an Application Load Balancer and manage them using an Auto Scaling Group across multiple Availability Zones. This eliminates single points of failure and provides virtually limitless capacity.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What types of scaling do you know about in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Vertical Scaling (Scale Up / Down)**: Modifying the hardware capacity of an existing instance (e.g., resizing an EC2 instance from `t3.small` to `m5.large`). Requires a brief downtime or restart.
- **Horizontal Scaling (Scale Out / In)**: Adding or removing instances dynamically to handle changing traffic demands using an Auto Scaling Group, with zero downtime.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: When using an Auto Scaling Group, since you can't manually deploy instances the way you would from an AMI directly, what mechanism or template do you use to define instance configuration for the Auto Scaling Group?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We use an **AWS Launch Template** (recommended by AWS over legacy Launch Configurations). The Launch Template encapsulates all instance configurations: AMI ID, instance type, key pair, Security Groups, storage volume mappings, IAM instance profile, and user-data bootstrap script used to launch every new instance in the Auto Scaling Group.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If you've deployed multiple instances via an Auto Scaling Group but clients are still only reaching a single instance, what additional component do you need to set up?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
You need to set up an **Application Load Balancer (ALB)**. You attach the Auto Scaling Group to the ALB's Target Group. Clients connect to the ALB's DNS name, and the load balancer automatically distributes incoming requests evenly across all healthy instances in the group.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would you manage and distribute traffic across multiple EC2 instances?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
By provisioning an **Application Load Balancer (ALB)** in public subnets:
- Configure ALB listener rules (HTTP/HTTPS).
- Define a Target Group pointing to the EC2 instances.
- Configure health checks (e.g., `GET /health` expecting HTTP 200) so traffic is only routed to healthy instances.
- The ALB distributes client traffic evenly using round-robin or least-outstanding-requests algorithms.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What are the major types of load balancers available in AWS that you know of?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Application Load Balancer (ALB)**: Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS). Supports host-based and path-based routing, SSL termination, and websockets.
2. **Network Load Balancer (NLB)**: Layer 4 (TCP/UDP/TLS). Ultra-high performance, handles millions of requests per second with ultra-low latency, and supports static Elastic IPs.
3. **Gateway Load Balancer (GWLB)**: Layer 3/4. Used to deploy and scale third-party virtual appliances (firewalls, deep packet inspection).
4. **Classic Load Balancer (CLB)**: Legacy load balancer supporting basic Layer 4 and Layer 7 traffic.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Given that you have EC2 instances behind a load balancer and the application works fine, what else would you improve in this architecture to achieve high availability?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To achieve true High Availability:
1. **Multi-AZ Deployment**: Distribute instances across at least 2 or 3 distinct Availability Zones.
2. **Auto Scaling Group**: Configure min, max, and desired capacity to replace unhealthy instances automatically.
3. **Multi-AZ Database**: Use Amazon RDS Multi-AZ with automated synchronous failover.
4. **Static Content Offloading**: Store static assets in S3 and serve them via Amazon CloudFront (CDN) to reduce load on EC2.
5. **DNS Failover**: Use Amazon Route 53 health checks for failover routing.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between a public subnet and a private subnet in a VPC?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Public Subnet**: Its associated Route Table has a route for `0.0.0.0/0` targeting an **Internet Gateway (IGW)**. Instances with public IP addresses can directly send and receive traffic from the internet.
- **Private Subnet**: Its Route Table does **not** have a direct route to an Internet Gateway. To access the internet (e.g., for updates), it routes outbound traffic `0.0.0.0/0` through a **NAT Gateway** located in a public subnet, preventing external entities from initiating inbound connections.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If you have a group of EC2 instances for production and need to create separate development and staging environments, how would you design your VPC architecture to support these three different environments?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The recommended AWS best practice is **Multi-Account / Separate VPCs**:
- Create independent VPCs for Dev, Staging, and Prod (ideally under separate AWS accounts using AWS Organizations).
- Each VPC has its own CIDR block, public subnets (for ALBs/NAT Gateways), and private subnets across multiple AZs (for app and database tiers).
- This guarantees complete isolation: development activities or testing cannot accidentally consume production quotas, leak data, or impact production workloads.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Specifically regarding VPC design (not EKS), how would you connect the VPCs across your different environment accounts?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **AWS Transit Gateway (TGW)**: Recommended for enterprise multi-VPC hub-and-spoke connectivity. You attach all VPCs to a central Transit Gateway and control routing using TGW route tables.
- **VPC Peering**: For simple, direct 1-to-1 connections between VPCs (e.g., between a Shared Services VPC and Dev/Staging VPCs) with no bandwidth bottleneck.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Regarding security groups in AWS, can you block a specific IP address using a security group, or not?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**No**, you cannot block a specific IP address using a Security Group. Security Groups are stateful and support **allow rules only**—all traffic is implicitly denied unless explicitly permitted.
To explicitly block or deny a specific IP address, you must use a **Network Access Control List (NACL)** associated with the subnet, which supports ordered allow and deny rules evaluated sequentially.
</details>

</details>
</details>
