#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain the architecture of Kubernetes, including the control plane and data plane? What are the key components, and what does each component do?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Kubernetes architecture is split into the Control Plane and Data Plane:
- **Control Plane:**
  - `kube-apiserver`: Central API gateway for all cluster management and internal communications.
  - `etcd`: Distributed, consistent key-value store holding the entire cluster state and configuration.
  - `kube-scheduler`: Assigns newly created pods to optimal worker nodes based on resource capacity and constraints.
  - `kube-controller-manager`: Runs controllers (Node, Deployment, ReplicaSet) to maintain desired cluster state.
- **Data Plane (Worker Nodes):**
  - `kubelet`: Primary node agent ensuring containers described in PodSpecs are running and healthy.
  - `kube-proxy`: Maintains network rules and packet forwarding to enable service discovery and communication.
  - `Container Runtime` (`containerd`): Pulls images and executes containers.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are the different types of deployment strategies used in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The primary deployment strategies in Kubernetes are:
1. **RollingUpdate (Default):** Gradually replaces pods with new versions one by one to ensure zero downtime.
2. **Recreate:** Terminates all existing version pods before launching new version pods (incurs downtime).
3. **Blue/Green:** Runs two identical environments in parallel; once the new environment (Green) passes tests, traffic is switched over instantaneously.
4. **Canary:** Routes a small percentage of production traffic to the new version to validate performance and error rates before full rollout.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you explain the difference between the Recreate and Rolling Update deployment strategies in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **RollingUpdate:** Upgrades application instances incrementally. It uses parameters `maxUnavailable` (maximum pods that can be down) and `maxSurge` (maximum extra pods created), ensuring users experience zero downtime.
- **Recreate:** Shuts down 100% of existing application pods simultaneously, resulting in a service outage, and then boots up the new pods. It is chosen when the application cannot support multi-version concurrency or requires exclusive database schema migrations.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Specifically, how does the Recreate strategy handle pod deployment when rolling out a new application version, compared to how the Rolling Update strategy handles new pods?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Recreate Strategy:** It scales the old ReplicaSet down to `0` first. All old pods are terminated and deleted. Once all old pods are gone, it scales the new ReplicaSet up from `0` to the desired count.
- **Rolling Update Strategy:** It creates the new ReplicaSet and scales it up incrementally while scaling the old ReplicaSet down in parallel, ensuring that total available pods never drop below the configured threshold.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain what Ingress is in Kubernetes and why it was introduced? If you have 8 microservices and only one application load balancer, and you don't want to create multiple load balancers for each microservice, how does Ingress help solve this, and what is the traffic flow?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Without Ingress, exposing 8 microservices externally using `LoadBalancer` service types would create 8 separate cloud load balancers, drastically increasing cloud costs.
**Ingress** provides a single entry point (Layer 7 HTTP/HTTPS router) managing external traffic to internal cluster services:
- **Traffic Flow:**
  1. External user request hits a single AWS Application Load Balancer (ALB).
  2. The ALB forwards the traffic to the **Ingress Controller** (e.g., NGINX or AWS Load Balancer Controller) running inside the cluster.
  3. The Ingress Controller evaluates Ingress routing rules (path-based like `/orders` -> `orders-svc` or host-based like `api.example.com`).
  4. Traffic is routed directly to the appropriate ClusterIP Service, which distributes it to backend pods.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a Kubernetes pod is continuously failing or stuck in a pending state, how would you troubleshoot it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To troubleshoot a failing or pending pod:
1. **Inspect Status & Events:** Run `kubectl describe pod <pod-name>`:
   - If **Pending:** Check Events for insufficient node CPU/memory, node taint mismatches, or unbound PersistentVolumeClaims (PVC).
   - If **CrashLoopBackOff:** Container started but exited with an error.
2. **Inspect Logs:** Run `kubectl logs <pod-name> --previous` to inspect the crash stack trace.
3. **Verify Configuration:** Check that ConfigMaps, Secrets, environment variables, and liveness/readiness probes are correctly configured.
4. **Node Capacity:** Run `kubectl top nodes` and `kubectl describe node` to check resource allocation.
</details>

#### 【 NETWORKING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you worked with VPC Peering, Transit Gateway, and Direct Connect? Can you explain how VPC Peering and Transit Gateway work?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **VPC Peering:** Connects two VPCs directly 1-to-1 over AWS's private network backbone. It has no bandwidth bottleneck and no extra hourly charge. However, it is **non-transitive** (no daisy-chaining).
- **AWS Transit Gateway (TGW):** Acts as a cloud router connecting multiple VPCs and on-premises networks in a hub-and-spoke model. It supports transitive routing, making multi-account enterprise networking simple to manage.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you continue explaining how AWS Transit Gateway works?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AWS Transit Gateway operates as a regional virtual router:
- You deploy a central Transit Gateway and create **Attachments** for each VPC, Site-to-Site VPN, or Direct Connect connection.
- TGW uses **Transit Gateway Route Tables** to control how traffic flows between attachments.
- When an instance in VPC-A communicates with VPC-B, VPC-A's subnet route table directs VPC-B's CIDR traffic to the TGW attachment. The Transit Gateway inspects the packet and routes it directly to VPC-B over the AWS private network, avoiding mesh peering complexity.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If you need to connect three VPCs (A, B, and C) using VPC Peering, how would you ensure all three are interconnected? How do you establish these connections?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Because VPC peering does not support transitive routing, connecting three VPCs (A, B, and C) requires creating a full-mesh topology with **3 separate peering connections**:
1. Connection 1: **VPC A <-> VPC B**
2. Connection 2: **VPC B <-> VPC C**
3. Connection 3: **VPC A <-> VPC C**
In each VPC's route table, you must add explicit routes directing the other two VPCs' CIDR ranges to their respective peering connection IDs (`pcx-xxxx`).
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a junior team member comes to you saying they are unable to SSH into an EC2 instance (getting a timeout), how would you troubleshoot this? What initial things would you check, and what might be the possible causes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
An SSH connection **timeout** indicates that packets are being dropped before reaching the operating system:
1. **Security Group:** Verify that the EC2 instance's Security Group has an inbound rule allowing port 22 (SSH) from the user's current public IP address.
2. **Route Table & Subnet:** Ensure the instance is in a Public Subnet with a route to an Internet Gateway (`0.0.0.0/0 -> igw-xxxx`) and that a Public IP or Elastic IP is assigned.
3. **Network ACL (NACL):** Check that the subnet NACL allows inbound traffic on port 22 and outbound ephemeral ports (1024-65535).
4. **AWS Console Status:** Check instance state and verify that system and instance status checks (2/2 checks) are passing.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Assuming the user already has all the necessary access permissions and regularly uses a UAT/staging/dev EC2 instance, but suddenly cannot SSH into it, what other reasons (besides access/IAM issues) could cause this?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
If IAM and network rules were previously functional, common causes for sudden SSH timeouts include:
- **Public IP Changed:** The EC2 instance was stopped and started without an Elastic IP attached, changing its public IP address.
- **User Public IP Changed:** The developer's ISP or VPN IP address rotated, no longer matching the Security Group whitelist.
- **Server Overload / Freeze:** High CPU utilization (100%) or memory exhaustion (OOM lockup) causing the OS kernel to stop responding.
- **OS-Level Firewall:** A host firewall (e.g., `ufw` or `iptables`) was enabled or misconfigured inside the Linux OS blocking port 22.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is an S3 bucket, and why is it used in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
An S3 bucket is a globally unique top-level container in Amazon Simple Storage Service (S3) that stores unstructured data as objects (files and metadata). It is used for:
- Storing backups, log files, and application media.
- Storing Terraform state files (`terraform.tfstate`) with versioning enabled.
- Hosting static web content behind CloudFront.
- Storing big data analytics datasets with 11 9's durability.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you worked with AWS RDS and Aurora? Can you explain the architectural difference between RDS and Aurora?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have worked with both:
- **AWS RDS:** Standard database running on EC2 instances with dedicated EBS volumes. Multi-AZ is achieved via synchronous EBS block replication to a single standby in another AZ.
- **Amazon Aurora:** Decouples database compute from storage. Storage is distributed across a shared SSD storage fleet spanning 3 Availability Zones with 6-way data replication. Aurora provides up to 5x performance of standard MySQL and up to 15 auto-scaling read replicas.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a client wants to save costs by installing MySQL directly on an EC2 instance instead of using RDS, how would you convince them of the advantages of using RDS over self-managing a database on EC2?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I would present the **Total Cost of Ownership (TCO)** and operational risk:
- **Hidden Labor Costs:** Managing MySQL on EC2 requires dedicated engineering hours to configure backups, monitor replication lag, handle OS security patches, and test disaster recovery procedures.
- **High Availability:** Setting up automated zero-data-loss failover on EC2 requires complex clustering. RDS Multi-AZ handles failover automatically in 60-120 seconds.
- **Risk Mitigation:** RDS provides automated point-in-time recovery, automatic minor version patching, and push-button storage scaling. The risk of database downtime or data loss on EC2 far exceeds the minor savings on compute.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What specific advantages do you get from using RDS (with MySQL) compared to installing and managing MySQL yourself on an EC2 instance long-term?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Concrete long-term advantages:
1. **Automated Maintenance:** Point-in-time recovery to any second, daily snapshots, and automatic engine security patches.
2. **High Availability:** Out-of-the-box Multi-AZ synchronous replication with automatic failover.
3. **Read Replicas:** One-click deployment of up to 5 read replicas to offload read-heavy queries.
4. **Storage Auto-Scaling:** Dynamically expands storage capacity as data grows without downtime.
5. **Observability:** Built-in CloudWatch metrics, Enhanced Monitoring, and Performance Insights.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain the difference between Network ACLs (NACLs) and Security Groups in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Security Groups:** Operate at the **instance (ENI) level**, are **stateful** (return traffic is automatically allowed), support **allow rules only**, and evaluate all rules before permitting traffic.
- **Network ACLs:** Operate at the **subnet level**, are **stateless** (inbound and outbound rules must be defined explicitly), support both **allow and deny rules**, and evaluate rules in **strict numerical order**.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you worked on any cost optimization techniques for AWS infrastructure to reduce costs? Can you describe how you implemented them?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I implemented several key cost-saving measures:
1. **Automated Scheduling:** Wrote a Python Boto3 script scheduled via EventBridge to shut down non-production EC2 and RDS instances after 7 PM and on weekends, saving ~65% on non-prod compute.
2. **Compute Savings Plans & Right-Sizing:** Analyzed CloudWatch and AWS Compute Optimizer metrics to downsize underutilized EC2 instances and purchased 1-year Savings Plans for stable workloads.
3. **Storage Lifecycle Management:** Implemented S3 lifecycle rules transitioning old build artifacts and logs to S3 Standard-IA after 30 days and Glacier after 90 days.
4. **Cleanup:** Automated cleanup of unattached EBS volumes and outdated EBS snapshots.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain one of your CI/CD pipelines end-to-end, from code commit to production deployment?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In my recent project, our CI/CD pipeline is implemented using GitHub, Jenkins, Docker, and AWS EKS:

1. **Trigger & Checkout**: When a developer merges code into the `main` branch, a GitHub webhook triggers our declarative Jenkins pipeline, which pulls the latest code.
2. **Build & Unit Test**: Jenkins executes Maven unit tests (`mvn clean test`) and compiles the application.
3. **Static Code Analysis**: The pipeline triggers a SonarQube scan to verify code quality, security vulnerabilities, and code coverage, halting if Quality Gates fail.
4. **Container Build & Push**: Jenkins builds a Docker container image, tags it with the Git commit hash and build number, and pushes it to Amazon ECR.
5. **Deployment**: Jenkins updates the Kubernetes deployment manifest with the new image tag and deploys it to our AWS EKS cluster using `kubectl` or Helm, executing a zero-downtime rolling update.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How did you integrate SonarQube and Artifactory with Jenkins in your CI/CD pipeline?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **SonarQube Integration**:
  1. Installed the **SonarQube Scanner** plugin in Jenkins.
  2. Configured the SonarQube server URL and secret authentication token under **Manage Jenkins > System**.
  3. In the `Jenkinsfile`, wrapped the Maven scan inside `withSonarQubeEnv('SonarQube') { sh 'mvn sonar:sonar' }` followed by `waitForQualityGate()` to block the pipeline if quality rules fail.
- **Artifactory Integration**:
  1. Installed the **JFrog Artifactory** plugin and stored credentials in Jenkins Credentials Manager.
  2. In the pipeline, used `rtServer` or Docker pipeline steps (`docker.withRegistry()`) to authenticate and push built application binaries (JAR/WAR) and container images directly to Artifactory repositories.
</details>

#### 【 DOCKER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain the lifecycle of a Docker container?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A Docker container goes through several distinct states in its lifecycle:

- **Created**: The container is created from an image using `docker create`, allocating filesystem layers without starting the process.
- **Running**: The container actively executes its main application process via `docker run` or `docker start`.
- **Paused**: All running processes in the container are temporarily suspended in memory using `docker pause` (resumed with `docker unpause`).
- **Stopped**: The container process is gracefully terminated using `docker stop` (`SIGTERM` signal, followed by `SIGKILL` if it doesn't terminate within 10 seconds).
- **Exited / Dead**: The process completes its execution or crashes.
- **Removed**: The container metadata and writable storage layer are permanently deleted using `docker rm`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain what a multi-stage Dockerfile is and why it's used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A multi-stage Dockerfile uses multiple `FROM` statements in a single Dockerfile to separate the application build environment from the final runtime environment:

- **How it works**:
  - *Build Stage*: Uses a heavyweight SDK image (e.g., `maven:3.8-openjdk-17`) to compile source code, download dependencies, and package the binary.
  - *Final Runtime Stage*: Uses a lightweight base image (e.g., `openjdk:17-alpine`) and copies only the compiled JAR/binary from the build stage using `COPY --from=builder /app/target/app.jar /app/app.jar`.
- **Why it's used**:
  1. **Smaller Image Size**: Drastically reduces image size (from ~800MB down to ~150MB).
  2. **Enhanced Security**: Eliminates compilers, package managers, and unnecessary tools from production containers, reducing vulnerabilities.
  3. **Simplicity**: Keeps the entire build and packaging workflow in one clean, version-controlled file.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you troubleshoot a CrashLoopBackOff error in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
`CrashLoopBackOff` means a container repeatedly starts, crashes, and Kubernetes restarts it with exponential delay. Here is my troubleshooting process:

1. **Check Pod Events**: Run `kubectl describe pod <pod-name>` to check termination reasons (e.g., `OOMKilled` with exit code 137, failed liveness probes, or missing ConfigMaps/Secrets).
2. **Check Container Logs**: Run `kubectl logs <pod-name> --previous` to inspect the logs of the crashed container instance before termination (e.g., database connection failure, invalid application configuration, or syntax error).
3. **Verify Configurations**: Ensure all environment variables, Secret keys, and ConfigMaps referenced in the deployment manifest actually exist.
4. **Resource Constraints**: If terminated due to `OOMKilled`, increase the container's memory requests and limits in the deployment YAML.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between a ClusterIP service and a NodePort service in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **ClusterIP**:
  - The default service type in Kubernetes.
  - Assigns a stable, internal IP address accessible **only** within the Kubernetes cluster.
  - Used for inter-service communication between internal microservices, backend APIs, and databases.
- **NodePort**:
  - Exposes the service on each worker node's external IP address at a static port (default port range: 30000–32767).
  - External clients can access the application by connecting to `<NodeIP>:<NodePort>`.
- In production, we rarely expose NodePorts directly to the public internet; instead, we expose ClusterIP services securely through an **Ingress Controller** or AWS Application Load Balancer.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you briefly explain the difference between ConfigMaps and Secrets in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Both ConfigMaps and Secrets decouple application configuration data from container images, but serve different purposes:

- **ConfigMap**:
  - Used for **non-sensitive** configuration values such as environment variables, server URLs, port numbers, and configuration files (e.g., `nginx.conf` or `application.properties`).
  - Stored in plain text.
- **Secret**:
  - Used for **sensitive** information such as database passwords, API tokens, SSH keys, and TLS certificates.
  - Stored in base64-encoded format by default (and can be encrypted at rest using AWS KMS or integrated with external secret managers like AWS Secrets Manager or HashiCorp Vault).
- Both can be injected into pods as environment variables or mounted as files inside volumes.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain the Terraform workflow from 'terraform init' to 'terraform apply'?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The standard Terraform workflow follows four core steps:

1. `terraform init`: Initializes the working directory, downloads required provider plugins (e.g., AWS provider), child modules, and configures the remote backend (S3 with DynamoDB locking).
2. `terraform validate`: Verifies the syntax, arguments, and internal consistency of the configuration files without reaching out to cloud APIs.
3. `terraform plan`: Compares the desired state in code against the existing state and cloud resources, generating an execution plan that details which resources will be created (`+`), modified (`~`), or destroyed (`-`).
4. `terraform apply`: Prompts for confirmation and provisions/updates the declared resources in the cloud provider, updating the `terraform.tfstate` file upon completion.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you roll back changes made through Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Terraform does not have an automatic `terraform rollback` command. In a 5-year DevOps GitOps workflow, we roll back using Git and Terraform plan/apply:

1. **Revert Git Commit**: Revert the problematic code commit in Git using `git revert <commit_id>` and push it through a pull request.
2. **Review Plan**: Run `terraform plan` to verify that Terraform intends to undo the unwanted changes and bring infrastructure back to the previous desired state.
3. **Apply Reverted State**: Run `terraform apply` to apply the reverted configuration against the cloud provider.
4. **State Restoration (if corrupted)**: If the state file itself was corrupted during a failed run, restore the previous healthy state file using versioning enabled on the remote S3 bucket.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain what a Terraform remote backend is and how it works?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A Terraform remote backend defines where Terraform stores its state file (`terraform.tfstate`) centrally, rather than on local developer laptops.

- **How it works (AWS S3 + DynamoDB)**:
  1. **Centralized Storage**: The state file is stored in an Amazon S3 bucket with versioning and server-side encryption enabled.
  2. **State Locking**: A DynamoDB table with primary partition key `LockID` is configured for locking.
  3. **Execution**: When a developer or CI/CD runner runs `terraform plan` or `terraform apply`, Terraform acquires an exclusive lock in DynamoDB. Other runs are blocked until execution completes, preventing concurrent runs and state corruption. Once complete, updated state is written to S3 and the lock is released.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain what an Ansible inventory is and how it's used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
An Ansible inventory is a file (in INI or YAML format) that defines the target hosts and groups of servers that Ansible manages:

- **How it's used**:
  - **Host Grouping**: Categorizes managed servers into logical groups (e.g., `[webservers]`, `[databases]`, `[prod]`).
  - **Variable Assignment**: Sets connection variables like `ansible_user`, `ansible_host`, `ansible_port`, or `ansible_ssh_private_key_file`.
- **Types**:
  1. *Static Inventory*: A text file manually listing fixed IP addresses and hostnames.
  2. *Dynamic Inventory*: A script or plugin (e.g., `aws_ec2` plugin) that dynamically discovers EC2 instances from cloud APIs at runtime using tags and states.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain how auto scaling works in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AWS Auto Scaling dynamically adjusts the number of EC2 instances in an Auto Scaling Group (ASG) to handle traffic demand and minimize costs:

1. **Launch Template**: Specifies instance AMI ID, instance type, key pair, security groups, and user-data bootstrap script.
2. **Auto Scaling Group (ASG)**: Defines VPC subnets, minimum, maximum, and desired capacity, attached to an Application Load Balancer (ALB).
3. **Scaling Policies**:
   - *Target Tracking*: Automatically scales instances to keep a target metric steady (e.g., keep average CPU utilization at 60%).
   - *Step / Scheduled Scaling*: Scales based on CloudWatch alarm thresholds or predefined business schedules.
4. **Self-Healing**: Continuously monitors EC2 status and ALB health checks, automatically terminating unhealthy instances and replacing them with fresh ones.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you differentiate between an IAM role and an IAM user in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **IAM User**:
  - Represents a specific person or service requiring persistent access to AWS.
  - Uses long-term credentials: username/password for AWS Management Console, and Access Key ID / Secret Access Key for CLI/API.
  - Requires manual credential rotation and poses security risks if keys are leaked.
- **IAM Role**:
  - An identity with permission policies that is **not** tied to a specific person.
  - Assumed temporarily by trusted entities such as AWS services (EC2 instances, Lambda functions, EKS pods) or federated corporate users via SSO.
  - Uses short-lived, automatically rotating credentials provided by AWS STS (Security Token Service), eliminating the need for hardcoded secrets.
</details>

#### 【 MONITORING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How many monitoring tools do you have experience with?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In my 5 years of DevOps experience, I have hands-on production experience with three primary monitoring toolsets:

1. **Prometheus & Grafana**: Deployed in Kubernetes (EKS) for cluster and container metrics, writing PromQL queries, building operational dashboards, and routing alerts via Alertmanager.
2. **AWS CloudWatch**: Used for native AWS infrastructure monitoring (EC2 CPU, RDS connections, ALB latency and 5xx errors), log groups, and CloudWatch Alarms.
3. **ELK / EFK Stack (Elasticsearch, Fluentd/Filebeat, Kibana)**: Used for centralized application log aggregation, real-time searching, and log-based troubleshooting.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How does Prometheus collect metrics from Kubernetes nodes and pods?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Prometheus uses an HTTP **pull-based scraping model** to collect metrics from Kubernetes:

1. **Node Metrics**: **Node Exporter** runs as a DaemonSet on every worker node, exposing host OS and hardware metrics (CPU, RAM, disk, network) on port 9100 at `/metrics`.
2. **Container Metrics**: Kubelet contains an embedded **cAdvisor** engine that collects container-level resource usage (CPU, memory, filesystem) and exposes them on the kubelet metrics endpoint.
3. **Pod / App Metrics**: Applications expose metrics using Prometheus client libraries at an HTTP endpoint (typically `/metrics`).
4. **Service Discovery**: Prometheus discovers pods and nodes automatically using Kubernetes API service discovery (`kubernetes_sd_configs`) or Prometheus Operator `ServiceMonitor` resources based on labels and annotations, scraping targets at regular intervals.
</details>

#### 【 SYSTEM DESIGN 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain what a canary deployment strategy is?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A canary deployment is a progressive deployment strategy where a new version of an application is rolled out to a small subset of real users before releasing it to the entire infrastructure:

1. **Initial Phase**: Deploy the new version alongside the current stable version. Route a small percentage of user traffic (e.g., 5% to 10%) to the new version using an Ingress Controller, AWS ALB weighted target groups, or a Service Mesh (Istio).
2. **Observation**: Monitor golden signals—HTTP error rates, response latency, and CPU/memory usage—comparing the canary version against the baseline.
3. **Full Rollout or Rollback**:
   - If performance and error rates are healthy, gradually increase traffic (25%, 50%, 100%) and decommission the old version.
   - If errors spike, immediately redirect 100% of traffic back to the stable version with zero downtime for users.
</details>

</details>

</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the role of the inventory file in Ansible?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The inventory file serves as the central directory and source of truth for all target systems managed by Ansible. Its role is to:

1. List hostnames and IP addresses of target managed servers.
2. Group servers into logical roles (e.g., `[webservers]`, `[dbservers]`, `[prod]`).
3. Define host-level and group-level connection variables (e.g., `ansible_user`, `ansible_ssh_private_key_file`, `ansible_port`).
4. Enable playbooks and ad-hoc commands to target specific environments or server tiers without hardcoding host details.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What are the different types of inventory files in Ansible?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
There are two primary types of inventory files in Ansible:

1. **Static Inventory**: A plain text file formatted in INI or YAML that manually lists static IP addresses, hostnames, and groups. Best suited for on-premises or stable environments where server IPs rarely change.
2. **Dynamic Inventory**: A dynamic script or plugin (e.g., `aws_ec2` plugin, Azure, or GCP plugins) that automatically queries cloud provider APIs at runtime to discover running instances based on tags, regions, and instance states. Essential for auto-scaling and cloud environments.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How does variable precedence work in Ansible?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Ansible has an established variable precedence hierarchy (around 22 levels) where narrower, more specific scopes override wider, general scopes.

In simple terms from lowest to highest:
1. Role defaults (`roles/my_role/defaults/main.yml`) – lowest priority.
2. Inventory file group variables and host variables (`group_vars`, `host_vars`).
3. Playbook vars and included vars.
4. Role vars (`roles/my_role/vars/main.yml`).
5. Task variables and registered vars.
6. Extra vars passed on the command line (`-e "var=value"`) – absolute highest priority.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In Ansible's variable precedence hierarchy, which source has the highest precedence?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Extra vars** passed via the command line with `-e` or `--extra-vars` have the absolute highest precedence in Ansible.

For example:
```bash
ansible-playbook deploy.yml -e "app_version=2.1.0 env=production"
```
Any variable defined with `-e` overrides values set anywhere else—including inventory files, role defaults, role vars, and playbook vars.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● When would you use handlers in Ansible?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Handlers are used when you want a task to run **only** when a change has actually occurred on the managed host.

- **Common Use Case**: Restarting or reloading a service (such as Nginx, Apache, or Docker) only when its configuration file template has been modified.
- **How it works**: A task includes a `notify: Restart Nginx` directive. If the task makes a change (`changed=true`), the handler is flagged. Handlers run once at the very end of the play, preventing unnecessary repeated service restarts even if multiple tasks notify the same handler.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How does Ansible ensure secure communication between the control node and target hosts?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Ansible ensures secure communication using native, industry-standard encrypted protocols without requiring custom agent daemons:

1. **Linux/Unix Targets**: Communicates over **SSH (OpenSSH)** using public/private key authentication or passphrase-protected SSH keys with AES encryption.
2. **Windows Targets**: Communicates over **WinRM (Windows Remote Management)** configured with HTTPS (port 5986) and TLS/SSL certificates.
3. **Sensitive Data**: Sensitive credentials, passwords, and tokens within playbooks and variable files are encrypted at rest using **Ansible Vault** (`ansible-vault encrypt`).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: You mentioned Ansible can also use HTTPS for secure communication — for which specific operating system is that used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Ansible uses HTTPS for **Microsoft Windows** operating systems.

It connects to Windows hosts using **WinRM over HTTPS** on default TCP port `5986`. This ensures all remote management traffic, PowerShell commands, and credentials transmitted between the Linux Ansible control node and Windows managed nodes are securely encrypted using TLS/SSL.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are Ansible facts and how are they used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Ansible facts are host-specific system properties and hardware details automatically discovered and gathered by the `setup` module when a playbook begins execution.

- **Information Collected**: OS distribution (`ansible_os_family`, `ansible_distribution`), IP addresses, CPU architecture, memory, disk partitions, and hostname.
- **How they are used**:
  1. *Conditional execution*: Running tasks conditionally, e.g., `when: ansible_os_family == "RedHat"`.
  2. *Dynamic templates*: Injecting dynamic host IP or hostname into configuration files via Jinja2 templates.
  3. *Dynamic decisions*: Allocating memory or worker thread counts proportional to total system RAM.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you handle errors or exceptions in Ansible playbooks?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Ansible provides multiple directives to manage errors gracefully:

1. `ignore_errors: yes`: Continues playbook execution even if a specific non-critical task fails.
2. `failed_when: <condition>`: Defines custom failure conditions (e.g., `failed_when: "'ERROR' in result.stdout"`).
3. `block`, `rescue`, and `always`: Implements structured exception handling similar to try-catch-finally:
   - `block`: Main tasks to execute.
   - `rescue`: Tasks that execute only if a task in `block` fails (e.g., send alerts or rollback).
   - `always`: Tasks that run regardless of success or failure (e.g., clean up temporary files).
4. `max_fail_percentage`: Aborts batch execution across a cluster if failures exceed a given threshold.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you manage or control the order of execution for tasks in an Ansible playbook?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
By default, Ansible executes tasks sequentially from top to bottom within a play. We control execution order using:

1. **Playbook Sections**: Ordering tasks across `pre_tasks`, `roles`, regular `tasks`, `post_tasks`, and `handlers`.
2. **Modular Inclusions**: Using `import_tasks` (static pre-parsing order) or `include_tasks` (dynamic conditional evaluation at runtime).
3. **Serial Execution**: Using the `serial` keyword (e.g., `serial: 2` or `serial: "20%"`) to execute all tasks on batches of hosts rather than all hosts at once.
4. **Conditionals**: Using `when` clauses to determine whether specific tasks run or are skipped.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Why do we use ad hoc commands in Ansible when playbooks are generally the preferred approach?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Ad hoc commands (`ansible <hosts> -m <module> -a "<args>"`) are used for quick, one-off operational tasks where creating, testing, and committing a full playbook is unnecessary overhead:

- **Quick Checks**: Checking uptime or memory across 50 servers (`ansible all -m command -a "uptime"`).
- **Emergency Actions**: Instantly restarting a crashed service across web nodes (`ansible webservers -m service -a "name=nginx state=restarted"`).
- **Rebooting & Patching**: Triggering a reboot or ping check across a fleet (`ansible all -m ping`).
Playbooks are saved for repeatable, version-controlled automation.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose you need to update a web application running on 50 servers without causing any downtime for users — how would you approach this using Ansible?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I would implement a zero-downtime rolling update using Ansible's `serial` keyword combined with load balancer connection draining:

1. Set `serial: 5` (or `10%`) to process the 50 servers in batches of 5.
2. In `pre_tasks`, use the AWS ALB target group module or an API call to deregister the current batch of 5 servers from the Load Balancer and wait for connection draining.
3. In `tasks`, deploy the new application code, update configurations, and restart the application service.
4. Run a health check using the `uri` module (`http://localhost:8080/health` returning 200 OK).
5. In `post_tasks`, re-register the servers in the Load Balancer target group and verify they are healthy.
6. Ansible automatically repeats this process for the remaining batches until all 50 servers are updated.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you resolve package dependencies in Python?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We resolve and manage Python dependencies using:

1. **Virtual Environments (`venv`)**: Isolate project dependencies from system-level Python packages using `python -m venv venv` and activating it with `source venv/bin/activate`.
2. **pip and requirements.txt**: Define direct dependencies and install them via `pip install -r requirements.txt`.
3. **Dependency Pinning (`pip freeze`)**: Lock exact package versions and their sub-dependencies into `requirements.txt` using `pip freeze > requirements.txt` to guarantee reproducible builds across environments.
4. Modern tools like `pipenv` or `poetry` can also be used to automatically resolve dependency conflicts and maintain a lockfile.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you write to or read from a file in Python?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Python, the standard practice is using the built-in `open()` function within a `with` statement (context manager), which ensures files are automatically and safely closed:

```python
# Writing to a file (mode 'w' overwrites, 'a' appends)
with open("status.txt", "w") as file:
    file.write("Deployment succeeded\n")

# Reading from a file
with open("status.txt", "r") as file:
    content = file.read()
    print(content)
```
Using `with` guarantees proper resource cleanup even if exceptions occur.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you handle exceptions in Python?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We handle exceptions using `try`, `except`, `else`, and `finally` blocks:

```python
try:
    response = boto3.client('s3').get_object(Bucket="my-bucket", Key="config.json")
except s3_client.exceptions.NoSuchKey:
    print("Specified S3 object key does not exist.")
except Exception as e:
    print(f"Error occurred: {str(e)}")
else:
    print("Object retrieved successfully.")
finally:
    print("Cleanup operations completed.")
```
This ensures error handling is graceful and avoids script crashes during automated runs.
</details>

</details>

#### 【 LINUX 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you rate your proficiency and experience with Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I would rate my Linux proficiency an 8 out of 10. Over 5 years of daily hands-on DevOps work, I have extensive experience with Ubuntu, RHEL, and Amazon Linux. I comfortably manage user permissions (`chmod`, `chown`), processes (`ps`, `top`, `kill`), disk space (`df -h`, `du -sh`), networking (`ss`, `netstat`, `curl`), services via `systemctl`, and automate tasks using Bash scripting with `grep`, `awk`, and `sed`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you copy a file from one server to another server using Linux commands?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We can copy files securely between Linux servers using `scp` or `rsync`:

- **Using `scp` (Secure Copy)**:
  ```bash
  scp -i key.pem /local/path/file.txt user@remote-ip:/remote/destination/
  ```
- **Using `rsync` (Recommended)**:
  ```bash
  rsync -avz -e "ssh -i key.pem" /local/path/file.txt user@remote-ip:/remote/destination/
  ```
`rsync` is preferred in production because it supports resume capabilities, preserves file permissions, and transfers only delta changes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you create a 0-byte (empty) file in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The standard and most common command is `touch`:
```bash
touch myfile.txt
```
If `myfile.txt` does not exist, `touch` creates an empty 0-byte file.
Alternative quick methods include:
- Shell redirection: `> myfile.txt` or `: > myfile.txt`
- Using `truncate`: `truncate -s 0 myfile.txt`
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you create a file in Linux with the default file permissions?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Creating a file using standard commands like `touch filename` or `> filename` automatically applies the default file permissions.

- In Linux, the default permissions are determined by subtracting the system `umask` from the maximum file permission (`666` for regular files).
- With a standard umask of `022` (`666 - 022`), the newly created file receives default permissions of `644` (`-rw-r--r--` : read/write for owner, read-only for group and others).
</details>

Integrated with Jenkins, the playbook automatically accepts build parameters, performs pre-deployment backups, validates configuration syntax before reloading services, and runs health checks. This reduced deployment time from 45 minutes to under 3 minutes with 100% consistency.

<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What specific types of Ansible playbooks have you written?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I have written several types of playbooks:
1. **Provisioning & Hardening Playbooks**: Setting up base OS packages, user accounts, SSH keys, firewall rules, and CIS benchmark hardening.
2. **Web & App Server Playbooks**: Installing and configuring Nginx, Apache, Tomcat, and Node.js runtimes.
3. **Application Deployment Playbooks**: Managing application releases, artifact extraction, configuration templating, and service restarts.

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you demonstrate how you write an Ansible playbook from scratch, for example by typing a sample playbook in an editor?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Here is a clean, standard Ansible playbook to install, configure, and start an Nginx web server:

```yaml
---
- name: Install and configure Nginx web server
  hosts: webservers
  become: yes
  tasks:
    - name: Install Nginx package
      yum:
        name: nginx
        state: present

    - name: Copy custom Nginx configuration
      copy:
        src: files/nginx.conf
        dest: /etc/nginx/nginx.conf
        owner: root
        group: root
        mode: '0644'
      notify: Restart Nginx

    - name: Ensure Nginx service is running and enabled
      service:
        name: nginx
        state: started
        enabled: yes

  handlers:
    - name: Restart Nginx
      service:
        name: nginx
        state: restarted
```
This playbook targets the `webservers` group, escalates privileges (`become: yes`), installs the package, copies configuration, triggers a handler only if modified, and ensures the service is enabled.
</details>

#### 【 SYSTEM DESIGN 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Are you familiar with SRE (Site Reliability Engineering), and can you explain what it is?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes. Site Reliability Engineering (SRE) is a discipline pioneered by Google that applies software engineering principles to operations and infrastructure problems to build highly scalable and reliable systems.

Core concepts include:
- **SLI (Service Level Indicator)**: A measurable metric of service performance (e.g., latency or error rate).
- **SLO (Service Level Objective)**: A target reliability goal agreed upon with business stakeholders (e.g., 99.9% uptime).
- **SLA (Service Level Agreement)**: A formal contract with customers specifying consequences if SLO is missed.
- **Error Budget**: The acceptable margin of unreliability (`100% - SLO`), balancing rapid software delivery against stability.
- **Toil Reduction**: Eliminating repetitive manual operational work through automation.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Have you implemented any SRE best practices in your current role?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, we implemented several key SRE practices:
1. **SLO Tracking**: Defined SLIs/SLOs for our microservices (99.9% availability and p95 latency < 300ms) and tracked them via Grafana dashboards.
2. **Toil Automation**: Automated repetitive manual tasks—like server patching, disk log rotation, and deployment rollbacks—using Ansible and Jenkins pipelines.
3. **Blameless Post-Mortems**: Held blameless retrospectives after major production incidents to document root causes and action items.
4. **Smart Alerting**: Replaced noisy individual metric alerts with actionable alerts based on SLO error budget burn rates to reduce alert fatigue.
</details>

</details>

</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a Python script runs successfully when executed manually but fails when run through Jenkins, how would you debug and troubleshoot the issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
This is typically caused by environment differences between user sessions and the Jenkins service:

1. **User Permissions**: Jenkins runs under the `jenkins` system user, which may lack permissions to specific files, directories, or SSH keys that your personal user has.
2. **Environment Variables & PATH**: The Jenkins shell environment has a minimal `PATH`. Ensure Python and required binary paths are explicitly exported or use absolute paths (e.g., `/usr/bin/python3`).
3. **Dependencies & Virtualenv**: The script may rely on packages installed in a personal user profile; ensure Jenkins activates the proper virtual environment (`source /path/to/venv/bin/activate`).
4. **Working Directory Context**: Verify the script's execution directory in Jenkins workspace versus where it was run manually.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● If you had 200 Windows servers and needed to automate daily log cleanup on all of them, how would you approach it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I would use **Ansible** configured with WinRM:

1. Organize all 200 Windows hosts into an Ansible inventory group `[windows_fleet]`.
2. Write an Ansible playbook using the `ansible.windows.win_find` module to find `.log` files older than 14 or 30 days in specific directories, and `ansible.windows.win_file` with `state: absent` to delete them.
3. Execute the playbook centrally from the Linux Ansible control node, or schedule it via a Jenkins scheduled pipeline with log reporting.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In that log cleanup automation scenario, how would you schedule the Ansible playbook to run automatically?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I would schedule it through **Jenkins**:

1. Create a Jenkins declarative pipeline project containing the Ansible execution step (`ansible-playbook -i inventory/windows_hosts cleanup_logs.yml`).
2. Configure the pipeline's **Build Periodic / Cron Trigger** (e.g., `H 2 * * *` to run nightly at 2 AM).
3. This approach provides centralized execution history, build console logs, execution time tracking, and automated email/Slack alerts if any server fails during cleanup.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Since crontab doesn't work on Windows, how would you schedule the log cleanup task specifically on Windows servers?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
On Windows servers, we use the **Windows Task Scheduler**:

- We can write a PowerShell script that cleans up files:
  ```powershell
  Get-ChildItem -Path "C:\logs" -Filter *.log -Recurse | Where-Object { $_.LastWriteTime -lt (Get-Date).AddDays(-30) } | Remove-Item -Force
  ```
- We can schedule it locally via PowerShell:
  ```powershell
  $action = New-ScheduledTaskAction -Execute 'PowerShell.exe' -Argument '-File C:\scripts\cleanup.ps1'
  $trigger = New-ScheduledTaskTrigger -Daily -At 2am
  Register-ScheduledTask -Action $action -Trigger $trigger -TaskName "DailyLogCleanup" -User "SYSTEM"
  ```
- Across 200 servers, we deploy and register this task automatically using Ansible's `community.windows.win_scheduled_task` module.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you worked with AWS Security Groups and Network ACLs (NACLs)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, both are essential network security layers in AWS VPC:

- **Security Groups (SG)**:
  - Act as a virtual firewall at the **instance / ENI level**.
  - **Stateful**: Return traffic is automatically allowed regardless of inbound rules.
  - Support **allow rules only** (all other traffic is denied by default).
- **Network ACLs (NACL)**:
  - Act as a firewall at the **subnet boundary level**.
  - **Stateless**: Inbound and outbound traffic must be explicitly permitted.
  - Support both **allow and deny rules**, evaluated in numerical order.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you create explicit deny rules in an AWS Security Group?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
No, you cannot create explicit deny rules in an AWS Security Group.

Security Groups operate on an implicit "deny-all" model where you can only specify **allow** rules. Any traffic not explicitly allowed is blocked. If you need an explicit deny rule (for example, blocking traffic from a specific malicious IP address or subnet), you must configure a **deny rule in a Network ACL (NACL)** at the subnet level.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If you have an application hosted on an EC2 instance that is inaccessible, how would you troubleshoot the issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I follow an outside-in, systematic troubleshooting methodology:

1. **Check AWS Instance Health**: Check EC2 console for System and Instance Status Checks (ensure 2/2 checks pass).
2. **Verify Network & Security Groups**: Ensure the instance's Security Group allows inbound traffic on the application port from the client IP, and subnet route tables route traffic to the Internet Gateway.
3. **Connect to the Instance**: Connect via SSH or AWS Systems Manager (SSM) Session Manager.
4. **Verify Application Process**: Check if the application service is running using `systemctl status <app>` or `ps aux | grep <app>`.
5. **Check Port Listening**: Run `netstat -tulnp` or `ss -tulnp` to confirm the application is listening on `0.0.0.0:<port>` rather than `127.0.0.1`.
6. **Inspect Logs**: Check application logs and system logs (`journalctl -xe`) for crash errors.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you walk through your step-by-step troubleshooting process for this inaccessible application scenario?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Step 1 - External Test**: Test reachability from the outside using `curl -Iv http://<public-ip>:<port>` and `telnet <public-ip> <port>` to check if the connection times out (firewall/network issue) or is refused (service down).
2. **Step 2 - Security & Routing**: Verify Security Group inbound rules, Subnet Route Tables (0.0.0.0/0 -> IGW), and NACLs.
3. **Step 3 - Host Login**: Log in to the server via SSH or SSM Session Manager.
4. **Step 4 - Local Verification**: Run `curl http://localhost:<port>`. If this succeeds locally, the issue is strictly AWS networking or security groups.
5. **Step 5 - Service Diagnostics**: If local curl fails, run `ps aux` and `systemctl status <service>` to see if it crashed, and inspect `/var/log` application logs to fix the root cause.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you take backups of your infrastructure on AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We take backups using a combination of AWS native services and Infrastructure as Code:

1. **AWS Backup**: A centralized backup service where we define scheduled backup plans, lifecycle rules (moving to cold storage), and retention periods for EBS volumes, RDS databases, EFS file systems, and DynamoDB.
2. **Amazon Data Lifecycle Manager (DLM)**: Configured to automate scheduled snapshots of EBS volumes based on resource tags.
3. **RDS Automated Snapshots**: Daily automated snapshots with point-in-time recovery (PITR) enabled.
4. **Infrastructure as Code (Terraform)**: All VPC, compute, and security group definitions are versioned in Git, allowing us to recreate infrastructure in any region instantly.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: You mentioned EBS volumes being attached — what did you mean by that, and what are they attached to?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Amazon EBS (Elastic Block Store) provides block-level network storage volumes.

When we say an EBS volume is "attached", it means the virtual block storage volume is connected across the AWS network fabric to an **EC2 instance**. Once attached, the EC2 operating system recognizes it as a block device (e.g., `/dev/xvda` for root or `/dev/xvdf` for data), which can then be formatted with a filesystem (like ext4 or xfs) and mounted to a directory to store persistent data.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you take backups of your EBS volumes specifically?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
EBS volume backups are taken as **point-in-time EBS Snapshots** saved into Amazon S3:

1. **Amazon Data Lifecycle Manager (DLM)**: The best-practice automated method. We define a DLM lifecycle policy that looks for volumes with specific tags (e.g., `Backup=Daily`), takes snapshots automatically every night, and deletes snapshots older than 30 days.
2. **AWS Backup**: Provides cross-region and cross-account snapshot replication policies.
3. **Manual / CLI**: Run `aws ec2 create-snapshot --volume-id vol-0123456789abcdef0 --description "Manual pre-upgrade backup"`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● In your enterprise environment, is all your infrastructure deployed in a public subnet or a private subnet?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our enterprise environment, backend infrastructure—including EC2 application servers, EKS worker nodes, and RDS database instances—is deployed exclusively in **private subnets** with no public IP addresses.

- Only public-facing **Application Load Balancers (ALB)** and **NAT Gateways** reside in **public subnets**.
- Inbound internet traffic enters through the ALB, which forwards requests internally to private application instances.
- Outbound internet access from private instances (for OS patching or external API calls) routes securely through the NAT Gateway.
</details>

#### 【 MONITORING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● If you received 10,000 alert emails generated from AWS overnight, what would be your steps when you come in the next morning to handle this situation?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Immediate Triage & Impact Check**: Check the alert subject lines and open our production Grafana/CloudWatch dashboards to determine whether there is an active production outage affecting users or if it is a flapping threshold alert.
2. **Mute / Suppress**: Temporarily disable or throttle the offending CloudWatch Alarm or SNS subscription to stop further email flooding while investigating.
3. **Identify Root Cause**: Analyze CloudWatch metrics and logs around the time the storm began (e.g., a batch job causing CPU spikes, an unhandled exception looping, or a flapping health check).
4. **Remediate**: Fix the underlying issue (scale resources or fix code/config).
5. **Long-Term Preventive Measures**: Tune alarm thresholds to require multiple consecutive evaluation periods (e.g., 3 out of 3 datapoints instead of 1) and route alerts to an incident response tool (like PagerDuty or Slack) that performs deduplication and alert aggregation.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is the difference between Python and PowerShell as scripting languages?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **PowerShell**:
  - Developed primarily for Windows system administration and automation.
  - Works with **.NET objects**: commands (cmdlets) pass rich structured objects through pipelines rather than plain text streams, making it very powerful for querying and configuring Windows OS and Active Directory components.
- **Python**:
  - A general-purpose, platform-independent programming language.
  - Uses text/data structures (lists, dictionaries) and has a vast open-source library ecosystem (`boto3`, `requests`, `pandas`).
  - Industry standard for cross-platform cloud automation, CI/CD tooling, and data manipulation across Linux, macOS, and cloud APIs.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you handle exceptions in Python?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We handle exceptions using `try`, `except`, `else`, and `finally` blocks:

```python
try:
    response = boto3.client('s3').get_object(Bucket="my-bucket", Key="config.json")
except s3_client.exceptions.NoSuchKey:
    print("Specified S3 object key does not exist.")
except Exception as e:
    print(f"Error occurred: {str(e)}")
else:
    print("Object retrieved successfully.")
finally:
    print("Cleanup operations completed.")
```
This ensures error handling is graceful and avoids script crashes during automated runs.
</details>

Yes. IIS (Internet Information Services) is Microsoft's web server software designed to run on Windows Server, serving the same role as Nginx or Apache in Linux.

It is used to host websites, web applications, and REST APIs, especially those developed with Microsoft .NET and ASP.NET. It features application pools for process isolation, graphical management via IIS Manager, and remote configuration support via PowerShell.

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain the CI/CD setup you have worked with?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In my recent project, our CI/CD workflow is built with GitHub, Jenkins, Docker, and AWS EKS:

1. Developers push code to GitHub; a webhook triggers our declarative Jenkinsfile pipeline.
2. Jenkins checks out the repository, executes unit tests, and triggers a **SonarQube** code quality and security scan.
3. Once the Quality Gate passes, Jenkins builds a Docker container image tagged with the Git commit ID and pushes it to **AWS ECR**.
4. In the deploy stage, Jenkins updates the Kubernetes deployment manifests and applies them to our **Amazon EKS** cluster using `kubectl` / Helm, performing a rolling update with zero downtime.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you deployed any monitoring systems and worked with microservices running in an EKS cluster?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes. In our AWS EKS cluster, we deployed **Prometheus and Grafana** using the `kube-prometheus-stack` Helm chart:

- Prometheus scrapes metrics from Kubernetes nodes (via Node Exporter), containers (via cAdvisor), and cluster components (via kube-state-metrics).
- For microservices, we configured `ServiceMonitor` custom resources to scrape application-specific endpoints (`/metrics`).
- We configured Grafana dashboards to visualize real-time application throughput, error rates, and pod resource consumption, with Alertmanager sending critical alerts to Slack.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: As part of this CI/CD setup, did you deploy applications to an EKS cluster?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes. Our Jenkins pipeline automates deployments directly to EKS:

- Jenkins assumes an IAM role configured in the EKS `aws-auth` ConfigMap or uses an IAM OIDC role with deployment permissions.
- Using the AWS CLI, the pipeline runs `aws eks update-kubeconfig --name <cluster-name> --region <region>`.
- It then updates the deployment image tag using `kubectl set image deployment/<app-name> <app-name>=<ecr-image>:<tag>` or via `helm upgrade --install`, ensuring Kubernetes performs an automated rolling deployment.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If, after a deployment, a user is unable to access the application, how would you troubleshoot the issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I follow this step-by-step troubleshooting checklist:

1. **Check Pod Status**: Run `kubectl get pods -n <namespace>` to check if pods are `Running` and `Ready` (look for `CrashLoopBackOff`, `ImagePullBackOff`, or `OOMKilled`).
2. **Inspect Logs & Events**: Run `kubectl describe pod <pod-name>` to check for failing readiness/liveness probes and `kubectl logs <pod-name>` for application startup errors.
3. **Verify Service & Endpoints**: Run `kubectl get svc` and `kubectl get endpoints <svc-name>` to ensure the Service selector matches pod labels and has healthy endpoint IPs.
4. **Check Ingress & Load Balancer**: Check Ingress controller logs and AWS ALB target group health checks.
5. **Immediate Rollback**: If troubleshooting takes too long, run `kubectl rollout undo deployment/<app-name>` immediately to restore user access.
</details>

#### 【 MONITORING 】

<details>

**Answer:**
- **Metrics with Prometheus & Grafana**: Deployed using the `kube-prometheus-stack` Helm chart. We configured `ServiceMonitor` resources matching microservice labels to scrape Prometheus metrics from `/metrics`. Added Prometheus as a datasource in Grafana and imported Kubernetes dashboards.
- **Logs with ELK / EFK**: Deployed **Fluentd** or **Filebeat** as a DaemonSet on each EKS node. It collects container logs from `/var/log/containers/*`, parses JSON application logs, and forwards them to an Amazon OpenSearch / Elasticsearch cluster. Configured Kibana index patterns allowing developers to search logs by `kubernetes.pod_name` and `kubernetes.namespace_name`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What kind of dashboards did you create in Grafana?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I created three primary categories of Grafana dashboards:

1. **Cluster Infrastructure Dashboard**: Displays overall cluster health, CPU/memory usage per worker node, node disk space, and network I/O.
2. **Pod & Workload Health Dashboard**: Displays pod status counts, container restarts, memory usage versus limits (to detect OOM risks), and CPU throttling.
3. **Application Golden Signals Dashboard**: Tracks microservice performance metrics: Request Rate (RPS), Error Rate (percentage of 4xx and 5xx HTTP responses), Response Latency (p50, p95, p99 percentiles), and JVM/database connection pool metrics.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is SLI (Service Level Indicator) and SLO (Service Level Objective)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **SLI (Service Level Indicator)**:
  - A measurable quantitative metric that reflects the performance or reliability of a service in real time.
  - *Example*: Proportion of successful HTTP requests: `(Successful Requests (non-5xx) / Total Requests) * 100`.
- **SLO (Service Level Objective)**:
  - A target reliability goal for an SLI agreed upon by engineering and business teams over a specific time window.
  - *Example*: 99.9% of payment requests must succeed over a rolling 30-day window.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Given a payment microservice, how would you define SLIs and SLOs for it, and how would you implement monitoring for them using Grafana?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Definitions**:
  - *Availability SLI*: `(Successful HTTP 2xx/201 payment transactions / Total payment transactions) * 100`.
  - *Availability SLO*: 99.95% over a 30-day window.
  - *Latency SLI*: Percentage of successful requests completed in under 500ms.
  - *Latency SLO*: 99% of requests < 500ms over a 30-day window.
- **Grafana Implementation**:
  1. Microservice exports Prometheus metrics: `payment_requests_total{status="200"}` and latency histogram `payment_duration_seconds_bucket`.
  2. Write PromQL queries to calculate real-time SLI and error budget burn rate:
     `sum(rate(payment_requests_total{status="200"}[5m])) / sum(rate(payment_requests_total[5m])) * 100`.
  3. Build a dedicated Grafana SLO panel with gauge indicators and set Alertmanager alerts when the error budget burns faster than expected.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In the context of dashboards, do you know what the 'golden signals' are in monitoring?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, the "Four Golden Signals" defined by Google SRE are:

1. **Latency**: The time it takes to service a request (measuring both successful and failed requests).
2. **Traffic**: A measure of system demand, such as HTTP requests per second (RPS) or network I/O throughput.
3. **Errors**: The rate of requests that fail, either explicitly (HTTP 500 status codes) or implicitly (incorrect response content).
4. **Saturation**: How full the service is, measuring constrained system resources such as CPU usage, memory utilization, or database connection pool capacity.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Suppose you need to collect and monitor logs from millions of real users in real time — how would you design and implement such a real-time monitoring system?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To handle millions of events per second with high availability:

1. **Edge Ingestion / Buffering**: Application servers and edge nodes push logs into a distributed message broker like **Apache Kafka** or **Amazon Kinesis Data Streams** to buffer traffic spikes and prevent data loss.
2. **Lightweight Log Shippers**: Deploy **Fluentbit** on container nodes to collect and stream logs to Kafka in structured JSON format.
3. **Stream Processing**: Use stream processors (such as Logstash, Apache Flink, or AWS Kinesis Data Firehose) to filter, parse, and mask sensitive PII data.
4. **Storage & Search**: Ingest processed logs into **Amazon OpenSearch / Elasticsearch** for real-time indexing and search, and archive raw logs to **Amazon S3** with lifecycle rules for cost-effective cold storage.
5. **Dashboarding & Alerts**: Visualize errors and throughput on OpenSearch Dashboards or Grafana, setting alarms for error rate anomalies.
</details>

</details>

</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain how a Jenkins CI/CD pipeline works?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A Jenkins CI/CD pipeline automates the software delivery lifecycle through a declarative `Jenkinsfile`:

1. **Trigger**: A code push or PR merge in GitHub automatically triggers the Jenkins pipeline via a webhook.
2. **Checkout**: Jenkins pulls the latest source code onto an executor agent.
3. **Build & Test**: Compiles application code and executes unit tests (e.g., `mvn clean test package`).
4. **Quality & Security**: Scans code with SonarQube, evaluating Quality Gates before proceeding.
5. **Containerize**: Builds a Docker image, tags it with the build ID/commit hash, and pushes it to AWS ECR.
6. **Deploy**: Updates deployment manifests and deploys the new image to Kubernetes or EC2 instances with zero-downtime rolling updates.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain how you automate infrastructure provisioning and management?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We automate infrastructure using **Terraform** for provisioning and **Ansible** for configuration management:

- **Terraform (Provisioning)**: We write modular Terraform code to declare cloud infrastructure—VPCs, subnets, EC2 instances, EKS clusters, and RDS databases. State is stored remotely in Amazon S3 with DynamoDB locking.
- **CI/CD Integration**: Terraform execution is automated via Jenkins pipelines (`terraform plan` on pull request, `terraform apply` on merge to main).
- **Ansible (Configuration)**: Once virtual machines are provisioned, Ansible playbooks configure OS packages, security baselines, and runtime dependencies.
- This ensures all infrastructure is version-controlled, repeatable, and free from manual errors.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If you have provisioned your infrastructure on AWS using infrastructure-as-code and someone makes manual changes via the AWS console, how would you handle this situation?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
This situation is called **configuration drift**. Here is how I handle it:

1. **Detect Drift**: Run `terraform plan`. Terraform compares the actual AWS state against the state file and code, highlighting the manual modifications.
2. **Resolve**:
   - *Revert manual change*: If the manual change was unauthorized or accidental, run `terraform apply` to overwrite the change and restore the infrastructure back to the code definition.
   - *Adopt manual change*: If the manual change was a necessary emergency fix, update the Terraform code to reflect the change, verify with `terraform plan`, and commit the updated code to Git.
3. **Prevent**: Enforce AWS IAM policies removing manual console write permissions, mandating that all changes must go through the CI/CD pipeline.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain what modules are (in the context of infrastructure-as-code tools like Terraform)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Terraform, a module is a container for multiple resources that are used together to encapsulate and standardize infrastructure components.

- A module consists of a dedicated directory containing `.tf` files (`main.tf`, `variables.tf`, `outputs.tf`).
- **Benefits**:
  1. **Reusability**: Write once (e.g., a VPC module) and instantiate it across multiple environments (Dev, Staging, Prod) by passing different input variables.
  2. **Maintainability**: Breaks down complex monolithic architectures into manageable, clean components.
  3. **Best Practices**: Ensures corporate security and tagging standards are baked into modules and consistently applied.
</details>

#### 【 NETWORKING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between VPC peering and AWS Transit Gateway?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **VPC Peering**:
  - A direct 1-to-1 network connection between two VPCs.
  - **Non-transitive**: If VPC A is peered with VPC B, and VPC B is peered with VPC C, VPC A cannot communicate with VPC C through B.
  - Best suited for connecting a small number of VPCs (e.g., 2–3 VPCs) with minimal cost.
- **AWS Transit Gateway (TGW)**:
  - A regional network hub (hub-and-spoke model) that connects hundreds of VPCs and on-premises networks together.
  - **Transitive**: Supports transitive routing, eliminating the need for a complex full mesh of peering connections.
  - Centralizes route management, network policies, and security appliance attachments.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In what scenarios would you choose to use VPC peering versus Transit Gateway?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Choose VPC Peering when**:
  - You only have a few VPCs (e.g., 2 to 4) needing simple communication.
  - Cost is a key constraint, as VPC Peering has no hourly connection fee (only data transfer costs apply).
  - You need maximum network throughput with lowest possible latency and no gateway hops.
- **Choose Transit Gateway when**:
  - You manage a large multi-VPC enterprise environment (10+ or hundreds of VPCs) across multiple AWS accounts.
  - You need to connect cloud VPCs to on-premises data centers via AWS Direct Connect or Site-to-Site VPN through a single hub.
  - You require centralized traffic inspection, network segmentation, and transitive routing.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain the different S3 storage classes in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Amazon S3 offers several storage classes tailored to different data access patterns and cost requirements:

1. **S3 Standard**: High durability, high availability, and low latency for frequently accessed data (web assets, active application files).
2. **S3 Intelligent-Tiering**: Automatically shifts objects between access tiers based on usage patterns to save costs with no retrieval fees.
3. **S3 Standard-IA (Infrequent Access)**: Lower storage cost for data accessed less frequently but requiring rapid millisecond access when requested (backups, disaster recovery data).
4. **S3 One Zone-IA**: Lower cost for infrequently accessed data stored in a single Availability Zone (re-creatable data).
5. **S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval**: Low-cost archive for data accessed a few times per year, with retrieval times ranging from minutes to hours.
6. **S3 Glacier Deep Archive**: Lowest-cost storage class for long-term regulatory compliance archiving (retrieval within 12 hours).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you connect different AWS accounts when services in those accounts need to communicate with each other?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Depending on whether communication is network-level or service/API-level, we use:

1. **AWS Transit Gateway with Resource Access Manager (RAM)**: Share a central Transit Gateway across multiple AWS accounts to enable private network communication between VPCs.
2. **VPC Peering**: Directly connect two VPCs in different accounts using cross-account peering.
3. **AWS PrivateLink (VPC Endpoint Services)**: Share a backend service hosted behind an NLB in Account A with Account B securely without network peering or internet traversal.
4. **Cross-Account IAM Roles**: Allow IAM users or compute instances in Account A to assume a role in Account B via AWS STS to access resources like S3 buckets or DynamoDB.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you automate taking backups of data in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We automate backups across AWS using:

1. **AWS Backup**: The primary managed solution. We define centralized backup policies, schedules (e.g., daily at midnight), and retention lifecycle rules (e.g., transition to cold storage after 30 days, delete after 1 year) across EBS, RDS, EFS, and DynamoDB.
2. **Amazon Data Lifecycle Manager (DLM)**: Automatically creates, retains, and deletes EBS snapshots based on EC2 resource tags.
3. **RDS Automated Backups**: Configured with daily snapshots and 7 to 35-day point-in-time recovery (PITR).
4. **S3 Cross-Region Replication & Versioning**: Protects S3 objects against accidental deletion and disaster.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What disaster recovery strategies should be followed in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AWS identifies four disaster recovery (DR) strategies, balancing Recovery Time Objective (RTO) and Recovery Point Objective (RPO) against cost:

1. **Backup & Restore**: Lowest cost, highest RTO/RPO. Regularly back up data to S3 and rebuild infrastructure using Terraform if disaster strikes.
2. **Pilot Light**: Core data is continuously replicated to a secondary region, and minimal core components (like database read replicas) run continuously. Compute servers are launched on demand during a disaster.
3. **Warm Standby**: A scaled-down but fully functional duplicate environment runs continuously in the secondary region, ready to scale up immediately upon failover.
4. **Multi-Region Active-Active**: Highest cost, near-zero RTO/RPO. Production traffic is served simultaneously from two or more AWS regions using Route 53 latency or geolocation routing.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between AWS SNS and SQS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **AWS SNS (Simple Notification Service)**:
  - A **publish/subscribe (Pub/Sub)** notification service.
  - **Push-based**: Messages published to an SNS topic are immediately pushed out to multiple subscribers (fan-out pattern to SQS queues, email, SMS, Lambda functions, HTTP endpoints).
  - Messages are not stored; if no subscriber is active, the message is dropped.
- **AWS SQS (Simple Queue Service)**:
  - A **message queuing** service.
  - **Pull-based**: Consumer applications poll the queue to retrieve, process, and delete messages.
  - Decouples producers and consumers, storing messages safely in the queue (retention up to 14 days) until processed.
</details>

</details>

</details>
