#### 【 GIT 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: When you mentioned that you create the PR, what exactly do you mean by 'creating the PR'?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Creating a Pull Request (PR) means proposing code changes from a feature branch to be merged into a target base branch (such as `develop` or `main`).

In practice:
- After pushing my local commits to GitHub (`git push origin feature/user-auth`), I go to the GitHub repository UI (or use the GitHub CLI: `gh pr create`).
- I open a formal PR specifying the source branch and destination branch.
- I provide a title, a clear summary of changes, links to the relevant Jira ticket, and assign designated team members as code reviewers.
- This creates a centralized discussion thread, shows code diffs, and automatically triggers automated CI pipeline checks.
</details>

#### 【 GIT 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Isn't the PR creation process entirely handled by GitHub itself, with no manual action needed from your side?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
No, GitHub does not automatically create Pull Requests on its own simply because a branch is pushed.

- When a developer runs `git push origin <branch>`, GitHub only receives and stores the commits on that remote branch. It will display a UI helper banner ("Compare & pull request"), but an engineer or an automation script must explicitly initiate the PR creation.
- Once created, GitHub handles the automated aspects: calculating diffs, running branch protection rules, triggering CI webhooks, and tracking approval reviews.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Suppose you want to configure the CI pipeline so that whenever a user commits code, a PR is automatically created and the CI pipeline is triggered without any manual intervention. How would you set this up in your CI configuration?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
You can automate PR creation and CI triggering using a GitHub Actions workflow:

1. **Trigger on Branch Push**:
   ```yaml
   on:
     push:
       branches:
         - 'feature/**'
   ```
2. **Automated PR Creation Step**:
   Use the official GitHub CLI (`gh`) or a pre-built GitHub Action within the workflow job:
   ```yaml
   steps:
     - name: Checkout repository
       uses: actions/checkout@v3

     - name: Create Pull Request automatically
       env:
         GH_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}
       run: |
         gh pr create --base develop --head $(git branch --show-current) \
                      --title "Automated PR: ${{ github.ref_name }}" \
                      --body "Automatically created by CI pipeline on commit." || true
   ```
3. Once the PR is created, it automatically triggers any secondary CI validation workflows configured with `on: pull_request`.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Besides configuring the webhook trigger, what specific variable would you use in the CI configuration to ensure the CI pipeline automatically triggers when a PR is created?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **In GitHub Actions**:
  - We use the `github.event_name` context variable to check if the trigger was a PR:
    ```yaml
    if: github.event_name == 'pull_request'
    ```
  - We also check `github.base_ref` (the target branch being merged into) and specify event types in the trigger:
    ```yaml
    on:
      pull_request:
        types: [opened, synchronize, reopened]
    ```

- **In Jenkins**:
  - When using the GitHub Branch Source or Multibranch Pipeline plugin, we check the environment variable **`CHANGE_ID`** (which contains the Pull Request number) or **`CHANGE_TARGET`** (the base branch). If `CHANGE_ID` is set, Jenkins knows the run is triggered by a Pull Request.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is a CI/CD pipeline, and why do we need pipelines in the software development lifecycle (SDLC)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A CI/CD pipeline is an automated series of steps that takes code from a developer's machine through to production delivery:

- **Continuous Integration (CI)**: Automatically builds, tests, and validates code changes on every commit or PR, detecting syntax errors, bugs, and integration conflicts immediately.
- **Continuous Delivery / Deployment (CD)**: Automatically packages the validated build into deployable artifacts (e.g., Docker images) and deploys them to staging and production environments safely.

**Why we need pipelines in SDLC**:
1. **Eliminates Manual Errors**: Replaces human error-prone manual builds and FTP/SSH copy deployments.
2. **Faster Time to Market**: Reduces release cycles from months or weeks to hours or minutes.
3. **Enforces Quality & Security**: Ensures every piece of code passes unit tests, SonarQube quality gates, and security scans before deployment.
4. **Reliable Rollbacks**: Provides automated, consistent rollback capabilities in production.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would you design a complete continuous integration (CI) pipeline for a project, from code commit through to build and testing?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A complete CI pipeline design consists of:

1. **Trigger**: GitHub webhook triggers the pipeline upon code push or PR creation.
2. **Checkout**: Jenkins agent clones the exact Git commit SHA.
3. **Lint & Static Code Analysis**: Run linters and SonarQube Scanner to verify code formatting, check for bugs, and enforce Quality Gates.
4. **Compile & Build**: Compile source code using the appropriate build tool (e.g., `mvn clean compile`).
5. **Automated Testing**: Run unit tests (`mvn test`) and integration tests; publish test reports and code coverage (e.g., JaCoCo).
6. **Vulnerability Scanning**: Trivy or Snyk scans project dependencies for open CVEs.
7. **Container Build & Scan**: Build Docker image and scan image layers with Trivy.
8. **Artifact Publishing**: Push verified container image to Amazon ECR tagged with commit SHA.
9. **Status Feedback**: Report pass/fail status back to the GitHub PR commit check.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What build tools would you use to build the source code, for example in a Java application?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **For Java Applications**:
  - **Apache Maven**: The industry standard we use most frequently. It manages project dependencies, plugins, and build lifecycles defined in `pom.xml` (`mvn clean package`).
  - **Gradle**: A fast, modern alternative using Groovy or Kotlin DSL with incremental build caching.

- **For Other Common Tech Stacks**:
  - **Node.js**: `npm` or `yarn` (executes `npm install` and `npm run build`).
  - **Python**: `pip`, `poetry`, or `setuptools` (building wheels).
  - **Go**: `go build`.
</details>

</details>

</details>

#### 【 LINUX 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Could you write a shell script to delete log files that are older than 30 days?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Here is a clean, simple Bash script to delete log files older than 30 days:

```bash
#!/bin/bash
LOG_DIR="/var/log/myapp"

# Verify that the target directory exists before running cleanup
if [ -d "$LOG_DIR" ]; then
    find "$LOG_DIR" -type f -name "*.log" -mtime +30 -exec rm -f {} \;
    echo "Logs older than 30 days deleted successfully from $LOG_DIR."
else
    echo "Directory $LOG_DIR does not exist. Aborting."
    exit 1
fi
```
This script checks if `/var/log/myapp` exists, searches for regular files ending with `.log` modified more than 30 days ago (`-mtime +30`), and removes them. We schedule this script in `crontab` (e.g., `0 2 * * * /opt/scripts/cleanup_logs.sh`) to run automatically every night.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In the shell script command you wrote, are you missing anything, such as specifying the directory to search in with the find command?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
If we omit the search directory path, the `find` command defaults to the current working directory (`.`). In production, if the script is executed by cron or a CI runner where the working directory is `/` or `/root`, running `find` without an explicit path could scan the entire system and delete files in the wrong place. To avoid this, I always explicitly declare the target directory variable (`LOG_DIR="/var/log/myapp"`) and verify it exists using `[ -d "$LOG_DIR" ]` before passing it to `find "$LOG_DIR"`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What kind of Linux activities and tasks have you performed in your role?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In my daily role as a DevOps engineer, I handle:
- **Resource Monitoring & Troubleshooting:** Checking CPU, memory, and disk health using `top`, `htop`, `df -h`, `free -m`, and `iostat`.
- **Service & Process Management:** Managing system services using `systemctl start/stop/restart/status` and checking processes with `ps aux | grep <service>`.
- **Log Analysis:** Inspecting application and system logs using `journalctl -u <service> -f`, `tail -f`, `grep`, `awk`, and `sed`.
- **User & Permission Administration:** Managing user accounts, groups, and file access using `useradd`, `usermod`, `chmod`, and `chown`.
- **Network Diagnostics:** Verifying open ports and connectivity using `ss -tuln`, `netstat`, `curl -Iv`, `telnet`, and `ping`.
- **Shell Automation:** Writing bash scripts for daily log cleanups, backups, and automating tasks via `crontab`.
</details>

#### 【 GIT 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Could you explain the Git branching strategy you have used in your project?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our project, we follow a standard GitFlow branching model:
- `main`: Contains production-ready code. Direct pushes are blocked; only thoroughly tested release branches are merged here via Pull Requests.
- `develop`: The central integration branch where all feature branches are merged for testing in DEV and QA environments.
- `feature/*`: Created from `develop` by developers to work on new user stories. Once complete, they are merged back into `develop` via a reviewed Pull Request.
- `release/*`: Created from `develop` when preparing for a production deployment to conduct final regression testing and minor bug fixes.
- `hotfix/*`: Created directly from `main` to address critical production issues immediately, and merged back into both `main` and `develop`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is the difference between a hotfix branch and a bug fix branch in your Git branching strategy?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Bug fix branch:** Used for regular defects found during development or QA testing in lower environments. It is created from `develop` (or a `release` branch), tested within normal sprint cycles, and merged back into `develop`.
- **Hotfix branch:** Used for urgent, critical defects occurring directly in live production (e.g., payment gateway failure or security patch). It is branched directly from `main`, patched, verified in a staging environment, and merged into both `main` (with a patch tag) and `develop` so the fix isn't lost in the next release.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: From which branch do you create the bug fix branch?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A bug fix branch is created from the `develop` branch if the bug was found during active sprint development. If the bug was discovered during release hardening or pre-production QA testing, it is created from that active `release/*` branch.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Again, from which branch specifically is the bug fix branch created?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Specifically:
- During standard sprint development: It is branched directly from **`develop`**.
- During the pre-release testing window: It is branched specifically from the candidate **`release`** branch (e.g., `release/v2.1.0`) so the fix only applies to the upcoming release and does not pick up newer, unreleased code in `develop`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: From which branch is the hotfix branch created?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A hotfix branch is always created strictly from the **`main`** (or `master`) branch. Since `main` reflects the exact code currently running in production, branching from `main` ensures the hotfix contains only the necessary emergency patch without pulling in any unfinished features currently residing in `develop`.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Could you write a Jenkins declarative pipeline that you have used in a project, including its stages?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Here is a standard declarative Jenkinsfile pipeline used in our project:

```groovy
pipeline {
    agent any
    tools {
        maven 'Maven-3.8.6'
        jdk 'JDK-17'
    }
    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                git branch: 'main', url: 'https://github.com/myorg/payment-service.git'
            }
        }
        stage('Build & Test') {
            steps {
                sh 'mvn clean test'
            }
        }
        stage('SonarQube Analysis') {
            steps {
                withSonarQubeEnv('SonarQube') {
                    sh 'mvn sonar:sonar'
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Docker Build & Push') {
            steps {
                sh '''
                docker build -t 123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/payment-service:${BUILD_NUMBER} .
                docker push 123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/payment-service:${BUILD_NUMBER}
                '''
            }
        }
        stage('Deploy to DEV') {
            steps {
                sh 'kubectl set image deployment/payment-deployment payment=123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/payment-service:${BUILD_NUMBER} -n dev'
            }
        }
    }
    post {
        always {
            cleanWs()
        }
        failure {
            echo "Pipeline failed! Sending notification to team."
        }
    }
}
```
This pipeline checks out code from Git, runs unit tests via Maven, performs static code analysis with SonarQube, builds and pushes a Docker image to AWS ECR, and deploys the new image to our Kubernetes DEV namespace.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What does your Jenkins master-agent architecture look like, and how many masters and agents have you configured?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We use a distributed Jenkins master-agent architecture:
- **Jenkins Master (Controller):** Runs on a dedicated EC2 instance (`t3.large`) inside our private VPC. It manages the web UI, pipeline schedules, credentials, and dispatches build jobs. It does not execute build jobs directly to keep the controller responsive.
- **Jenkins Agents (Worker Nodes):** We have 3 permanent Linux EC2 worker instances (`t3.medium`) connected via SSH for standard builds, plus dynamic agent pods running in Amazon EKS via the Jenkins Kubernetes plugin that scale up on demand for container builds and scale to zero when idle.
- In our team, we maintain 1 master and 3 to 4 agent nodes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can Jenkins be configured with multiple masters/controllers?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In standard open-source Jenkins, native active-active clustering of a single master is not supported because `$JENKINS_HOME` cannot be written to concurrently by multiple masters. However, organizations handle multiple controllers in two ways:
1. **Active-Passive High Availability:** An active master serves traffic while a passive standby points to the same storage (e.g., AWS EFS) and starts up if the primary fails.
2. **Multiple Independent Masters:** Partitioning controllers by department or team (e.g., Payments Team Controller, Analytics Team Controller) managed under CloudBees CI Operations Center or behind an Application Load Balancer.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain the Kubernetes architecture, including its main components?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Kubernetes operates on a client-server architecture split into the **Control Plane** and **Worker Nodes**:
- **Control Plane (Master):**
  - **`kube-apiserver`:** The central management hub and API endpoint that processes all REST requests (`kubectl`).
  - **`etcd`:** Consistent, distributed key-value database that stores the cluster's entire state and configurations.
  - **`kube-scheduler`:** Assigns newly created pods to optimal worker nodes based on resource requests and affinity rules.
  - **`kube-controller-manager`:** Runs core control loops (Node Controller, Deployment Controller) to maintain the desired state.
- **Worker Nodes:**
  - **`kubelet`:** Agent running on each node that ensures containers in PodSpecs are running and healthy.
  - **`kube-proxy`:** Manages network routing and IP tables/IPVS rules for Kubernetes Services across pods.
  - **Container Runtime:** The low-level engine (like `containerd`) that pulls images and runs container processes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Which version of Kubernetes have you been using, and for how many years have you worked with Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I have worked hands-on with Kubernetes for about 3.5 to 4 years out of my 5 years in DevOps. Currently, we are using Kubernetes version **1.28** (and evaluating 1.29) on Amazon EKS across our development, staging, and production environments.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is the current Kubernetes version you are using in your project?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our current production environment, we are running Kubernetes version **1.28** on AWS EKS. We are currently validating version **1.29** in our development sandbox so we can complete our next scheduled minor version upgrade before AWS ends standard support for 1.28.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What Kubernetes version were you using at the start of your career with Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
When I first started working with Kubernetes around 3.5 to 4 years ago, our clusters were running on Kubernetes version **1.20** / **1.21**. That was around the period when Docker runtime deprecation was announced and clusters were migrating to `containerd` via the Container Runtime Interface (CRI).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Since you have been working on EKS, have you ever performed a cluster upgrade?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have performed several EKS cluster upgrades, such as upgrading from 1.26 to 1.27, and from 1.27 to 1.28. EKS requires upgrading one minor version at a time sequentially. We always test and validate the upgrade in DEV and QA environments first, verify all application workloads and ingress controllers, and then perform the production upgrade during a planned off-peak maintenance window.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What prerequisites do you follow before upgrading an EKS cluster?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Before upgrading an EKS cluster, I complete these prerequisites:
1. **Check API Deprecations:** Run tools like `pluto` or `kubent` to detect deprecated API versions in our Helm charts and Kubernetes manifests.
2. **Verify Add-ons:** Check compatibility matrices and plan upgrades for AWS VPC CNI, CoreDNS, and `kube-proxy`.
3. **Check Ingress & CSI Drivers:** Ensure the AWS Load Balancer Controller and AWS EBS CSI driver are compatible with the target version.
4. **Inspect PodDisruptionBudgets (PDB):** Ensure PDB configurations will not block pod evictions during node draining.
5. **Backups:** Take Velero cluster backups and EBS volume snapshots of stateful applications.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How many days does it typically take to upgrade the production environment after validating in lower environments?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The validation cycle across DEV, QA, and Staging takes about **1 to 2 weeks** to ensure complete regression testing and application stability. Once validated, the actual production upgrade is executed during a planned maintenance window (usually on a weekend) and takes about **2 to 3 hours**. The entire production change window—from pre-checks and upgrade execution to 24-hour post-upgrade monitoring—covers about 2 days.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Do you cordon the nodes during the EKS cluster upgrade process?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, cordoning and draining nodes is an essential part of the worker node upgrade process:
- `kubectl cordon <node-name>`: Marks the node as unschedulable so Kubernetes does not schedule any new pods on it.
- `kubectl drain <node-name> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data`: Safely evicts existing pods to other healthy worker nodes in the cluster.
When using AWS Managed Node Groups, AWS automatically executes this cordon-and-drain sequence node-by-node during rolling updates, ensuring zero downtime for live applications.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How much time does the overall cluster upgrade process take?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
For a single EKS cluster, the active hands-on upgrade process takes approximately **2 to 3 hours**:
- EKS Control Plane upgrade: ~30 to 45 minutes.
- Core Add-ons upgrade: ~15 minutes.
- Worker Node Groups rolling replacement: ~45 to 60 minutes.
- Post-upgrade health checks and smoke testing: ~30 minutes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How long does it take to upgrade the control plane, node groups, and add-ons specifically during an EKS upgrade?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Specifically:
- **Control Plane:** Takes about **30 to 45 minutes**. AWS updates the managed API server and etcd across multiple Availability Zones with zero API downtime.
- **Add-ons:** Takes about **10 to 15 minutes** to update the AWS VPC CNI, CoreDNS, and `kube-proxy` manifests.
- **Node Groups:** Takes about **45 to 60 minutes** for AWS Managed Node Groups, as AWS provisions new EC2 instances with updated AMIs, drains old nodes one by one, and terminates the old instances.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What deployment strategy do you use for deploying applications in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our project, we use:
1. **Rolling Update (Default):** Native Kubernetes Deployment strategy configured with `maxSurge: 25%` and `maxUnavailable: 0`. It spins up new pods, waits for readiness probes to succeed, and then terminates old pods gradually with zero downtime.
2. **Blue-Green Deployment:** Used for major version upgrades. We run both versions simultaneously and switch live traffic by updating the Service selector label (`version: green`) once verified.
3. **Canary Deployment:** For critical customer APIs, routing a small percentage (e.g., 10%) of live traffic to the new version using Ingress annotations or Argo Rollouts before rolling it out to 100%.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you been using Terraform in your project?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have been using Terraform for over 4 years to provision and manage cloud infrastructure on AWS. I write Terraform code using modular structures to manage VPCs, public/private subnets, Security Groups, EC2 instances, EKS clusters, RDS Aurora databases, S3 buckets, and IAM roles across our development and production environments.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What kind of Terraform are you using—Terragrunt or plain Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our current project, we use **plain Terraform**. We organize our repository into reusable child modules (`modules/vpc`, `modules/eks`) and separate environment directories (`environments/dev`, `environments/prod`) with environment-specific `.tfvars` files. This keeps our pipeline simple, maintainable, and standard without introducing the extra tooling layer of Terragrunt.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the purpose of the 'terraform fmt' command?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The `terraform fmt` command formats Terraform configuration files (`.tf`) to follow HashiCorp's canonical style conventions. It automatically standardizes indentation, aligns equals signs (`=`), and cleans up whitespace. We run `terraform fmt` before committing code and enforce `terraform fmt -check` in our CI pipelines to maintain consistent code formatting across the team.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What does the 'terraform validate' command do?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
`terraform validate` checks whether your configuration files are syntactically correct and internally consistent. It verifies resource argument names, attribute types, and module inputs without calling any remote cloud APIs or requiring cloud credentials. It is run after `terraform init` to catch typos and syntax errors early.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What happens when you run the 'terraform init' command?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
When you run `terraform init`:
1. It initializes the backend and connects to remote state storage (e.g., AWS S3 and DynamoDB).
2. It downloads and installs the required provider plugins (such as `aws` and `kubernetes`) into the local `.terraform` directory.
3. It downloads any referenced child modules from Git or the Terraform Registry.
4. It creates or updates the `.terraform.lock.hcl` lock file to pin provider versions.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you manage the Terraform state file in your project?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We manage Terraform state remotely using an **Amazon S3 backend** with **DynamoDB state locking**:
- **S3 Bucket:** Stores `terraform.tfstate` with **versioning enabled** so we can restore previous states if corruption occurs, with KMS encryption at rest.
- **DynamoDB Table:** Uses a `LockID` primary key to provide distributed state locking, preventing race conditions if multiple engineers or CI pipelines run `apply` at the same time.
- **Isolation:** We separate state files per environment (`dev`, `stage`, `prod`) and per infrastructure layer (`networking`, `eks`, `database`) to minimize blast radius.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If there is a configuration drift between Terraform state and actual AWS infrastructure, how would you handle it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
When configuration drift occurs (e.g., someone manually modified a security group rule in the AWS console):
1. **Detect Drift:** Run `terraform plan` to compare the live AWS infrastructure against the Terraform state file and configuration code.
2. **Evaluate the Drift:** Determine whether the manual change was an intentional emergency fix or an unauthorized modification.
3. **Reconcile:**
   - If unauthorized: Run `terraform apply` to overwrite the manual changes and restore the infrastructure to the desired state declared in code.
   - If intentional and approved: Update the `.tf` code to reflect the new configuration so `terraform plan` reports zero changes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: After identifying drift using 'terraform plan', how would you bring the resource back to the desired managed state?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
If the desired state is defined by the existing Terraform configuration code, we simply execute:
```bash
terraform apply
```
Terraform detects the differences between the live cloud resources and the code, calls the AWS APIs to undo the manual out-of-band changes, and brings the live infrastructure back into alignment with the code.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is 'terraform taint' and how is it used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
`terraform taint` marks a managed resource in the state file as degraded or corrupted, forcing Terraform to destroy and recreate it on the next `terraform apply`. For example:
```bash
terraform taint aws_instance.web_server
```
In modern Terraform versions (0.15+), HashiCorp recommends using the `-replace` flag instead:
```bash
terraform apply -replace="aws_instance.web_server"
```
which achieves the same recreation without mutating the state file beforehand.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is 'terraform import' and how is it used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
`terraform import` brings pre-existing cloud resources that were created manually or by other tools under Terraform state management.
1. Write the matching resource block in your `.tf` configuration file:
   ```hcl
   resource "aws_s3_bucket" "my_bucket" {
     bucket = "my-existing-bucket"
   }
   ```
2. Run the import command:
   ```bash
   terraform import aws_s3_bucket.my_bucket my-existing-bucket
   ```
This updates the state file to track the existing resource without destroying or recreating it.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Wouldn't you use 'terraform import' to resolve drift instead of taint?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
No, because `import` and `taint` serve completely different purposes:
- **`terraform import`** is used only when an existing cloud resource is **not in the Terraform state file at all**. If you run `import` on a resource already tracked in state, Terraform returns an error that it is already managed.
- **Drift** occurs when a resource is **already tracked in state**, but someone modified its attributes manually in AWS. You fix drift by running `terraform apply` or updating the code.
- **`terraform taint`** is used when a tracked resource is corrupted or unhealthy and you want to explicitly destroy and recreate it from scratch.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Why would you use 'terraform taint' to force recreation of a resource instead of other approaches?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
You use `terraform taint` (or `-replace`) when a resource is in a corrupted or non-working state at the OS or application level, but its cloud attributes (like AMI ID, instance type, or subnet) match the Terraform code. Because cloud attributes match, a normal `terraform apply` would report "No changes". Tainting forces Terraform to terminate the broken instance and provision a clean, newly bootstrapped replacement.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What cost optimization strategies have you implemented in your AWS projects?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We implemented several practical cost optimization strategies:
1. **Rightsizing Compute:** Monitored CloudWatch CPU/memory metrics and downsized over-provisioned EC2 instances.
2. **Savings Plans & Spot Instances:** Purchased 1-year Compute Savings Plans for steady production workloads and used Spot instances for DEV/QA environments and Jenkins build agents.
3. **Storage Cleanup:** Migrated EBS volumes from `gp2` to `gp3` (saving 20% on storage costs), removed unattached EBS volumes and stale snapshots, and configured S3 Lifecycle rules to move older logs to Glacier.
4. **Automated Scheduling:** Set up AWS Lambda and EventBridge rules to automatically stop non-production EC2 and RDS instances during off-hours (nights and weekends).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How did you determine that the cost optimization resulted in a 10% reduction?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We verified the 10% cost reduction using **AWS Cost Explorer** and **AWS Budgets**:
- We enabled Cost Allocation Tags (e.g., `Environment: dev`, `Team: platform`) to track spending by environment and service.
- We compared the month-over-month total billing reports before and after implementing the optimizations (e.g., comparing June spend vs. July spend).
- The reports in Cost Explorer showed a clear 10% reduction driven by lower EC2 compute running hours, EBS `gp3` migrations, and the cleanup of idle resources.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What type/kind of EC2 instances do you use for your workloads?</strong></summary>

We choose EC2 instance types based on workload requirements:

- **Compute Optimized (`c5.large`, `c5.xlarge`):** Used for CPU-intensive tasks like code compilation and batch jobs.
- **Memory Optimized (`r5.large`):** Used for memory-heavy applications like Redis caches and database instances.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● If you were designing a CI/CD pipeline for REST-based microservices, what stages would you include?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A standard CI/CD pipeline for REST-based microservices includes:
1. **Checkout:** Pull the latest source code from the Git repository.
2. **Build & Unit Test:** Compile the microservice and run unit tests (`mvn test` or `cargo test`).
3. **Code Quality & SAST:** Run SonarQube static code analysis and enforce Quality Gate standards.
4. **Docker Build:** Build the container image using a multi-stage Dockerfile.
5. **Security Scanning:** Scan the newly built Docker image for vulnerabilities using Trivy.
6. **Push Image:** Push the validated image tagged with the Git commit SHA to Amazon ECR.
7. **Deploy:** Deploy the updated manifest to Kubernetes (DEV/QA) or update the GitOps repository for Argo CD.
8. **Smoke Tests & Notifications:** Run automated health check tests on the REST endpoint and notify the team via Slack.
</details>

#### 【 DOCKER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What would you use as a Docker image repository for this platform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
On AWS, we use **Amazon Elastic Container Registry (ECR)** as our private Docker repository. It integrates natively with AWS IAM and Kubernetes IRSA for secure authentication, supports automated vulnerability scanning on image push, enables image tag immutability to prevent overwriting production releases, and provides low-latency image pulls directly into our EKS worker nodes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you optimize Docker images for a Rust application?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To optimize a Docker image for a Rust application:
1. **Multi-Stage Build:** Use an official Rust builder image (`rust:alpine` or `rust:slim`) to compile the binary, then copy only the resulting binary into a minimal runtime image like `alpine` or `scratch`.
2. **Release Build with Stripping:** Run `cargo build --release` and execute `strip target/release/app` to remove debugging symbols, reducing binary size drastically (from 80MB+ down to ~15MB).
3. **Dependency Caching:** Copy only `Cargo.toml` and `Cargo.lock` first and compile dummy dependencies so external crates are cached across builds when only application source code changes.
4. **Non-Root User:** Run the container process under a non-root user for security.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What Kubernetes probes are you aware of, and how do they work?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Kubernetes provides three health probes:
1. **Startup Probe:** Checks if the application inside the container has successfully started. It disables liveness and readiness checks until it succeeds, preventing slow-starting applications from being terminated prematurely.
2. **Liveness Probe:** Checks if the container process is still healthy and running. If it fails repeatedly, Kubernetes terminates the container and restarts it according to the pod's `restartPolicy`.
3. **Readiness Probe:** Checks if the pod is ready to accept network traffic. If it fails, Kubernetes removes the pod's IP from the Service endpoints so no traffic is routed to it until it recovers.
- Probes can be configured via HTTP GET requests, TCP socket checks, or exec commands.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How does Kubernetes Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) work?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) automatically adjusts the number of replica pods in a Deployment based on observed resource utilization:
- HPA periodically queries the Kubernetes Metrics Server (every 15 seconds by default) for CPU and memory usage.
- It calculates the required number of replicas using the formula:
  `Desired Replicas = Current Replicas * (Current Metric Value / Target Metric Value)`
- If average CPU utilization exceeds the target percentage (e.g., 70%), HPA scales up replicas up to `maxReplicas`. When traffic drops, it scales back down gradually after a cooldown stabilization window.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you implement a blue-green deployment strategy?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To implement a Blue-Green deployment in Kubernetes:
1. Maintain two identical Deployments: `app-blue` (currently live version) and `app-green` (new version).
2. The live Kubernetes Service points to `version: blue` via its label selector (`app: myapp, version: blue`).
3. Deploy the new code version to `app-green` and test it internally using a private test service.
4. Once verified, update the live Service selector to point to `version: green`:
   ```bash
   kubectl patch service my-service -p '{"spec":{"selector":{"version":"green"}}}'
   ```
5. Traffic immediately switches to the green deployment with zero downtime. If any issue arises, quickly patch the selector back to `version: blue`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Do you have knowledge about canary deployments, and can you explain how they work?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes. In a Canary deployment, a new version of the application is released to a small subset of live users (e.g., 10% of traffic) while 90% continues hitting the stable version:
- We monitor key operational metrics like error rates (HTTP 5xx) and latency on the canary pods.
- If the canary remains healthy, we gradually shift traffic (e.g., 25% -> 50% -> 100%).
- If error rates spike, traffic is immediately rolled back to the stable version, minimizing the blast radius.
- In Kubernetes, canary deployments can be implemented using NGINX Ingress canary annotations, service meshes (Istio), or **Argo Rollouts**.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you check the health of a pod in a Kubernetes (AKS) cluster?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To check pod health in an AKS (or EKS) cluster:
1. **Check Pod Status:** Run `kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -o wide` to inspect pod state and restart counts.
2. **Inspect Conditions & Events:** Run `kubectl describe pod <pod-name> -n <namespace>` to check pod conditions (`Ready`, `ContainersReady`) and look at probe failure events.
3. **Check Container Logs:** Stream real-time logs with `kubectl logs <pod-name> -n <namespace> --tail=100 -f` (or add `--previous` if the pod crashed).
4. **Azure Portal (AKS):** View pod CPU/memory utilization and health metrics directly via Azure Monitor and Container Insights.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you manage Terraform state?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We manage Terraform state following standard best practices:
- **Remote Backend:** State is stored in an Amazon S3 bucket with **versioning enabled** so we can restore prior states if corruption occurs.
- **State Locking:** We use an Amazon DynamoDB table with a `LockID` primary key to prevent simultaneous runs from corrupting the state file.
- **Security:** The S3 bucket is encrypted using AWS KMS and access is restricted through least-privilege IAM policies.
- **State Separation:** We separate state files by environment (`dev`, `stage`, `prod`) and layer (`vpc`, `eks`, `database`) to keep state files small and isolate changes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What Terraform modules are you familiar with or have used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I have worked with both community modules and custom internal modules:
- **Community Modules (AWS):**
  - `terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws`: To deploy VPCs, public/private subnets, NAT Gateways, and route tables.
  - `terraform-aws-modules/eks/aws`: To provision managed EKS clusters, node groups, and IAM roles.
  - `terraform-aws-modules/security-group/aws`: For managing security groups and ingress/egress rules.
  - `terraform-aws-modules/rds/aws`: For provisioning RDS and Aurora database instances.
- **Custom Modules:** We build custom modules to enforce organizational standards for microservice deployments, IAM policies, and encrypted S3 buckets.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are the key AWS services required for building a Rust-based microservices platform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Key AWS services include:
- **Compute & Orchestration:** **Amazon EKS** (or ECS/Fargate) to host lightweight containerized Rust microservices.
- **Networking:** **Application Load Balancer (ALB)** and **Route 53** for traffic routing and SSL termination.
- **Container Registry:** **Amazon ECR** for storing Docker images.
- **Database & Cache:** **Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL** for relational data, **DynamoDB** for NoSQL key-value storage, and **ElastiCache (Redis)** for caching.
- **Messaging:** **Amazon SQS / SNS** or **Amazon MSK (Kafka)** for asynchronous event-driven communication.
- **Security:** **AWS Secrets Manager**, **KMS**, and **IAM** (IRSA).
- **Observability:** **Amazon CloudWatch** and **AWS X-Ray** for logs, metrics, and tracing.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: For a Rust-based microservices platform on AWS, what would you use for asynchronous communication between services?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We use:
1. **Amazon SNS and SQS (Fan-out Pattern):** Microservices publish events to an SNS topic, which delivers messages to multiple SQS queues subscribed by other services. Worker services poll SQS queues asynchronously with built-in retry logic and Dead Letter Queues (DLQs).
2. **Amazon MSK (Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka):** Used when the platform requires high-throughput event streaming, strict message ordering, and replayability across distributed consumer groups.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: For a Rust-based microservices platform on AWS, what services would you use for data storage?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Depending on access patterns and data types:
- **Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL:** For structured relational data requiring ACID transactions (e.g., user profiles, orders, and payment records).
- **Amazon DynamoDB:** For fast NoSQL key-value lookups requiring single-digit millisecond latency (e.g., session states and shopping carts).
- **Amazon ElastiCache for Redis:** For in-memory caching of frequent database queries to keep API latency low.
- **Amazon S3:** For object storage (e.g., document uploads, media assets, reports, and log archives).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are the major differences between AWS ECS, EKS, and Fargate for orchestration?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **AWS ECS:** AWS's proprietary container orchestrator. It is simple, easy to set up, and deeply integrated with AWS services, but proprietary to AWS.
- **AWS EKS:** AWS's managed Kubernetes service. It is cloud-agnostic, highly customizable, and supports the extensive CNCF open-source ecosystem (Helm, Istio, Argo CD), though it comes with higher operational complexity.
- **AWS Fargate:** A serverless compute engine for containers. It works as an execution backend for **both** ECS and EKS. With Fargate, you do not manage or patch EC2 instances—AWS runs and scales the containers directly.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Are you familiar with AWS X-Ray?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes. AWS X-Ray is a distributed tracing service that helps analyze and debug distributed microservices. In a microservice architecture, a single client request often travels through an ALB, API Gateway, multiple microservices, and databases. X-Ray generates a visual service map and trace timelines showing the path and duration of each call. This allows us to quickly identify bottlenecks, high-latency downstream calls, and HTTP 4xx/5xx errors across services.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you perform cost optimization on AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We optimize AWS costs using four core practices:
1. **Rightsizing Compute:** Analyze CloudWatch CPU and memory metrics to downsize underutilized EC2 and RDS instances.
2. **Savings Plans & Spot Instances:** Purchase Compute Savings Plans for steady production baselines and leverage Spot instances for non-production environments and CI/CD agents.
3. **Storage Optimization:** Migrate EBS volumes from `gp2` to `gp3`, delete unattached EBS volumes and snapshots, and configure S3 Lifecycle policies to transition older data to Glacier.
4. **Off-Hours Scheduling:** Automatically shut down non-production EC2 and RDS instances on nights and weekends using AWS Lambda and EventBridge.
</details>

#### 【 MONITORING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you monitor REST APIs in production?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We monitor REST APIs using the **Four Golden Signals**:
1. **Latency:** Tracking p95 and p99 response times via CloudWatch or Prometheus/Grafana.
2. **Traffic:** Monitoring request throughput (requests per second) at the Application Load Balancer or API Gateway.
3. **Errors:** Setting alarms on HTTP 5xx error rates (e.g., alerting if 5xx errors exceed 1% over 5 minutes).
4. **Saturation:** Monitoring CPU, memory, database connection pool, and Kubernetes pod health.
- We also deploy **Synthetic Canaries** (AWS CloudWatch Synthetics) to make automated HTTP requests every minute to verify uptime, endpoint health, and SSL certificate validity.
</details>

#### 【 SECURITY 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: For a Rust-based microservices platform on AWS, which services or measures would you use for security?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We implement security across multiple layers:
1. **Network Security:** Place microservices and databases in private VPC subnets; configure Security Groups to restrict access; attach AWS WAF to the ALB to protect against OWASP Top 10 web vulnerabilities.
2. **IAM & Pod Identity:** Use IAM Roles for Service Accounts (IRSA) so pods obtain temporary credentials without embedding static AWS access keys.
3. **Secrets Management:** Store database credentials and API keys in AWS Secrets Manager or Systems Manager Parameter Store.
4. **Encryption:** Enforce TLS 1.3 for data in transit and KMS encryption at rest across databases, S3, and EBS.
5. **Container Security:** Use minimal distroless/alpine base images, run containers as non-root users, and scan images for vulnerabilities using Trivy.
</details>

#### 【 SYSTEM DESIGN 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you explain the circuit breaker pattern used to prevent cascading failures in microservices?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The Circuit Breaker pattern prevents a failure in one microservice from causing a cascading outage across the entire system:
- **Closed State:** Normal operation. Requests flow to the downstream service. If requests succeed, the breaker stays closed.
- **Open State:** If the downstream service fails repeatedly (e.g., 50% errors over 10 seconds), the circuit trips Open. Subsequent requests fail immediately without calling the downstream service, returning a fallback response. This protects the calling service's thread pool and gives the failing service time to recover.
- **Half-Open State:** After a timeout period, the breaker allows a few trial requests through. If they succeed, it resets to Closed; if they fail, it trips back to Open.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would you prevent cascading failures in a microservices system, such as when a circuit opens after repeated failures?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Beyond circuit breakers, we prevent cascading failures by:
1. **Setting Strict Timeouts:** Ensure every HTTP/RPC client call has a short timeout (e.g., 1-2 seconds) so worker threads are not held indefinitely.
2. **Graceful Fallbacks:** Provide fallback responses (e.g., returning cached data or a default value) instead of returning hard 500 errors.
3. **Exponential Backoff with Jitter:** When retrying failed requests, back off exponentially with added randomness to prevent overwhelming the recovering service.
4. **Bulkhead Pattern:** Isolate critical connection pools and thread pools so that issues in one non-critical service cannot exhaust resources needed for core checkout/payment flows.
5. **Rate Limiting:** Protect backend services from traffic surges using API Gateway rate limiting.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Suppose API latency suddenly increases from 50 milliseconds to 2 seconds in production. How would you troubleshoot this issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I follow a step-by-step troubleshooting workflow:
1. **Check Load Balancer Metrics:** Check CloudWatch for ALB `TargetResponseTime` vs ALB latency to verify if the delay is in the application backend or the load balancer/network.
2. **Inspect Distributed Traces (AWS X-Ray):** Check trace waterfalls to pinpoint where the delay occurs—application code, a downstream microservice, or a database query.
3. **Check Database Health:** Look at RDS Performance Insights for high CPU, connection pool saturation, long-running queries, or table locks.
4. **Check Pod & Node Health:** Run `kubectl top pods` and `kubectl top nodes` to check for CPU/memory throttling or pod restarts.
5. **Check Recent Deployments:** Verify if a new application release was rolled out recently, and roll back immediately if the latency spike correlates with the release.
</details>
</details>
